# Exact quantized speculative decoding: early feasibility findings

These are **diagnostic screening results**, not a validated exact algorithm or a paper benchmark. All ratios below are paired `vanilla total latency / method total latency` on the same prompt; a ratio above 1 means the method finished faster. A latency ratio for an inexact output is not an exact-decoding speedup.

## BNB NF4, RTX 4050, 30 prompts, 64-token budget

Source: [`pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n30/rows.jsonl`](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n30/rows.jsonl), [`analysis.json`](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n30/analysis.json), and the [`manifest`](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n30/manifest.json). The prompts are the first 10 from each of the repository's GSM8K, HumanEval, and CNN/DailyMail evaluation files. These inputs were used in previous ZASSD work; the run is a **single-session diagnostic pilot**.

| Method | Exact whole sequences | Mean paired latency ratio | Interpretation |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Vanilla | 30/30 | 1.000 | Quantized reference |
| PLD batched verification | 23/30 | 1.078 | Faster, but 7 output trajectories differ |
| PLD sequential verification | 30/30 | 0.981 | Reference-equivalent in this test, slightly slower |
| Existing margin guard, threshold 1.5 | 29/30 | 0.197 | Inexact on `cnndm_006`; very slow |

The guard spent an average **77.8%** of its measured request time in canonical replay. If this measured replay time were subtracted for free while keeping every other observed action fixed, its mean latency ratio would be **0.876** over all 30 prompts, or **0.879** over the 29 where the guard happened to match vanilla. Only 1 of those 29 would exceed vanilla under that optimistic cost subtraction. This is a **conditional cost counterfactual**, not an implementable or certified decoder. It suggests that merely making the current guard's full-prefix replay free would not make that policy broadly competitive on this pilot.

A whole-prompt hindsight oracle chose the fastest *observed exact* method separately for each prompt and reached a mean ratio of **1.083**: batched PLD on 19 prompts, vanilla on 6, sequential PLD on 5, guard on 0. This shows some room for choosing among these methods with perfect knowledge, but does not provide an online selector, a cycle-level oracle, or a proof that the selector could be cheap.

The guard's 29/30 at 64 tokens contrasts with an earlier 30/30 held-out result at 32 tokens. The current run is neither a replication on the same cohort nor evidence that the earlier result was wrong; it does show that a calibrated margin threshold cannot be treated as an exactness guarantee. The mismatching guard prompt is `cnndm_006`.

The matched [`cnndm_006` first-divergence trace](cnndm_006_guard_first_divergence.json) uses the pinned Qwen revision, BNB NF4 with double quantization, SDPA and 64 output tokens. Two vanilla repeats agree. The first differing output is token index **12**: vanilla emits `5899`, guard emits `4545`. The first recorded cache hash difference appears at output start 3, after a previous batched verification. At output start 11, a single-token fallback using the active cache selects pending token `4545`; that pending token is emitted at output start 12. The margin guard triggers **during** the cycle at output start 12 and rebuilds canonical KV, but it does not reselect the already chosen pending token. This trace explains this concrete failure path. It does not prove that every guard mismatch follows the same path.

## BNB NF4, same GPU, three 256-token diagnostic prompts

The [paired rows](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n3_256/rows.jsonl), [run manifest](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n3_256/manifest.json) and [analysis](pilot_rtx4050_bnb_n3_256/analysis.json) cover one previously used prompt from each of GSM8K, HumanEval and CNN/DailyMail. **Every method generated 256 tokens on each prompt.** This is a one-session stress pilot, not the frozen held-out cohort or a three-model/two-GPU benchmark.

| Method | Whole sequences matching vanilla | Mean paired latency ratio | Mean peak allocated VRAM |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Vanilla | 3/3 | 1.000 | 2211 MB |
| Batched PLD | 1/3 | 1.433 | 2216 MB |
| Sequential PLD | 3/3 | 1.021 | 2216 MB |
| Existing margin guard | 3/3 | 0.061 | 2503 MB |

The batched ratio **cannot be described as an exact-decoding speedup** on its two mismatching prompts. The guard's measured canonical replay used an average 94.1% of its request time. Even its conditional free-replay cost calculation reached only 0.992 mean latency ratio, with one of three prompts above 1. Sequential PLD happened to match on all three and averaged 1.021, but empirical equality on three sequences is not a theorem. These results strengthen the case for a shape-invariant verification path or a genuine state-error certificate before policy tuning.

## Single-prefix linear shape probe

The [`Qwen2.5-0.5B BNB probe`](qwen05b_bnb_linear_shape_probe.json) compared five candidate-token positions after the same 75-token prefix. Ordinary batched verification differed from sequential verification in logits by 0.1875–0.2734 max absolute per row, flipped one of five row argmaxes, and produced nonzero KV differences starting at layer 1. A diagnostic variant forced all 169 quantized linear/LM-head modules to invoke their original kernel separately on each token row (845 one-row calls). Its five argmaxes matched sequential verification, **but logits and KV still differed**, with the first KV difference again at layer 1. Thus quantized linear execution shape can affect a decision in this one prefix, while changing linear calls alone does not establish whole-model shape invariance. The variant is intentionally slow and is not a decoding algorithm or proof.

The same probe on the main [`Qwen2.5-3B target`](qwen3b_bnb_linear_shape_probe.json) used five candidate positions after a 37-token prefix. Both ordinary batched and singleton-linear batched execution matched sequential argmax on all five rows, yet both had nonzero logit and KV differences, again starting at layer 1. This second prefix supports the narrower conclusion that normalizing only the quantized linear calls does **not** restore state equivalence. The operator-hook audit below localizes one early difference.

A third probe on [`Llama-3.2-1B`](llama1b_bnb_linear_shape_probe.json) also found nonzero logit and KV differences beginning at layer 1 after a 75-token prefix. All five argmax decisions happened to match in both batched variants. These are three **single-prefix diagnostics**, not the planned three-model, 256-token validation.

The instrumented module audits on [`Qwen2.5-0.5B`](qwen05b_bnb_operator_shape_audit.json), [`Qwen2.5-3B`](qwen3b_bnb_operator_shape_audit.json) and [`Llama-3.2-1B`](llama1b_bnb_operator_shape_audit.json) all found the same candidate intrinsic shape-sensitive operator: `model.layers.0.self_attn.q_proj`. In each audit, the recorded primary input tensor was bitwise equal between one-token and five-token execution, while the 4-bit projection output differed by a maximum of 0.0625, 0.0625 and 0.03125 respectively. This directly localizes one early source of shape-dependent numerical error under BNB NF4. Hooks record only the primary tensor input and first tensor output; the result does not establish that q_proj is the only source, and it does not supply a global error bound.

### Phi-3.5-Mini BNB shape probe

To add an independent architecture family, [`microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct`](https://huggingface.co/microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) was loaded at pinned revision `2fe192450127e6a83f7441aef6e3ca586c338b77` in BNB NF4, double-quantized FP16 compute and SDPA. On the previously used 481-token `cnndm_002` development prefix, M=3/5/9 each had matching argmaxes at every candidate row, while ordinary batched logits differed from sequential by up to 0.53125/0.578125/0.578125 and active KV differed at all three M values. Splitting 4-bit linears into single-row kernel calls did not restore KV equality.

The [operator-hook audit](phi35_cnndm002_operator_shape_k4_v1.json) found 129/129 hooked modules with differing outputs overall. The first hooked module with bitwise-equal primary input but differing output was Phi's `model.layers.0.self_attn.qkv_proj` (output max difference 0.015625). At M=5 the first differing KV layer moved from layer 0 in ordinary batching to layer 1 with singleton-linear calls. See the [consolidated report](phi35_shape_forensics_cnndm002_v1.json) and raw [M=3](phi35_cnndm002_shape_k2_v1.json), [M=5](phi35_cnndm002_shape_k4_v1.json), and [M=9](phi35_cnndm002_shape_k8_v1.json) probes. This is one prefix and no multi-cycle token divergence was measured; it extends the shape-sensitivity observation beyond Qwen/Llama but does not establish B1 correctness on Phi.

### Cross-model shape-sensitivity sweep

To test whether the numeric effect was confined to one checkpoint, M=5 was measured under the same BNB NF4/FP16/SDPA runtime on two common long development prompts (`cnndm_001` and `cnndm_002`) across seven checkpoints: Qwen2.5 0.5B/1.5B/3B, Llama 3.2 1B/3B, Phi-3.5-Mini 3.8B, and OLMo 2 1B. The [14-configuration aggregate](crossmodel_shape_sweep_m5_two_prompts_v1.json) shows nonzero logit and active-KV differences in **14/14** configurations. Candidate-row argmax agreed in 69/70 positions; the lone mismatch was Qwen2.5-0.5B on `cnndm_001`. After splitting quantized linear calls into singleton rows, candidate argmax agreed in 70/70 positions, but KV still differed in 14/14 configurations.

Instrumented M=5 runs locate the first equal-primary-input/different-output module in the initial Q/K projection family: `q_proj` for Qwen2.5-3B, Llama 3.2-3B and OLMo 2 1B; Phi uses fused `qkv_proj`. See the [Qwen](crossmodel_operator_qwen25_3b_cnndm002_m5_v1.json), [Llama](crossmodel_operator_llama32_3b_cnndm002_m5_v1.json), [OLMo](crossmodel_operator_olmo2_1b_cnndm002_m5_v1.json), and [Phi](phi35_cnndm002_operator_shape_k4_v1.json) audits. This supports a general claim about **shape-sensitive BNB NF4 numerical state changes across these four architecture families in this pinned runtime**. It does not show universal token divergence: only one of 70 candidate-row decisions flipped, two prompts are a narrow sample, and fixed-row B1 remains implemented only for Qwen2. To claim broad model support, the next step is a model-adapter/capability contract that validates the relevant projection, attention, cache, and rollback properties per architecture and fails closed when they are not established.

#### Locked cross-task and M expansion

The [locked protocol](crossmodel_shape_sweep_protocol_v1.json), [frozen 9-prompt cohort](crossmodel_shape_cohort_v1.jsonl), [aggregate run summary](crossmodel_shape_sweep_v1/summary.json), and [stratified analysis](crossmodel_shape_sweep_v1/analysis.json) extend that screening to 7 pinned checkpoints × 3 tasks × 3 proposal lengths (M=3/5/9), for 189 completed configurations with no failures. The cohort uses three development prompts each from GSM8K, HumanEval and CNN/DailyMail; GSM8K test indices 300–349 were excluded. Backend remained BNB NF4, double quantization, FP16 compute and SDPA on one RTX 4050 runtime.

Ordinary batched verification had nonzero logit drift in **189/189** configurations and active-KV drift in **188/189**. Candidate-row argmax matched in **1052/1071 (98.2%)** positions; 18/189 configurations had at least one flipped candidate-row argmax. This is a prefix-local diagnostic and must not be reported as a full output exactness rate. Every task stratum had logit drift in 63/63 configurations; active-KV drift occurred in 62/63 GSM8K and 63/63 for HumanEval and CNN/DailyMail. At M=3/5/9, argmax agreement was 186/189, 309/315 and 557/567 respectively, so there is no evidence here that larger M removes the numerical drift.

| Checkpoint | Configs with logit drift | Configs with KV drift | Ordinary candidate argmax | After singleton-linear intervention |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Qwen2.5-0.5B | 27/27 | 27/27 | 147/153 | 149/153 |
| Qwen2.5-1.5B | 27/27 | 27/27 | 151/153 | 149/153 |
| Qwen2.5-3B | 27/27 | 27/27 | 151/153 | 153/153 |
| Llama-3.2-1B | 27/27 | 26/27 | 153/153 | 153/153 |
| Llama-3.2-3B | 27/27 | 27/27 | 151/153 | 153/153 |
| Phi-3.5-Mini | 27/27 | 27/27 | 150/153 | 150/153 |
| OLMo-2-1B | 27/27 | 27/27 | 149/153 | 151/153 |

The singleton-linear intervention increased aggregate argmax matches from 1052 to 1058, but left logit drift in 188/189 and KV drift in 188/189 configurations. Its effect varied by checkpoint: it repaired the candidate-row flips for Llama-3.2-3B and Qwen2.5-3B, while Qwen2.5-1.5B had two more flips afterward. Thus this intervention is evidence about one possible source of shape sensitivity, not a general correction. The measured signal generalizes across all seven checkpoints, four architecture families, three tasks and three M values in this pinned backend/runtime. It still does not establish universal model/runtime behavior, B1 correctness outside the Qwen2 implementation, multi-cycle token equivalence, or full-sequence exactness.

#### Expanded prompt cohort and independent session repeat

The next locked protocol used 10 prompts per task from the local extended development sources (`gsm8k_ext150`, `humaneval_ext114`, and `cnndm_ext150`), 7 pinned checkpoints, M=3/5/9, and two separate Python processes. Each session completed all **630/630** configurations without failure. The two sessions reproduced the candidate IDs and every ordinary and singleton-linear logit/KV metric exactly in **630/630** matched configurations. The selected IDs were absent from earlier exact-feasibility shape-sweep result files, though some had appeared in general benchmark runs elsewhere in this workspace; this is broader development evidence, not an untouched held-out cohort. The exact prompt/source hashes and run manifests are in the [locked protocol](crossmodel_shape_repeat_protocol_v1.json), [cohort](crossmodel_shape_expanded_cohort_v1.jsonl), and [session directories](crossmodel_shape_repeat_v1/).

Across either session, ordinary verification had logit drift in **626/630** configurations and active-KV drift in **623/630**. Candidate-row argmax agreed in **3539/3570 (99.1%)** positions; 31/630 configurations had at least one flipped candidate-row argmax. Per checkpoint, logit drift was present in 86–90/90 configurations, KV drift in 86–90/90, and candidate-row argmax agreement ranged from 499/510 for Phi-3.5-Mini to 510/510 for OLMo 2 1B. By task, logit drift was 206/210 on GSM8K and 210/210 on each of HumanEval and CNN/DailyMail. The detailed model/task/M splits and session comparison are in [analysis.json](crossmodel_shape_repeat_v1/analysis.json).

One repeatable outlier is Phi-3.5-Mini on `gsm8k_059`, M=9. Both sessions used identical candidate IDs and recorded max logit difference **25.125**, KV key difference **12.875**, and KV value difference **17.46875**. This occurs at candidate row 5 (zero-based), whose candidate token is newline (ID 13); all nine candidate-row argmax decisions nevertheless match. Singleton-linear calls reduce the KV maxima in that configuration to 4.3046875/3.375 but leave a 24.640625 maximum logit difference. It is a reproducible state/logit divergence for this particular continuation, not a token-row flip or a full-generation mismatch measurement. The broad cohort therefore strengthens reproducibility of the finite-precision shape-sensitivity claim while also showing that argmax flips are sparse and that error magnitude can be highly prompt/model/continuation dependent.

The [layerwise trace](phi35_gsm8k059_m9_error_growth_diagnosis_v1.json) localizes the first recorded perturbation to `model.layers.0.self_attn.qkv_proj`: its primary input is bitwise equal, while its output differs by 0.03125. Candidate row 5 then grows to a maximum decoder-layer output difference of 0.5 at layer 12, 2.625 at layer 13, 11.75 at layer 19, and 732.75 at layer 29. The largest late module deltas are in MLP `down_proj` at layers 25 and 29–31. This supports an early shape-dependent perturbation followed by depth-wise amplification in this trajectory; the hooks do not observe every internal attention argument/state and cannot establish a unique causal chain.

## Legacy KV-drift trace

[`legacy_drift_lead_summary.json`](legacy_drift_lead_summary.json) reanalyzes an older 16-prompt NF4 trace. Eight prompts had a token divergence within the trace; seven of those had the first recorded KV drift in an earlier cycle. All eight prompts without token divergence also had recorded KV drift. This diagnostic cohort was selected for numerical investigation, and the first-drift ordering is descriptive rather than a causal proof. It demonstrates why token equality at one step is insufficient to certify later steps.

## HQQ/GemLite, same GPU, 3-prompt diagnostic pilot

Source: [`pilot_rtx4050_hqq_n3/rows.jsonl`](pilot_rtx4050_hqq_n3/rows.jsonl) and [`analysis.json`](pilot_rtx4050_hqq_n3/analysis.json). At a 64-token budget, all four methods matched **HQQ's own** vanilla token sequence on these three prompts. Mean paired latency ratios were 0.895 for batched PLD, 0.767 for sequential PLD, and 0.184 for the guard. Replay occupied 79.0% of measured guard time; its free-restoration cost counterfactual was 0.856. The sample is too small for a backend ranking, and the short warmup may not cover all HQQ execution shapes. Cross-backend token equality measures quantization fidelity, not speculative correctness.

## B1 fixed-row prototype

The implementation is in [`src/zassd/certification/qwen2_padded.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/qwen2_padded.py), [`src/zassd/decoding/b1_exact.py`](../../src/zassd/decoding/b1_exact.py), and runner [`scripts/run_b1_block_pilot.py`](../../scripts/run_b1_block_pilot.py). The singleton `b1_exact_generate` uses the original one-token target call and canonical cache. The fixed-row reference pads each decode-time Qwen2 linear/RMSNorm call to a pinned physical row count and computes attention one query at a time against the corresponding causal KV prefix. The block prototype applies that same physical row count to the speculative block, crops cache to the accepted prefix, and keeps draft tokens out of target KV.

The first block probe exposed an important dispatch difference: passing an all-zero causal mask changed SDPA results even when query, key and value shapes matched. The `rowwise` attention path therefore truncates K/V to the logical causal prefix and passes no mask. An alternate `padded` path pads Q/K/V and uses an explicit causal mask so every singleton and block call uses fixed SDPA shapes. On the pinned Qwen2.5-3B NF4 setup, the padded path matched logits and KV bitwise for M=1..9 in the recorded single-prefix probe [`b1_probe_qwen3b_rows9_padded.json`](b1_probe_qwen3b_rows9_padded.json). These probes are finite implementation checks, not a proof for every prompt or runtime.

| Model / run | Budget, K, fixed rows | Match to fixed-row singleton | Match to original BNB greedy | Mean latency ratio vs original BNB |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Qwen2.5-0.5B, 3 development prompts | 64, K=4, rows=5 | 3/3; shared cycle-frontier KV hashes match | 3/3 | 1.096 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-0.5B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=2, rows=9 | 3/3 | 2/3 | 1.382 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-0.5B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=4, rows=9 | 3/3 | 2/3 | 1.795 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-0.5B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=8, rows=9 | 3/3 | 2/3 | 2.257 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 64, K=4, rows=5 | 3/3; shared cycle-frontier KV hashes match | 3/3 | 0.733 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=4, rows=9 | 3/3 | 1/3 | 0.921 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=8, rows=9 | 3/3 | 1/3 | 1.017 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=2, rows=3, padded attention | 3/3; 184/184, 146/146 and 184/184 shared frontier KV hashes match | 1/3 | 1.136 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=4, rows=5, padded attention | 3/3; 222/222, 117/117 and 149/149 shared frontier KV hashes match | 1/3 | 1.043 (one session) |
| Qwen2.5-3B, 3 development prompts | 256, K=8, rows=9, padded attention | 3/3; 214/214, 256/256 and 140/140 shared frontier KV hashes match | 1/3 | 1.111 (one session) |

### Repeated K sweep

The first three development prompts and the next row from each task were measured in two independent repeat rounds. Each prompt compares original BNB greedy, B1 singleton, and B1 block. Method order rotates by prompt and round; K order was K=2/4/8 in round 0 and K=8/4/2 in round 1. The added prompts (`gsm8k_001`, `HumanEval/1`, `cnndm_001`) have appeared in earlier workspace experiments, so this is a wider B1 development measurement, not fresh or held-out data. Full raw rows and manifests are in the run directories under the [`first prompt-window runs`](b1_repeat_s0_k2) and [`second prompt-window runs`](b1_extension_s0_k2_rows1) prefixes. The validated combined aggregate is [`b1_k_sweep_expanded_six_prompts_v2.json`](b1_k_sweep_expanded_six_prompts_v2.json).

| K / rows | Mean BNB speedup, round 0 / round 1 | Pooled mean / geometric mean | Exact B1; exact BNB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 / 3 | 1.095× / 1.081× | 1.088× / 1.078× | 12/12; 4/12 |
| 4 / 5 | 0.938× / 0.906× | 0.922× / 0.899× | 12/12; 2/12 |
| 8 / 9 | 0.978× / 0.955× | 0.967× / 0.945× | 12/12; 2/12 |

Across 12 paired prompt-round measurements, K=2 beat K=4 by a mean paired latency ratio of 1.178× and K=8 by 1.159× (other-K latency divided by K=2 latency). K=2 had the best BNB-relative speed in both rounds. The aggregate ratios still vary by task and prompt: K=2's six-prompt means were 0.989× on GSM8K, 1.219× on HumanEval and 1.057× on CNN-DM. Every block run matched its B1 singleton token sequence and every shared cycle-frontier KV digest matched. BNB sequence equality was 4/12 for K=2 and 2/12 for K=4/8. The added prompts had prior use elsewhere in the workspace, results are from one GPU and two rounds, and the sample remains too small for a general or held-out claim.

The separate single-prefix [`M=1..9` probes](b1_fixed_row_qwen05b_m9.json) and [`3B rowwise probe`](b1_fixed_row_qwen3b_m9.json) report bitwise-equal logits and KV for every tested M at one prefix per model. The padded path also passes [`M=1..3`](b1_probe_qwen3b_rows3_padded.json), [`M=1..5`](b1_probe_qwen3b_rows5_padded.json) and [`M=1..9`](b1_probe_qwen3b_rows9_padded.json) on one 3B prefix. The 64-token K=4/rows=5 runs compare 44–63 shared cycle-frontier state hashes per prompt; all shared hashes match. The 256-token runs compare token outputs and KV hashes only at shared cycle frontiers, not at every generated token. All prompts are previously used development inputs, and no held-out result is claimed.

This is a promising B1 prototype, not B2: fixed-row shape and selected attention strategy are part of the new greedy reference. Across six prompts, K=2 remains the strongest BNB-relative candidate among K=2/4/8 with 1.088× pooled mean and 1.078× geometric-mean speedup, but it is slower than BNB on some prompts and differs from BNB's token sequence on 8/12 paired observations. Against fixed-row singleton, mean speedup rises with K (1.469×, 1.557×, 1.622× for K=2/4/8). All configurations matched B1 outputs in all 12 prompt-round runs. The padded path removes the Python rowwise SDPA loop, but still uses padded BNB linear calls. On 0.5B, earlier rowwise runs were faster in three prompts but differed from original BNB on one prompt for K=2/4/8.

The implementation-specific [Qwen2 fixed-row operator contract](QWEN2_FIXED_ROW_OPERATOR_CONTRACT.md) is now written. The context manager rejects non-Qwen2 models, external masks/positions, and unsupported decode cache/modes; a guarded M=1..3 probe still matches bitwise. Padded token IDs still cannot be inferred reliably. The runner now records a packed model/quantization-state digest, and the branch audit exercises rejects, pending-token EOS, one accepted EOS draft, and budget completion. Broader cache-boundary coverage remains open. The conditional margin helper in `src/zassd/certification/greedy.py` still has no validated backend bounds.

### Per-cycle oracle pilot

The [K=1/2 cycle oracle](b1_cycle_oracle_k1_k2_v5.json) uses three previously seen development prompts, two process sessions, 64 output tokens and the fixed rows=3 reference. It includes measured draft/verify/commit wall times and keeps only actions whose token sequence and audited cache frontier match B1. Complete paths were found for 3/3 prompts. The median predicted speedup over B1 singleton was 1.211×; median gain over the best fixed-K path under the same additive cycle-cost model was only 1.0005×. This suggests no useful K-switching gain in this small sample. The oracle has future knowledge and omits selector, switch, checkpoint and broader cache-copy costs; treat it as an optimistic diagnostic, not an online policy or performance claim. Median cycle component-sum error was about 18 microseconds per cycle. As in the long-context pilot, cycle timing excludes trace-list append/state-observer hashing and the non-cycle residual is calibrated from observed runs; it may vary with cycle count.

The [independent repeat audit](b1_reference_repeat_audit_v3.json) also records 3/3 prompt token sequences and prefix KV digests equal across sessions for K=1/2. The GSM8K 300–349 cohort remains unused.

### Long-context, 256-token cycle and cache-cost audit

The [long-context cycle oracle](b1_cycle_oracle_long_cnndm_v2.json) uses the previously seen `cnndm_000` prompt (397 input tokens), 256 output tokens, fixed rows=3, and K=1/2 across two process sessions each. B1 block matched the singleton on 4/4 runs; original BNB matched 0/4. Singleton token and active-KV digests were identical across all four runs, with all shared block frontiers equal. The oracle path completed and used 184 cycle actions (91 K=1, 92 K=2, plus an already-pending terminal token); it predicted 1.456× versus singleton and only 1.0217× over fixed K=2 under the additive cycle model. Cycle intervals omit trace-list append and state-observer hashing; the per-request residual is calibrated from observed runs and may shift with a different cycle count. It switched K 64 times and saved about 133 ms in its idealized estimate, so the break-even unmodeled switch cost is about 2.07 ms each. This remains a one-prompt hindsight ceiling without selector/checkpoint costs.

The [per-schedule summary](b1_long_context_k_schedule_summary_v1.json) extends this same long-context prompt to K=4/rows=5 and K=8/rows=9, each with a separate singleton contract. Across two sessions per schedule, B1 block matched its own reference and shared KV state 2/2 for K=1,2,4,8; original BNB matched 0/2 in each group. Median block-over-singleton speed ratios were 1.294×, 1.456×, 1.733× and 1.832× respectively. These are within-contract measurements; physical row count changes the singleton reference, so they are not one common token-trajectory comparison. K4 and K8 per-cycle traces have complete paths under their own contracts and show no adaptive gain over their corresponding fixed K.

Contract-bound [operator probes](b1_operator_probe_long_cnndm_rows3_v1.json), [rows=5](b1_operator_probe_long_cnndm_rows5_v1.json) and [rows=9](b1_operator_probe_long_cnndm_rows9_v1.json) use the same 397-token prompt. They found bitwise-equal logits and active KV for M=1..3, M=1..5 and M=1..9 respectively. At M=2, the instrumented module outputs and attention tensors also showed no differences. These are finite same-prefix probes; the hook captures only selected module inputs/outputs and do not establish the operator contract universally.

The [cache primitive benchmark](b1_cache_checkpoint_cost_cnndm_v1.json) measured a 24.0 MB active KV prefix at length 652. Median host-wall times were about 0.011 ms for zero-copy fork, 0.005 ms for logical crop/length restore, 0.441 ms to restore an existing checkpoint, and 0.897 ms for clone plus restore. A hypothetical full clone/restore at each of the oracle's 64 K switches would cost about 57 ms, consuming part of the 133 ms idealized margin. This is a diagnostic cost model: the implementation does not yet use this checkpoint design, and the measured primitive may not represent an optimized checkpoint path.

The long-context repeat is recorded in [the state audit](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm_v1.json). The long-context test still covers one previously used prompt; no held-out data was used.

The [branch coverage report](b1_branch_coverage_long_cnndm_v1.json) isolates `cnndm_000` across the same two-session traces. Every K exercised accept-all and rejection: rejects covered position 0 for K=1; positions 0–1 for K=2; 0–3 for K=4; and 0–7 for K=8. All 8 runs matched their own B1 tokens and passed shared-frontier KV audits. This is useful branch coverage on one output trajectory, not coverage of every cache state or terminal condition.

To check whether fixed-row operator equality repeats on a different input, [`cnndm_001`](b1_long_context_cnndm_001_prompt.jsonl) (398 BPE tokens after tokenization) was probed under the rows=3/5/9 contracts. All M=1..3, M=1..5, and M=1..9 respectively had bitwise-equal logits and active KV, identical argmaxes, and matching cache lengths. At M=2, module and attention tensor differences were empty under each contract. See [rows=3](b1_operator_probe_cnndm001_rows3_v1.json), [rows=5](b1_operator_probe_cnndm001_rows5_v1.json), and [rows=9](b1_operator_probe_cnndm001_rows9_v1.json). This adds a second development prefix only; it is still finite empirical evidence.

### Second long-context prompt: paired B1 cycle audit

The [two-session aggregate](b1_multisession_long_cnndm001_v1.json) extends the 256-token cycle audit to `cnndm_001` (398 input tokens), using independent B1 contracts for K=2/rows=3, K=4/rows=5, and K=8/rows=9. All six blocks matched their own singleton token sequence, and every shared-frontier state audit passed. The three [singleton repeat audits](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm001_k2_v1.json) ([K=4](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm001_k4_v1.json), [K=8](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm001_k8_v1.json)) also pass: input identity, singleton tokens, and full recorded singleton state traces match between sessions within each contract.

| K / rows | B1 exact, sessions | BNB exact | Mean speedup vs B1 singleton | Mean speedup vs original BNB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 / 3 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.413× | 1.021× |
| 4 / 5 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.464× | 0.912× |
| 8 / 9 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.543× | 0.909× |

K=2 led K=4 and K=8 in both sessions; paired block latency ratios favor K=2 by 1.173× and 1.129× on average. The [branch audit](b1_branch_coverage_long_cnndm001_v2.json) covered rejects at positions 0–1, 0–3 and 0–7 respectively, plus accept-all and budget termination. This supports cycle/state behavior on a second long prompt, but it is one development input with two sessions on one GPU. It does not show B2 compatibility: all six runs differed from the original BNB greedy sequence.

The same protocol was then run on [`cnndm_002`](b1_long_context_cnndm_002_prompt.jsonl), a 419-token CNN/DM prompt already present in an older BNB pilot. The [two-session aggregate](b1_multisession_long_cnndm002_v1.json), [K2](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm002_k2_v1.json), [K4](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm002_k4_v1.json), [K8](b1_reference_repeat_long_cnndm002_k8_v1.json) singleton repeats and [branch report](b1_branch_coverage_long_cnndm002_v1.json) all pass. All six block runs match B1 tokens and shared frontier state; all six differ from original BNB.

| K / rows | B1 exact, sessions | BNB exact | Mean speedup vs B1 singleton | Mean speedup vs original BNB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2 / 3 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.376× | 1.003× |
| 4 / 5 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.579× | 0.995× |
| 8 / 9 | 2/2 | 0/2 | 1.651× | 0.991× |

Unlike `cnndm_001`, K choice is effectively tied here: paired session ratios vary around 1.0 and no setting is consistently faster across K. All three are close to the original BNB latency, with ratios varying by session around 0.97–1.02. Together, the two prompts show input-dependent K performance: K=2 led on `cnndm_001`, while K=2/4/8 were close on `cnndm_002`. This is still a small development sample, not a basis for freezing an adaptive policy or claiming a general speedup.

For reference, the [two-prompt synthesis](b1_long_context_two_prompt_synthesis_v1.json) gives equal weight to each prompt's two sessions. Across four paired prompt-session runs per K, B1 exactness is 4/4 for every K and BNB sequence exactness is 0/4. Mean speedup versus BNB is 1.012× for K=2, 0.954× for K=4 and 0.950× for K=8; mean speedup versus the within-contract B1 singleton rises from 1.394× to 1.522× and 1.597×. These descriptive averages combine per-prompt measurements, not token trajectories or reference contracts.

### Per-output-prefix active-KV audit

The runner now has a diagnostic audit mode that hashes the block cache at every committed output prefix. At each cycle boundary it hashes intermediate prefixes directly from the active K/V buffers, without changing logical cache lengths; thus the observation includes prefixes traversing later reject/crop cycles. On `cnndm_002`, K=2/rows=3, K=4/rows=5 and K=8/rows=9 each matched the B1 singleton at all 256 recorded prefix states (output prefixes 0–255), including the final canonical cache before the pending budget token. The [aggregate](b1_full_prefix_state_audit_cnndm002_v1.json) and [K2](b1_cycle_long_cnndm002_k2_allprefix_s0_v1/rows.jsonl), [K4](b1_cycle_long_cnndm002_k4_allprefix_s0_v1/rows.jsonl), and [K8](b1_cycle_long_cnndm002_k8_allprefix_s0_v1/rows.jsonl) raw rows contain the hashes. All three sequences still differ from original BNB. This closes the sampling gap for active KV on this finite prompt/runtime; the digest does not include unused static-cache suffix bytes or every hidden backend state, and one session per K is not a universal proof.

### Runtime identity and branch audit

The pinned [B1 reference contract](b1_reference_contract_v3.json) has ID `ca155f567ba080347f765335e8999b0256abafe87c88c0263ac38344b2b2ca27` for Qwen2.5-3B NF4 with padded physical rows=3 (K≤2), static KV capacity 2048, and the captured runtime/source hashes. The runner accepts a matching runtime and rejects a rowwise attention mismatch before writing a manifest or decoding. Its [independent repeat audit](b1_reference_repeat_audit_v3.json) covers three development prompts in two process sessions: singleton tokens and active-KV digest traces match 3/3, and block matches singleton 6/6. This finite repeat is not a correctness proof.

Pilot manifests now use schema `b1-fixed-row-v2`, record a SHA-256 `contract_id`, and capture GPU UUID/compute capability/driver when `nvidia-smi` is available, CUDA/PyTorch deterministic and SDPA flags, model-config and tokenizer vocabulary/special-token/template hashes, and source hashes for the schedule, decoder, candidate lookup, cache, backend loader and runner. The runner now records `quantized_state_sha256`; the latest EOS-candidate run has digest `c6212d37bd02338c60536b1ca674b3e0c76fbcd810ba5df8e8e28b0e16f4f6cf`. The digest covers named model parameters and buffers plus recursively exposed bitsandbytes quantization-state metadata/tensors. This makes loaded-state identification stronger, but it does not prove the kernel behavior or capture hidden runtime state.

The [updated branch audit](b1_branch_coverage_audit_v4.json) combines the 36 long-run prompt-round outputs, three short pending-token EOS prompts, and one targeted EOS-draft prompt. K=2 exercised accept-all and rejects at draft positions 0–1; K=4 at 0–3; K=8 at 0–7. All 40 outputs matched their B1 singleton token sequence and passed shared-frontier state audits. The targeted trace accepted EOS as a draft candidate (`draft_tokens=[151645]`, `accepted=1`) and terminated without consuming another target token. In total, the audit records four EOS outputs, one EOS-draft cycle, 36 budget outputs, and no unexpected stops. Some budget endings emit the already pending token without a final cycle trace.

This is finite branch evidence, not exhaustive cache-crop or combined EOS/budget coverage; nonstandard-input rejection paths also remain to be validated. The new guards and manifest fields do not establish a certified-mode claim.

#### Architecture capability gate

[`model_capabilities.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/model_capabilities.py) adds a fail-closed preflight for experimental fixed-row B1 schedules. It registers Qwen2 plus checkpoint-scoped adapters for both pinned Llama-3.2-1B/3B checkpoints, `microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct`, and `allenai/OLMo-2-0425-1B-Instruct`, checking model/config identity and pinned revision, eval mode, SDPA, expected attention module, supported attention strategy and runtime preconditions. Phi and OLMo support remain limited to their pinned checkpoints. These are empirical prototypes, not formal proofs.

The [runtime architecture inventory v8](crossmodel_b1_architecture_inventory_v8.json) loaded all seven pinned checkpoints and matched each first-layer profile with no missing required modules. The capability gate accepts the three Qwen2 checkpoints plus pinned Llama-3.2-1B/3B, Phi-3.5-Mini and OLMo-2-0425-1B. This inventory validates structural layout and checkpoint-scoped fail-closed dispatch, not numerical equivalence or a general theorem.

#### Llama-3.2-1B fixed-row adapter

The Llama-specific fixed-row schedule and architecture dispatcher are implemented in [`llama_padded.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/llama_padded.py) and [`fixed_row_dispatch.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/fixed_row_dispatch.py). For the pinned `unsloth/Llama-3.2-1B-Instruct` revision `5a8abab4a5d6f164389b1079fb721cfab8d7126c`, BNB NF4/FP16 compute and SDPA, the sequential-versus-batched probes matched logits, candidate argmax, and active KV prefixes bitwise for every M=1…9 on GSM8K, CNN/DailyMail and HumanEval. Both padded and rowwise attention strategies passed these shape probes.

The B1 pilots compared fixed-row block decoding to fixed-row singleton decoding over 64 output tokens on one prompt from each task. Every token and committed KV prefix matched for all 192 prefixes per attention strategy. Each run had 152 block cycles, 140 draft tokens and 40 accepted draft tokens; 29 cycles rejected a draft at positions 0, 1 or 3. Artifacts are in [`llama32_1b_b1_adapter_pilot_v1`](llama32_1b_b1_adapter_pilot_v1/) and [`llama32_1b_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1`](llama32_1b_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1/), with shape probes named `llama32_1b_fixed_row_*_rows9_v1.json`. This is a narrow, same-runtime development pilot: it does not establish performance gains, held-out accuracy, other Llama checkpoints, other backends, or formal all-input exactness.

The pinned Llama-3.2-3B revision `006f5dcd1393c3add266de40994ba96225e9689d` also passed all six M=1…9 probes (three tasks × two attention strategies) and both 64-token B1 pilots: 3/3 token matches and 192/192 KV-prefix matches per strategy. Its artifacts are `llama32_3b_fixed_row_*_rows9_v1.json` and [`llama32_3b_b1_adapter_pilot_v1`](llama32_3b_b1_adapter_pilot_v1/), [`llama32_3b_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1`](llama32_3b_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1/). This adds a second model size in the Llama family, within the same pinned runtime and development cohort.

The [paired performance pilot](llama32_3b_perf_rowwise_session0_v1/summary.json) adds two process sessions for each attention strategy on the same three prompts (12 paired rows total). Every block matched the fixed-row singleton (12/12), while only 8/12 matched original BNB greedy; both CNN/DailyMail runs per strategy differed from original BNB. Mean block speedup was **1.279× vs fixed-row singleton** (median 1.305×), but only **0.603× vs original BNB greedy**. The strategy means vs BNB were 0.590× rowwise and 0.616× padded. Thus the prototype's block path does not yet deliver an end-to-end speedup over the ordinary quantized baseline; fixed-row operator overhead dominates on this sample. These are short development measurements on one GPU and are not a performance claim.

A K/physical-row sweep compared padded K=1/rows=2, K=2/rows=3, K=3/rows=4 and K=4/rows=5 (two sessions × three prompts each). All 24 block outputs matched fixed-row singleton; 16/24 matched original BNB, with CNN/DailyMail mismatching in every BF16 run. Mean speed ratios against original BNB were **0.911× (K=1)**, **0.918× (K=2)**, **0.605× (K=3)** and **0.616× (K=4)**. K=2 was the best BF16 setting tested but remained slower than original BNB. A separate FP16 model-dtype K=2/rows=3 experiment matched original BNB on 6/6 rows, but its mean speed ratio was only **0.881×**. It improved absolute B1 block time in this sample but also sped up the vanilla baseline more; it is not enabled by default. Full aggregates are in [`llama32_3b_b1_tuning_summary_v1.json`](llama32_3b_b1_tuning_summary_v1.json). The session artifacts are under `llama32_3b_perf_padded_k*rows*_session*_v1/` and `llama32_3b_perf_fp16_k2rows3_padded_session*_v1/`.

The [operator profile](llama32_3b_ops_profile_padded_v1/analysis.json) shows that BNB 4-bit GEMM kernels accounted for **75.9%** of aggregate CUDA kernel duration (76.2% rowwise); Flash attention was under 1%. With K=4/rows=5, the 20-cycle trace recorded 3,360 BNB GEMMs with input `[1,5,3072]` and 560 with `[1,5,8192]`. The `[1,5,*]` shapes are the cost of padding every projected decode row to the fixed physical row count. Attention strategy changes the Flash-attention launch count substantially, but not the dominant 4-bit GEMM count. The current optimization target is therefore the quantized linear path and its fixed-row execution, not attention masking.

An opt-in Llama experiment reuses a per-linear output buffer for zero padding via `constant_pad_nd.out`, instead of allocating a new `F.pad` result on each call. The [within-process paired A/B](llama32_3b_reusable_pad_paired_ab_v1/summary.json) ran 12 pairs on the three prompts. Both variants matched singleton tokens and all KV prefixes in every run. Reuse-buffer speed was **0.967×** the fresh `F.pad` path (about 3.3% slower), so it remains disabled by default. Avoiding allocations alone did not reduce the dominant BNB GEMM time; the next optimization needs to target GEMM or reduce useful physical rows while preserving bitwise outputs.

A second opt-in path calls each BNB 4-bit linear once per sequence row, with rowwise attention, so the quantized GEMM sees M=1 instead of padded M=3. It passed logits/KV probes for M=1…9 on all three tasks (27/27 per measurement type), and matched singleton token/KV state in 6/6 paired pilot rows. It averaged **0.861× vs original BNB** and **1.061× vs fixed-row singleton**. This removes padded GEMM rows but loses the batching benefit; it is also not a speedup over the baseline. See the [summary artifact](llama32_3b_rowwise_linear_summary_v1.json). Neither opt-in experiment is enabled by default.

#### Phi-3.5-Mini fixed-row adapter

The Phi adapter in [`phi3_padded.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/phi3_padded.py) handles fused `qkv_proj`, rotary embeddings and the configured sliding-window boundary. It is enabled only for the exact pinned `microsoft/Phi-3.5-mini-instruct` revision `2fe192450127e6a83f7441aef6e3ca586c338b77` under BNB NF4/FP16 compute and SDPA. GSM8K, CNN/DailyMail and HumanEval probes at M=1…9 matched logits, candidate argmax and active KV prefixes bitwise under both padded and rowwise attention; see the six `phi35_fixed_row_*_rows9_v1.json` artifacts.

Both 64-token B1 pilots matched the fixed-row singleton token sequence on 3/3 prompts and all 192 committed KV prefixes per strategy. With K=4, each pilot ran 149 cycles, drafted 148 tokens and accepted 41. Artifacts are in [`phi35_b1_adapter_pilot_v1`](phi35_b1_adapter_pilot_v1/) and [`phi35_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1`](phi35_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1/). This is finite evidence on one checkpoint/runtime and three development prompts, with no performance claim or formal all-input guarantee.

#### OLMo-2-0425-1B fixed-row adapter

The OLMo-2 adapter in [`olmo2_padded.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/olmo2_padded.py) preserves separate Q/K/V projections, Q/K RMSNorm before rotary embedding, and OLMo-2's residual/MLP order. It is enabled only for the exact pinned `allenai/OLMo-2-0425-1B-Instruct` revision `48d788eca847d4d7548f375ad03d3c9312f6139e`, under BNB NF4/FP16 compute and SDPA. GSM8K, CNN/DailyMail and HumanEval probes at M=1…9 matched logits, candidate argmax and active KV prefixes bitwise under both padded and rowwise attention; see the six `olmo2_fixed_row_*_rows9_v1.json` artifacts.

Both 64-token B1 pilots matched the fixed-row singleton token sequence on 3/3 prompts and all 192 committed KV prefixes per strategy. Artifacts are in [`olmo2_b1_adapter_pilot_v1`](olmo2_b1_adapter_pilot_v1/) and [`olmo2_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1`](olmo2_b1_adapter_pilot_rowwise_v1/). This is finite evidence on one checkpoint/runtime and three development prompts, with no performance claim or formal all-input guarantee.

#### Qwen2 Triton NF4 linear prototype

An opt-in Qwen2 `triton_nf4` schedule was added at [`nf4_fixed_rows.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows.py). It reads packed NF4 and nested scale metadata directly, accumulates each output channel with an input-feature reduction, and launches independent programs per real token row. It avoids materializing an FP16 copy of the full weights. The pilot runner warms both singleton and block schedules before timing and records Triton version and kernel source hash.

A synthetic nested-NF4 microcase matched BNB dequantization followed by FP16 linear with **0 observed output difference**; repeated equal rows and the same row in singleton versus three-row calls were bitwise equal. On one 419-token development prompt (`cnndm_002`), Qwen2.5-3B, K=2/rows=3 and K=4/rows=5, padded SDPA, and a 32-token budget, both block outputs matched their Triton B1 singleton and active KV digests at all **32/32 committed prefixes**. The K=2 output also matched original BNB (32/32 tokens); K=4/R=5 did not. The [K2 run](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k2_rows3_v1/) records contract `c45ff588b1fd1f908cdf795f56139e32372f49e8a3db51d0e41600ad47c2edc0`; the [K4 run](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/) exercised four-candidate verification blocks (five input rows) in five cycles.

At K=2, paired latency was 2.347 s block, 2.024 s singleton and 0.960 s original BNB: **0.863×** speed versus B1 singleton and **0.409×** versus BNB. At K=4/R=5, latency was 2.761 s block, 2.027 s singleton and 0.973 s BNB: **0.734×** versus singleton and **0.352×** versus BNB. Peak allocated memory was 2228.9 MB and 2230 MB, respectively. The output tile sweep (N=16/32/64) and reduction tile sweep (K=128/256) did not improve on N=16/K=128; a tensor-core tile variant was also tried, preserved sampled tokens/state, but took about 13 s per method and was discarded. These are one-session measurements on one reused development prompt. The retained kernel has since been audited at PTX/SASS level for three linear dimensions and compared across R=1/R=5, but broader dimension/state stress and performance work remain open; G2 is not closed.

The [K4/R5 operator sweeps](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/operator_probe_m1_m5.json) compare singleton and block rows M=1…5 at the 419-token `cnndm_002` prefix and 398-token `cnndm_001` prefix under the same frozen contract. All ten prompt/row-size cases had bitwise-identical logits and active KV, identical argmax, and equal cache lengths; the recorded maximum logit difference was 0 for every M. At M=2 on both prompts, the selected module-output and attention-tensor diagnostics were also empty. The separate [PTX/SASS audit](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/kernel_code_audit/summary.json) inspected layer-0 q_proj (2048×2048), gate_proj (11008×2048), and down_proj (2048×11008) on RTX 4050 SM89 with Triton 3.6.0. For all three, PTX and cubin hashes were identical across R=1 and R=5; the launch-grid row count changed from 1 to 5. SASS used separate FADD/FMUL, with no FFMA/HMMA; it included shuffle and shared-barrier operations for the reduction. This is evidence that the inspected compiler binary does not specialize on row count for these dimensions and this toolchain. It does not prove every operator branch or future build, nor replace broader prompt/state stress. The second sweep artifact is [`operator_probe_cnndm001_m1_m5.json`](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/operator_probe_cnndm001_m1_m5.json); the R=1 machine-code comparison is in [`kernel_code_audit_rows1`](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/kernel_code_audit_rows1/).

The [synthetic boundary stress](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/boundary_stress.json) covers 15 `(K,N)` pairs around K tile/NF4 block boundaries 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, and 512, with output widths 15/16/17, plus three variants for non-nested scales and FP16 bias. Across 90 singleton-versus-M=1…5 first-row comparisons, every output was bitwise equal; repeating the same input across five rows also produced equal outputs in all 18 cases. Seventeen of 18 singleton microcases matched BNB dequantization followed by FP16 `linear` bitwise. One nested/no-bias `(K=130,N=17)` case differed by at most **1.22×10⁻⁴**. This is a small observed arithmetic difference against the BNB-derived calculation, while singleton/block invariance still held; it reinforces that the Triton path defines its own B1 reference and does not establish B2 equivalence. The harness is [`stress_nf4_fixed_rows_boundaries.py`](../../scripts/stress_nf4_fixed_rows_boundaries.py).

A paired q_proj microbenchmark on the pinned 3B model used 50 calls per sample and five rounds at each M. Median Triton/BNB CUDA-time ratios were **2.46× at M=1**, **9.19× at M=3**, and **8.00× at M=5** (2048×2048 projection). The M=5 medians were 0.345 ms for Triton and 0.0431 ms for BNB. This localizes a substantial cost in the current linear kernel itself, before attention or decoder overhead; the row-independent schedule is not yet viable as a speedup. See [`qproj_microbenchmark.json`](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_microbenchmark.json) and the reproducible [`profile_nf4_linear_qwen.py`](../../scripts/profile_nf4_linear_qwen.py).

An experimental [multi-row CTA kernel](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows_reuse.py) attempted to reuse a decoded weight tile across 2 or 4 rows. It stayed bitwise invariant against its singleton result at M=1/3/5, but a preliminary 20-call × 3-round q_proj benchmark was slower than the original row-independent Triton kernel at every M. At M=5, medians were 0.487 ms (2 rows/CTA), 0.554 ms (4 rows/CTA), and 0.411 ms (original Triton). This variant is not integrated into B1. The slowdown is consistent with expanded temporary tensors or register pressure, but occupancy/spill profiling has not confirmed the cause. See [`qproj_reuse_experiment.json`](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_reuse_experiment.json).

A further fixed-physical-row FP16 tensor-core prototype (`tl.dot`, 16 row slots, 32×32 output/reduction tiles) also kept row-0 output bitwise equal at M=1/3/5, but was slower: median q_proj CUDA time was 0.808/0.805/0.707 ms, or **23.4×/20.4×/13.4× BNB** for this short 10-call × 3-round exploration. A 64×64 tile exceeded RTX 4050 shared-memory capacity (110,592 required vs 101,376 available); the smaller tile compiled but did not improve performance. This path remains unintegrated and is a no-go in its current form. See [`qproj_tensorcore_experiment.json`](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_tensorcore_experiment.json) and [`nf4_fixed_rows_tensorcore.py`](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows_tensorcore.py).

#### Qwen2 group-scale NF4 schedule

The [group-scale kernel](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows_group_scale.py) loads one scale per 64-weight NF4 block and broadcasts it across the tile, reducing redundant metadata loads. The registered opt-in schedule uses `block_n=8, block_k=128` as a separate B1 reference and fails closed unless K and tile boundaries align to NF4 scale groups. At q_proj, this tile was bitwise invariant for M=1/3/5 on the same inputs and **43.7% faster at M=5** than the initial row-independent tile (0.181 ms vs 0.322 ms). It changes q_proj output relative to the initial N=16/K=128 schedule, so it has its own frozen [K4/R5 contract](triton_nf4_group_scale_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/reference_contract.json). Other tested group-scale tiles were slower or changed the reference; see the [tile sweep](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_group_scale_tile_sweep.json).

The group-scale operator probe matched logits and active KV bitwise for M=1…5 on both `cnndm_002` and `cnndm_001`, with no selected module or attention differences at M=2. Two 32-token K4/R5 pilots then matched their own B1 singleton at **32/32 tokens and 32/32 committed KV prefixes**; both happened to match original BNB token IDs too. The runner's paired speedup fields (singleton or BNB time divided by block time) were **0.773× vs singleton / 0.531× vs BNB** on `cnndm_002`, and **0.771× / 0.519×** on `cnndm_001`. Equivalently, block latency was **1.294× / 1.883×** the singleton/BNB latency on `cnndm_002`, and **1.297× / 1.927×** on `cnndm_001`. In a separate FP16 A/B on `cnndm_002`, group-scale block latency was 1.623 s versus 2.508 s for the initial Triton kernel, while both remained slower than BNB. This improved substantially over the initial prototype, but is still slower end-to-end than B1 singleton and BNB. Artifacts are in [`the K4/R5 group-scale run`](triton_nf4_group_scale_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/), [`the cnndm_001 run`](triton_nf4_group_scale_qwen3b_cnndm001_k4_rows5_v1/), and [`the matched FP16 A/B`](triton_nf4_fp16_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/).

Nsight Compute on q_proj shows why the grouped scale path helps: duration fell from **347.5 μs to 197.5 μs** in the profiled M=5 launch; registers fell from 70 to 40 per thread and achieved occupancy rose from 55.3% to 95.1%. Excessive global sectors dropped from 73% to 63%; L1/TEX remains heavily used and stores are still uncoalesced, so more work is needed. The [group-scale report](triton_nf4_group_scale_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_ncu_full.txt) and [initial-kernel report](triton_nf4_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_ncu_full.txt) retain the full metrics.

#### K=256 grouped-scale reduction tile

An opt-in `triton_nf4_group_scale_k256` schedule now pins `block_n=8, block_k=256` and has its own runtime identity/reference contract ([contract](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/reference_contract.json), ID `10b2da991db49afae753515a4681dc7c5d22262080980647212f370a8fb12c23`). On `cnndm_002`, its M=1…5 operator probe and 32-token K4/R5 pilot matched the K256 singleton logits/KV and all 32 committed prefixes; it also matched BNB token IDs in this finite run. The second 32-token K4/R5 pilot on `cnndm_001` likewise matched singleton and BNB token IDs, with 32/32 committed KV prefixes equal ([pilot](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k4_rows5_v1/)).

The initial K256 block pilot took 1.5819 s vs 1.2131 s singleton and 0.8677 s BNB on `cnndm_002` (block latency **1.304× / 1.823×**), and 1.6231 s vs 1.2062 s singleton and 0.8840 s BNB on `cnndm_001` (**1.346× / 1.836×**). The K256 operator probe now covers M=1…5 on both prompts; on `cnndm_001`, logits and KV were bitwise equal at every M, with no selected module or attention tensor differences at M=2 ([probe](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k4_rows5_v1/operator_probe_cnndm001_m1_m5.json)). A separate q_proj tile sweep found N8/K256 about 9.5% faster than N8/K128 at M=5, while the full-model cnndm_002 pilot was only about 2.5% faster than the separate K128 run.

Three new process sessions per prompt retained token equality vs both B1 and BNB and 32/32 matching committed KV prefixes in each run. Across the six paired observations, geometric-mean block latency was **1.300×** B1 singleton (range 1.288–1.307×; descriptive paired log-t 95% interval 1.290–1.309×) and **1.815×** original BNB (range 1.762–1.845×; interval 1.782–1.848×). The intervals use only six paired measurements on two fixed prompts and are diagnostic. Verification used 25–28 target calls but evaluated 48–50 target rows per 32 output tokens, versus 31 singleton calls; draft acceptance was 6/25 on `cnndm_001` and 3/20 on `cnndm_002`. This low acceptance plus the current per-row kernel cost explains why reducing target-call count did not reduce latency. K256 preserves the observed shape invariant in the tested domain but clearly misses the end-to-end speed gate. All BNB matches are finite observations, and K128 and K256 are distinct reference schedules. See the [paired multi-session summary](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_paired_multisession_summary_v1.json).

An Nsight Compute profile of the exact K256 q_proj launch measured 177.38 μs under profiling (M=5), versus 197.50 μs for K128 under the same full metric set. Both used 40 registers/thread and reached about 95% occupancy with no spills. K256 still had 62% excessive global sectors (5.08M of 8.20M) and 88.3% L1/TEX throughput; global-access efficiency remains a kernel optimization target. These profiler timings are diagnostic, not end-to-end timings. See the [K256 NCU report](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_ncu_full.txt).

A K-major traversal prototype was tested against the current N8/K256 tile on q_proj. It remained bitwise invariant across M=1/3/5 against its own singleton, but was **1.35× slower at M=1 and 1.50× slower at M=3/5**. Its output differed from the current K256 schedule by up to `4.88e-4` at M=5, so it would need a separate contract without a performance case to justify one. This layout change is a no-go and is not integrated into model dispatch. See the [comparison artifact](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_kmajor_layout_sweep_v2.json), [prototype kernel](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows_group_scale_kmajor.py), and [benchmark harness](../../scripts/benchmark_nf4_kmajor_qproj.py).

#### Packed-byte K256 strategy

The new opt-in `triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256` loads each packed NF4 byte once, looks up both high/low nibbles, and interleaves them back into original K order before reduction. It is a distinct reference schedule (contract ID `cf0d7b2094c6eb4a794e07e551c9fd5f44e536d5092a9dc99ce4ae64b9673fdc`). Two fresh q_proj microbenchmark processes gave packed/current latency ratios about 0.67 at M=1, 0.70 at M=3, and 0.70 at M=5. The packed output differs from the previous K256 reduction schedule by up to `1.22e-4` at M=5, while each schedule independently remained bitwise invariant across M=1/3/5.

NCU q_proj duration fell from 177.38 μs to 124.61 μs (M=5, profiled); excessive global sectors fell from 62% to 15% (4.88M/8.20M down to 0.488M/3.283M), at the cost of 56 rather than 40 registers/thread and 71.3% rather than 95.0% achieved occupancy. Three paired process sessions per prompt then matched B1 tokens, BNB tokens, and all 32 committed KV prefixes on both `cnndm_001` and `cnndm_002`. Geometric-mean block latency was **1.197×** B1 singleton (range 1.172–1.223×; diagnostic paired log-t 95% interval 1.173–1.220×) and **1.497×** original BNB (range 1.477–1.537×; interval 1.472–1.522×). Draft acceptance totaled 18/75 and 9/60 tokens on the two prompts. This is a clear improvement over the original grouped K256 schedule, but remains slower than both references. The intervals are diagnostic for six runs on two fixed prompts. See the [paired summary](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_paired_multisession_summary_v1.json), [q_proj sweep](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_packed_byte_sweep_v2.json), and [NCU report](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k4_rows5_v1/qproj_packed_ncu_full.txt).

A K=2/R=3 packed-byte pilot was then evaluated under its own contract (`4a190f8ae92791462997c64f06fa2857e9abf5e6f96d2ed7fdeac433c212088f`). M=1…3 operator probes on both prompts matched logits and KV bitwise. Across three sessions per prompt, all 6 runs matched B1 and BNB token IDs and all 32 committed KV prefixes. Geometric-mean block latency was **1.090×** its K2 singleton (range 1.033–1.129×; diagnostic 95% paired log-t interval 1.053–1.129×) and **1.352×** BNB (range 1.290–1.391×; interval 1.312–1.392×). Draft acceptance was 15/48 on `cnndm_001` and 6/36 on `cnndm_002`. K2/R3 materially narrows the latency gap relative to K4/R5, but remains slower in all six paired runs. These are separate reference schedules, and the intervals are diagnostic on two fixed prompts. See the [K2 paired summary](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_k2_paired_multisession_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_002 operator probe](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k2_rows3_v1/operator_probe_cnndm002_m1_m3.json), and [cnndm_001 probe](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k2_rows3_v1/operator_probe_cnndm001_m1_m3.json).

A K=1/R=2 packed-byte run was measured next under its own singleton contract (`2dd35565577e8c0e2bc4f6b1dd6c4ef1e257dba15bcd488a8cfea0ecabb7ca67`). The M=1…2 probes matched logits/KV bitwise on both prompts. Three process sessions per prompt all matched B1 and original BNB token IDs, and all 32 committed KV prefixes matched; the [repeat audit](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_k1_reference_repeat_audit_v1.json) validates all six manifests against the contract. The [paired summary](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_k1_paired_multisession_summary_v1.json) reports geometric-mean block latency **1.018×** K1 singleton (range 0.944–1.065×; diagnostic paired log-t 95% interval 0.974–1.064×) and **1.247×** original BNB (range 1.143–1.336×; interval 1.176–1.324×). The B1 interval crosses 1.0, so the six measurements do not establish either a speedup or slowdown against singleton; this is the closest measured K setting to break-even, while BNB remains about 25% faster. Across these runs, 35.5 target rows were verified on average for 32 output tokens, with 0.4 draft acceptance. Cycle traces put target verification at about 0.91 s per 32-token decode; draft and commit work averaged about 1.7 ms and 3.2 ms, respectively. This supports profiling the target-call and block-verification path at K=1/R=2 before increasing K again; it does not meet the end-to-end speed target. The intervals are diagnostic on two fixed development prompts and one GPU.

A one-process-per-prompt n-gram screen at K=1/R=2 tested n=2 and n=4 against the same contract. All four runs matched B1 and BNB token IDs and all 32 committed KV prefixes. Acceptance was unchanged from n=3: 4/9 on `cnndm_001` and 2/6 on `cnndm_002`; single-run latency varied by prompt, so this does not select a proposer size. See the [screen artifact](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_k1_ngram_screen_v1.json).

To localize K1 cost, paired PyTorch CUDA traces were captured for block and singleton on `cnndm_001` with the same packed K256 schedule. Each decode target forward invokes 252 quantized linear modules. Block used 27 target forwards and 6,804 packed-kernel launches versus 31 forwards and 7,812 launches for singleton, but block verified 36 rows versus 31. The summed packed-kernel duration in the trace was 585.3 ms for block versus 518.3 ms for singleton (1.129×), while LM-head CUTLASS calls fell from 31 to 27 and their summed duration from 104.1 to 92.4 ms. This explains the tradeoff: fewer launches and LM-head calls are offset by extra rowwise NF4 work. The trace is a single profiler-instrumented run and its absolute timings are perturbed; use it to identify the operator target, not as a speed comparison. See the [profile comparison](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/profile_comparison_summary.json), [block profile](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/profile_block_ops/profile.json), and [singleton profile](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/profile_singleton_ops/profile.json).

#### Two-row packed NF4 reuse prototype

The trace motivated an opt-in `triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_k256` path. For a two-row linear input, one CTA decodes each packed weight tile once, then performs the existing per-row reduction independently for both input rows. M=1 continues to use the original packed K256 kernel, so the singleton path is not padded or slowed to manufacture a larger reference. On q_proj, the prototype was 1.191× the packed kernel at M=1, but 0.658× at M=2; its output was bitwise equal to the packed kernel at both M values. Whole-model probes on `cnndm_001` and `cnndm_002` were bitwise exact for logits/KV at M=1 and M=2.

The new K=1/R=2 schedule has a separate reference contract (`40df82dc76c21bd085eff4836559fe22348a3d5797d5c9193489ebe258c46660`). Three process sessions per prompt matched B1 and original BNB tokens and all 32 committed KV prefixes in every run; the [repeat audit](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_rows2_reference_repeat_audit_v1.json) passes. The [paired aggregate](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_rows2_k1_paired_multisession_summary_v1.json) gives block/singleton latency **0.941×** (range 0.904–0.990×; diagnostic paired-log 95% interval 0.908–0.975×), equivalent to a 1.063× geometric-mean speedup over B1 singleton. Block/BNB remains **1.145×** (interval 1.097–1.196×), so the method is still slower than original BNB. Draft acceptance remains 18/45 (40%), with 35.5 verified rows per 32 output tokens. This is the first measured K=1/R=2 improvement over B1 singleton with an interval excluding 1, but it is below the 1.15× design target and is only a two-prompt, one-GPU development result. The M=1/2 operator probes are [cnndm_001](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/operator_probe_rows2_v1.json) and [cnndm_002](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm002_k1_rows2_v1/operator_probe_rows2_v1.json); the q_proj comparison is [here](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/qproj_packed_rows2_screen_v1.json).

The same strategy was extended to the existing development prompt `cnndm_000`; its M=1/2 logits and KV also matched bitwise. Three additional process runs matched B1/BNB tokens and 32/32 KV prefixes. Across all three prompts and nine runs, the [aggregate](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_rows2_k1_three_prompt_summary_v1.json) gives block/singleton latency **0.894×** (geometric-mean speedup 1.119×; range 0.789–0.990×; diagnostic paired-log 95% interval 0.838–0.953×) and block/BNB **1.093×** (range 0.960–1.202×; interval 1.028–1.163×). Acceptance was 18/45 on `cnndm_001/002` together and 24/36 on `cnndm_000`, for 42/81 overall (51.9%). The speedup versus B1 repeats on this third prompt, but the 1.119× mean is still below the 1.15× design target; BNB is near parity on `cnndm_000` and faster on the other two prompts. The intervals are descriptive diagnostics across three fixed prompts on one GPU, not a generalization claim. The [three-prompt repeat audit](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_rows2_reference_repeat_three_prompt_v1.json) validates all nine runs against the same contract.

#### N=16 packed two-row schedule

The q_proj tile sweep showed that N=16 changes the N=8 arithmetic (M=2 max difference about `1.9e-6`) but remains bitwise shape invariant when each row uses the N=16 singleton schedule. It therefore has a separate contract, `ac30b8a2be89e329102ad7ae6a29e383ca8f2d43224fdf759bdc079e95b50d32`; N=32 was not selected because it changed outputs more without a meaningful timing gain. q_proj N=16/N=8 median latency ratios were 0.963 at M=1 and 0.512 at M=2. Whole-model M=1/2 probes on all three development prompts matched logits and KV bitwise.

A 15-case synthetic stress varied K=64…1024, output tails around N=16/32, nested/non-nested NF4 scales, and FP16 bias. Both rows of each M=2 result matched their own M=1 singleton bitwise in all 15 cases; repeated-row results also matched. All 15 singleton outputs were bitwise equal to BNB dequantization followed by FP16 `F.linear`. This finite matrix covers aligned K values and tested tails only; it is not a universal kernel proof. See the [stress artifact](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_k256_synthetic_stress_v1.json) and [harness](../../scripts/stress_nf4_packed_rows2_n16.py).

Across three sessions on each prompt, all nine N=16 runs matched B1 and original BNB tokens and all 32 committed KV prefixes. The [aggregate](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_k1_three_prompt_summary_v1.json) reports block/singleton latency **0.882×** (geometric-mean speedup 1.134×; range 0.792–0.981×; diagnostic paired-log 95% interval 0.831–0.936×) and block/BNB **1.082×** (range 0.975–1.215×; interval 1.015–1.154×). Acceptance remained 42/81 (51.9%). Compared with matched N=8 prompt/session slots, N=16 block latency was only 0.989× on geometric mean, so this tile did not materially change end-to-end performance even though the q_proj result improved. In a single profiler trace, packed-kernel duration fell 35% (585.3→382.3 ms) and summed CUDA kernel duration fell 21.5%; the profiler-instrumented total was noisy and must not be treated as a speed comparison. This keeps N=16 as an experimental alternative, below the 1.15× B1 target and still slower than BNB on average. See the [repeat audit](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_reference_repeat_three_prompt_v1.json), [N8/N16 full-model comparison](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_vs_n8_three_prompt_comparison_v1.json), [profiler comparison](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_qwen3b_cnndm001_profile_comparison_v1.json), and [tile sweep](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_qwen3b_cnndm001_k1_rows2_v1/qproj_rows2_tile_sweep_v2.json).

A one-launch Nsight Compute basic profile of the N=16/K=256 M=2 kernel found q_proj at 27.07 μs with 64 registers/thread, 66.7% theoretical and 48.1% achieved occupancy; down_proj took 131.04 μs with 72 registers/thread, 58.3% theoretical and 46.1% achieved occupancy. Registers limit theoretical occupancy in both cases. Compute and DRAM throughput were both material (q_proj 61.2%/42.3%; down_proj 67.3%/60.3% of peak), so the next kernel experiment should reduce live register/accumulator pressure without changing each row's reduction order, then remeasure end-to-end. A separate local-memory counter run observed zero local-memory sectors for these two launches, giving no sign of runtime spill traffic for these shapes; this does not cover other shapes or compiler configurations. These are profiler launches, not comparable latency samples. See the [NCU summary and reports](ncu_nf4_rows2_n16_v1/summary.json).

A K-tile screening compared N=16/K=128 with K=256 at M=1/2 for q_proj and MLP down_proj. Each schedule was bitwise equal to its own singleton calls at both row counts. K=128 reduced registers/thread from 64→48 (q_proj) and 72→56 (down_proj), raising theoretical occupancy by 16.7 percentage points, while achieved occupancy improved only 2.3 and 3.4 points. At M=2, CUDA-event medians for K=128 were **1.230×** K=256 on q_proj and **1.356×** on down_proj. M=1 down_proj was 1.333× slower; q_proj M=1 was noisy and near parity. NCU single-launch durations were also longer for K=128. This candidate is not integrated: it is slower at the speculative M=2 shape and lacks whole-model validation under its separate arithmetic schedule. See the [operator benchmark](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_k128_exploration_v1/operator_benchmark.json), [screen summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_k128_exploration_v1/summary.json), and [NCU reports](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_k128_exploration_v1/).

The separate N=16/K=256 one-warp schedule (`triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_rows2_n16_warp1_k256`) is bitwise row-invariant for M=1/2 in the two tested projections and passed the whole-model operator probe. On three development prompts with three process sessions each, all nine 32-token pilots matched B1 and original BNB token IDs; all 288 shared committed KV prefixes matched. Acceptance was 42/81. The block/singleton geometric-mean speedup was **1.142×** (diagnostic paired-log 95% interval 1.077–1.211×), just below the 1.15× design target; block/original-BNB latency ratio was 1/0.916, so it remained slower than BNB on average. On six matched prompt/session slots versus the four-warp N=16 schedule, one-warp block latency was 0.923× and singleton latency 0.942×. NCU reported 166–168 registers/thread and only 11.9–12.1% achieved occupancy, with zero local-memory sectors in both profiled launches. Keep this as a separately contracted opt-in experiment; do not call it exact to warp4 or BNB from the finite pilots. See the [pilot summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/pilot_summary.json), [schedule summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/summary.json), and [contract](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp1_k256_qwen3b_cnndm000_k1_rows2_session0_v1/reference_contract.json).

A follow-up K-tile screen specific to the one-warp schedule compared K=64/128/256. Every tile passed M=1/2 singleton equality on q_proj and down_proj. K=128 was within 1% of K=256 for q_proj at M=2, but 5.2% slower for down_proj; K=64 was 0.6% slower on q_proj and 13.6% slower on down_proj at M=2. Keep K=256. This finite operator-only screen does not justify a new reference contract or whole-model run. See the [summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp1_k256_k_tile_sweep_v1/summary.json) and [raw benchmark](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp1_k256_k_tile_sweep_v1/operator_benchmark.json).

A fresh contract-v5 Nsight Compute capture measured the actual hooked one-warp kernel after normal BNB prefill initialized the linear biases. For M=2/N=16/K=256, q_proj ([report](ncu_nf4_rows2_n16_warp1_v1/q_proj.ncu-rep)) took 28.256 μs with 168 registers/thread; down_proj ([report](ncu_nf4_rows2_n16_warp1_v1/down_proj.ncu-rep)) took 139.392 μs with 167 registers/thread (168 allocated). Both launches had 128 CTAs, one warp per CTA, and about 12% achieved warp occupancy; SM throughput was 46.8% and 50.7% of peak, while L1/TEX throughput was 35.5% and 36.9%. The q_proj/down_proj timings are close to the earlier four-warp kernel captures (27.07/131.04 μs) despite the one-warp schedule's much higher register count and lower occupancy; those are separate profiler captures, not a controlled A/B. This confirms register pressure as a scheduling constraint but does not by itself identify a safe faster mapping. Next experiment should target the down_proj mapping only and compare same-process operator latency plus exact M=1/2 row equality before any model-level change. The capture used NCU's basic set, so it does not provide a new spill or DRAM-throughput measurement. See the [capture metadata and summary](ncu_nf4_rows2_n16_warp1_v1/summary.json).

A down_proj-only N=4 output-tile screen compared N=4/8/16 at one warp, with N=16/four-warps as a control. Every schedule preserved its own M=1/2 singleton rows. N=4 did not match the current N=16/one-warp output on the tested input, so it would need a separate reference contract; it was also slower, with M=2 median latency 0.1295 ms versus 0.1092 ms for N=16 (1.186×, seven alternating rounds). N=8 was also slower at M=2 (1.153×). Reject these smaller output tiles for down_proj. The finite operator result does not justify a model-level run. See the [benchmark artifact](triton_nf4_downproj_n4_warp1_v1/operator_benchmark.json) and [screen harness](../../scripts/benchmark_nf4_downproj_n4_warp1.py).

An isolated copy of the packed-row kernel tested ordered K-loop unroll factors 1/2/4/8 for down_proj. All variants matched their own M=1/2 singleton outputs; factors 2/4/8 were also bitwise equal to factor 1 on the tested inputs. Higher-repeat alternating A/B of factor 2 against the current N=16/one-warp reference showed no gain: the paired geometric reference/unroll latency ratio was 0.944 at M=1 and 0.981 at M=2 (ratios below 1 mean the current reference was faster). The run had substantial round-to-round noise, but both the initial screen and confirmation reject unrolling as a useful tuning direction. Do not integrate or assign a new contract. See the [decision summary](triton_nf4_downproj_unroll_v1/summary.json), [initial screen](triton_nf4_downproj_unroll_v1/operator_benchmark.json), [paired confirmation](triton_nf4_downproj_unroll_v1/paired_confirm.json), and [isolated kernel](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_fixed_rows_group_scale_packed_rows_unroll.py).

An experimental two-pass split-K schedule partitions each 256-feature K tile range into 8 fixed chunks and reduces their FP32 partials in a second kernel. On layer-0 down_proj, split-8 cut operator latency about 21% at M=2; on q_proj it was about 67% slower, so the adapter applies split-8 only to the pinned 11008→2048 down_proj shape and retains the warp1 schedule for the other Qwen2.5-3B linear shapes. All 36 down_proj layers passed M=1/2 singleton equality. Three development prompts × three process sessions then matched B1 tokens, original BNB tokens, and all 288 shared KV prefixes. Whole-model geomean speedup was 1.126× versus B1 (diagnostic paired-log 95% interval 1.063–1.192×) and 0.888× versus original BNB (0.832–0.948×); both are below the previous warp1-only aggregate (1.142× and 0.916×, respectively; cross-run comparison). A direct same-process paired block A/B across 3 prompts × 3 alternating-order repeats confirmed split-8 was **3.8% slower** than warp1 (geometric ratio 1.038; prompt-level diagnostic interval 1.018–1.058), with equal output tokens in every pair. Keep this as an opt-in B1 prototype for its fixed-order split reduction, but reject it as the current performance choice. See the [experiment summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/summary.json), [paired A/B](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/paired_warp1_vs_split8.json), [all-layer shape audit](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/down_layers_shape_audit.json), [operator benchmarks](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/), and [pilot sessions](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/).

Follow-up split-K screens found no cheap rescue: reusing the FP32 partial buffer changed down_proj M=2 latency by less than 0.2%, and increasing split8 from one warp to two/four warps made down_proj M=2 5.2%/15.6% slower. All variants retained singleton equality. Stop tuning this branch; a worthwhile follow-up needs a different one-launch or row/reduction mapping. See [workspace benchmark](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/operator_workspace_benchmark.json) and [warp sweep](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_splitk_v1/operator_warp_sweep.json).

A fresh BNB fixed-row K=2/R=3 run measured three CNN/DailyMail development prompts in three sessions at 64 and 256 output tokens. All 18 blocks matched their own B1 singleton and all 2,880 shared KV prefixes matched. At 64 tokens, every run also matched original BNB, while BNB-relative geomean latency was 0.908×. At 256 tokens, no run matched BNB tokens; B1 speedup was 1.397× (prompt-level diagnostic 95% interval 1.301–1.499×), and the BNB-relative estimate was 1.024× with an interval spanning 1 (prompt-level 0.951–1.102×). This makes K2/R3 a promising long-generation B1 schedule, with B2 divergence and uncertain original-BNB speedup stated separately. See the [budget summary](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/summary.json), [64-token sessions](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_v1/), and [256-token sessions](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/).
