A forced-prefix replay compared original BNB singleton with the padded fixed-row singleton on the saved BNB token paths. Across the three prompts, all three source sessions had identical BNB token paths. Prompt-prefill logits and active KV hashes were bitwise equal. After consuming the first generated token, logits already differed (max absolute difference 0.25–0.28125) and the active KV hashes differed, even though greedy argmax stayed equal until generated-token indices 91, 93, and 99, respectively. Thus delayed token divergence hides an early state/numerical divergence; this replay localizes the onset to the first decode transition, but does not yet identify the first differing layer/operator. See the [replay trace](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/bnb_logit_kv_replay_v1.json) and [replay script](../../scripts/audit_b1_bnb_replay_drift.py). Next G1 task: capture per-layer/operator outputs for that first decode transition under the pinned runtime before changing the kernel schedule. This remains finite B2 diagnostic evidence, not a proof.

The operator trace located that first padded-schedule mismatch in Qwen2 layer-0 SDPA: BNB singleton used Q `[1,16,1,128]`, KV `[1,2,prefix+1,128]`, no mask and GQA enabled; padded fixed-row used Q `[1,16,3,128]`, expanded KV heads and padded key rows with a mask. Comparing the real query row showed an SDPA difference of 0.00024–0.00098. This motivated an opt-in `bnb_rowwise` schedule that calls SDPA per real row with the pinned singleton GQA layout, and computes Qwen RMSNorm and `lm_head` per row at M=1. The first prototype still drifted after 62 tokens at `layers.33.post_attention_layernorm`; changing RMSNorm to M=1 removed that divergence through the full 256-token audit. Relevant traces: [padded operator trace](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/first_decode_module_trace_v4.json), [RMSNorm drift trace](qwen3b_bnb_fixedrows_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/first_drift_module_bnbrowwise_prefix61_v1.json), and [final replay](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/bnb_singleton_state_replay_v1.json).

Under the new [contract](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/reference_contract.json), K=2/R=3 `bnb_rowwise` block decoding ran 3 CNN/DailyMail development prompts in two process sessions at 256 tokens. All 6/6 runs matched both the B1 singleton and original BNB token IDs; all 1,536 shared B1 KV prefixes matched. A forced BNB-singleton replay found no logit or active-KV drift through 256 tokens on all three prompts. The paired geometric-mean speedup was **1.227× vs B1 singleton** (prompt-level bootstrap interval 1.161–1.305) and **0.944× vs original BNB** (0.886–1.010). This is finite, same-GPU evidence for a B2-compatible schedule, not a proof or a settled speedup over BNB. The block path meets the 1.15× B1 design target on this small cohort, while original BNB remains faster on average. See the [two-session summary](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_multisession_summary_v1.json), [session 0](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_v1/), and [contract-enforced session 1](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_session1_v1/).

Across those six runs, the n-gram proposer accepted 444/862 drafted tokens (51.5%) and the verifier executed 1,952 target rows. A one-prompt, 24-token profiler trace (instrumented; not a latency benchmark) attributed the largest CUDA entries to BNB 4-bit GEMM (`gemm_4bit_simt`, 250.6 ms) and per-row vocabulary projection (`aten::mm`, 90.9 ms for 27 calls). The next optimization target is quantized GEMM and LM-head cost, while preserving the rowwise singleton contract. The [profile artifact](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm001_profile_v1/profile.json) is included in the [multisession summary](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k2_r3_cnndm3_256_multisession_summary_v1.json).

A one-session K=1/R=2 screen also matched B1 and BNB tokens 3/3, all 768 block/singleton KV prefixes, and the forced BNB replay through 256 tokens. Its geomean was 1.211× vs B1 and 0.948× vs BNB, close to but not better than the repeated K2/R3 result; keep K2/R3 as the current candidate and repeat K1 only if later profiling suggests the smaller row shape can reduce 4-bit GEMM cost. See the [K1/R2 screen](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k1_r2_cnndm3_256_v1/summary_screen_v1.json).

A one-session K=4/R=5 screen remained exact to B1 (3/3 tokens, 768 KV prefixes) but matched original BNB 0/3 and was only 0.818× BNB-relative. Forced singleton replay with physical_rows=5 already diverged on its first decode transition; the first differing module was `model.layers.0.self_attn.q_proj`. Thus BNB compatibility is currently established only for the R=3 prototype, and increasing physical rows changes the BNB quantized-linear schedule before block verification begins. Reject K4/R5 for the B2 candidate until that linear behavior is reproduced at M=1. See the [K4/R5 screen](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k4_r5_cnndm3_256_v1/summary_screen_v1.json), [singleton replay](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k4_r5_cnndm3_256_v1/bnb_singleton_state_replay_v1.json), and [first-module trace](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_k4_r5_cnndm3_256_v1/first_decode_operator_trace_v1.json).

A paired K=2/R=3 seed on `cnndm_000` matched B1/BNB tokens and all 32 shared KV prefixes, but did not improve on K=1/R=2: acceptance fraction fell from 8/12 (66.7%) to 11/18 (61.1%), and speedup moved from 1.203× to 1.194× versus B1 and 0.953× to 0.940× versus BNB. This single seed is only a screen, but it does not justify expanding K=2. See the [paired seed comparison](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/k1_vs_k2_cnndm000_seed.json) and the [K=2 contract](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp1_k256_qwen3b_cnndm000_k2_rows3_session0_v1/reference_contract.json).

A profiler trace on `cnndm_001` recorded 6,804 custom packed-kernel launches for both warp counts. Warp=1 reduced the profiled custom-kernel self time 4.3% (382.3→365.8 ms), verify time 6.7% (1266.5→1182.0 ms), and profiler total 6.1% (1452.4→1364.4 ms); treat these as diagnostic trace deltas, not benchmark latency. The LM-head/projection `aten::mm` self time changed only 1.6%, and BNB `gemm_4bit` time was essentially unchanged. An N=8/16/32 screen for warp=1 kept N=16: N=8 gave no consistent gain across q_proj/down_proj, and N=32 was 1.49×/1.87× slower at M=2. See the [profiler comparison](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/profile_comparison_cnndm001.json) and [tile summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/output_tile_summary.json).

#### Triton SDPA B1 closed-loop screen

The Triton SDPA prototype was run through the actual B1 singleton and K=2/R=3 block state machines on development prompt `cnndm_000`, for 64 generated tokens. With the same fixed-row schedule, token IDs matched and all 64 committed KV prefixes had identical hashes. In three uninstrumented paired timing rounds, the block was 1.159×–1.189× faster than fixed-row singleton (geometric mean **1.179×**) and 0.861×–0.871× the speed of original BNB greedy (geometric mean **0.864×**, so still slower). The block also matched original BNB's 64 token IDs in all three rounds; BNB KV identity was not established by this experiment. This is one prompt and three timings, without a confidence interval.

The state-audit callbacks hash/cache-copy active KV and are deliberately excluded from the paired timing pass. The initial run's instrumented wall times suggested 3.64× and are invalid for performance comparison. The separate timing pass includes prefill, drafting, verification and commit. The attention adapter is still `bnb_rowwise`, so the Triton SDPA call runs once per query row; this experiment validates the closed-loop schedule and its speed contribution from the rest of block verification, but it does **not** yet demonstrate parallel attention rows. The custom singleton's KV also remains distinct from original BNB per the short transition audits above, so this is B1 evidence only. Runner: [closed-loop audit](../../scripts/audit_triton_b1_closed_loop.py); exactness and paired timings: [session 1 artifact](triton_b1_closed_loop_cnndm000_k2_r3_s1_v1.json). The earlier instrumented screen is retained as an audit artifact, not timing evidence: [session 0](triton_b1_closed_loop_cnndm000_k2_r3_s0_v1.json).

The same fixed-row B1 schedule was extended to 128 tokens on `cnndm_001`. It matched the singleton at all 128 KV prefixes. One uninstrumented timing round measured 1.159× speedup over B1 singleton and 0.843× versus original BNB; treat both as single-sample screens. The block matched BNB token IDs through generated index 27, then first diverged at index 28. This confirms that short BNB token agreement does not make the custom Triton schedule B2 exact. Artifact: [cnndm_001, 128 tokens](triton_b1_closed_loop_cnndm001_k2_r3_128_s2_v1.json).

#### Triton parallel query-row attention

The first batched query/head kernel path is wired through the Qwen adapter as `attention_strategy="triton_batched"`. It sends all real query rows through one SDPA call and Triton launch, with each CTA deriving its causal prefix from the active K/V length and query-row index. Its initial wrapper kept RMSNorm and `lm_head` rowwise; it matched B1 tokens and KV prefixes at 64 tokens on `cnndm_000` and 128 tokens on `cnndm_001`, but had poor end-to-end latency. The later fixed-M changes below supersede its timing. See the [initial 64-token artifact](triton_b1_parallel_attention_cnndm000_k2_r3_64_s7_v1.json), [initial 128-token artifact](triton_b1_parallel_attention_cnndm001_k2_r3_128_s8_v1.json), and [Qwen adapter](../../src/zassd/certification/qwen2_padded.py).

Each run has only one uninstrumented timing round. Block/singleton ratios were 1.095× and 1.174×; block/original-BNB ratios were 0.549× and 0.665×. So batched attention passes the current B1 state check but remains substantially slower end-to-end than BNB, and it has not shown a clear gain over the preceding serial-row Triton schedule. The fixed-row wrapper/prefill and quantized GEMM/LM-head costs now dominate the path. Treat the timing as a negative feasibility screen, not a performance result. The intermediate `triton_padded` branch (full dummy Q rows plus a checked causal mask) was also exact for 64/128 prefixes but had worse BNB-relative timing; keep it only as a comparison artifact.

The B1/B2 split remains unchanged: the custom Triton singleton can diverge from original BNB tokens at index 28 on `cnndm_001`, and its KV already differed in the short transition audit. There is no B2 state-equivalence claim for either Triton attention strategy.

#### Fixed-row RMSNorm and vocabulary projection

A 16-token operator profile identified the vocabulary projection as the dominant avoidable cost: rowwise `lm_head` made 416 `aten::mm` calls and used 1.476 s of profiled GPU time. The `triton_batched` B1 schedule now pads singleton `lm_head` inputs to M=3 and projects the block once at M=3, then crops unused singleton rows. RMSNorm also runs with M=3 in both singleton and block paths. This removes per-row Python/GPU launches while defining the same fixed-row B1 reference for each path. In the follow-up 16-token diagnostic profile, the block used 10 M=3 vocab projections at 34 ms total; its Triton attention calls summed to about 8 ms. The profile is diagnostic, not a benchmark. The remaining profile cost is dominated by NF4 linear GEMMs: `gemm_4bit_simt` was about 148 ms and BNB 4-bit GEMM entries were about 84 ms in that trace. See the [pre-change profile](triton_b1_parallel_attention_profile_cnndm001_v1/profile.json) and [post-change profile](triton_b1_parallel_attention_lmhead_fixed_profile_cnndm001_v1/profile.json).

The updated schedule retained B1 token and all-prefix KV identity at 64 tokens on `cnndm_000` and 128 tokens on `cnndm_001` and `cnndm_002`. Three uninstrumented paired rounds per prompt/budget gave geometric-mean block/B1 ratios of 1.379×, 1.250×, and 1.215×; block/original-BNB ratios were 1.035×, 0.942×, and 0.897×. Across all nine paired rounds, the descriptive geometric means are **1.280× vs B1** (diagnostic paired-log-t 95% interval 1.219–1.344) and **0.957× vs BNB** (0.908–1.008). B1's interval excludes parity; the BNB interval includes parity. These intervals summarize three fixed development prompts on one GPU and do not support broader generalization. Original-BNB token IDs matched all three 64-token `cnndm_000` runs, then diverged at generated index 28 for all `cnndm_001` runs and index 99 for all `cnndm_002` runs. A 256-token `cnndm_001` audit matched all B1 tokens/KV prefixes in one session; its single block/BNB ratio was 0.965×. BNB KV identity remains unproven and contradicted by the earlier layer-1 mismatch. Artifacts: [cnndm_000 64 tokens, 3 paired rounds](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_cnndm000_k2_r3_64_s16_v1.json), [cnndm_001 128 tokens, 3 paired rounds](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_cnndm001_k2_r3_128_s17_v1.json), [cnndm_002 128 tokens, 3 paired rounds and logical-length check](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_cnndm002_k2_r3_128_s22_v1.json), and [cnndm_001 256 tokens](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_cnndm001_k2_r3_256_s18_v1.json).

The existing packed NF4 linear kernel was screened under this updated schedule. B1 tokens and all 64 KV prefixes still matched, but block/singleton was only 0.980× and block/BNB 0.834× in the one-round screen. Do not use that linear strategy with this attention schedule without a better kernel; it regressed end-to-end latency. [Screen artifact](triton_b1_parallel_attention_packedlinear_cnndm000_k2_r3_64_s13_v1.json).

A separate N=16/one-warp NF4 linear schedule was repeated at R=3 on three development prompts. It matched all B1 tokens, KV prefixes, and shared-frontier logical lengths at 64 tokens on `cnndm_000` and 128 tokens on `cnndm_001/002`. Across nine paired rounds, block/B1 was **1.199×** (diagnostic paired-log-t 95% interval 1.141–1.260); block/original-BNB was **1.018×** (0.959–1.080). The BNB interval crosses parity. BNB tokens matched all three `cnndm_000` runs, then diverged at index 45 on `cnndm_001` and index 75 on `cnndm_002`. This is a distinct arithmetic schedule from `fixed_rows`; it improves BNB-relative latency on the three-prompt screen while reducing the B1 speedup, so keep it as an alternate and do not select it as the default yet. Artifacts: [cnndm_000](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm000_k2_r3_64_s23_v1.json), [cnndm_001](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm001_k2_r3_128_s24_v1.json), [cnndm_002](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k2_r3_128_s25_v1.json).

An independent fourth process session repeated the 128-token `cnndm_002` case. B1 tokens, all 128 KV prefixes, and shared-frontier logical lengths matched. Its three paired rounds measured 1.067× block/B1 and 0.884× block/BNB; BNB first diverged at generated index 75 again. Both `cnndm_002` sessions accepted 19/63 draft tokens (30.2%), compared with 41/65 (63.1%) on `cnndm_001`; their block verification took 3.47–3.67 s versus 2.83–3.00 s on `cnndm_001`. This points to low acceptance plus greater verification work as a likely contributor to the prompt-level regression, though the runs do not isolate causality. This repeat confirms exactness for the declared B1 schedule on this run, while showing that the slower-than-BNB result on this prompt is repeatable. It is not included in the original nine-round aggregate above. [Independent session 26](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k2_r3_128_s26_v1.json).

To test that acceptance lead, the closed-loop runner now exposes `--ngram-size` (default 3) and records it in each manifest. On `cnndm_002`, three paired rounds each for n=2 and n=4 both retained exact B1 tokens/KV/logical lengths. n=2 accepted 54/195 drafted tokens (27.7%) and measured 1.053× B1 / 0.879× BNB; n=4 accepted 57/189 (30.2%) and measured 1.063× B1 / 0.878× BNB. The n=3 sessions 25/26 accepted the same 57/189 and measured B1 ratios 1.106×/1.067× and BNB ratios 0.926×/0.884×. On this prompt, neither alternate n-gram improved acceptance; n=4 was close to n=3 acceptance but remained slower than BNB. Retain n=3 and do not broaden this small proposer screen into a general policy claim. Runner change: [closed-loop audit](../../scripts/audit_triton_b1_closed_loop.py). Artifacts: [n=2](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k2_r3_128_n2_s27_v1.json), [n=4](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k2_r3_128_n4_s28_v1.json).

K=1/R=2 was screened with the same fixed-M RMSNorm/LM-head and batched attention schedule on `cnndm_000`. It matched all 64 B1 KV prefixes and logical lengths, with three-round geometric speed ratios of 1.302× vs B1 and 0.962× vs BNB. K=2/R=3 remains the stronger current setting on this screen. [K1/R2 artifact](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_cnndm000_k1_r2_64_s21_v1.json).

The audit runner now compares the cache's scalar sequence length and every layer's active length at shared commit frontiers in addition to hashing K/V prefixes. On `cnndm_000`, the updated `triton_batched` schedule matched token IDs, all 64 KV prefix hashes, and logical lengths at every shared frontier. This closes the metadata-length check for this one 64-token B1 run; it does not prove BNB state identity or cover every decoder terminal/rejection branch. [Full-state frontier artifact](triton_b1_parallel_attention_fullstate_cnndm000_k2_r3_64_s20_v1.json); [runner](../../scripts/audit_triton_b1_closed_loop.py).

Next: keep n=3 and shift effort back to the target verification path, where `cnndm_002` spends more time verifying despite the same accepted-token count. Profile fixed-row NF4/attention and cycle overhead on this prompt, then choose one kernel-level change for a controlled paired A/B. Keep BNB token/state comparisons separate. GSM8K 300–349 remains sealed.

#### `cnndm_002` operator profile

Separate 32-token PyTorch profiler captures were made for B1 singleton and K=2/R=3 block with the N=16/one-warp NF4 strategy and `triton_batched` attention. Both accepted 7/15 draft tokens. Instrumented verify time was 1.488 s singleton and 1.331 s block; diagnostic end-to-end was 1.696/1.540 s, not comparable to the uninstrumented paired runs. In the aggregated operator events, `_packed_two_rows_kernel` accounted for 461/450 ms self CUDA time (7,812/6,300 calls); the M=3 vocabulary `aten::mm` accounted for 108/88 ms (31/25 calls), and fixed-row attention for 20.3/20.1 ms (1,116/900 calls). The kernel call count falls with block, but its summed self time remains the dominant profiled component. The profiler emitted its event-clear warning, so treat event totals as diagnostic and confirm any optimization with the paired end-to-end runner. Traces: [singleton](triton_b1_profile_cnndm002_n16_singleton_v1/profile.json), [block](triton_b1_profile_cnndm002_n16_block_v1/profile.json).

Current optimization target: reduce packed NF4 linear cost for verification rows while preserving the N=16/one-warp singleton arithmetic schedule. Attention is no longer the leading cost in this trace; avoid changing its reduction schedule until a separate measurement identifies a benefit.

#### K=1/R=2 screen with the N=16/one-warp schedule

The same linear/attention arithmetic was screened with `physical_rows=2`, K=1 and n=3 on the three development prompts, with two independent process sessions per prompt. All six runs matched their own B1 singleton token sequence, all 576 generated KV prefixes, and shared-frontier logical lengths. Across 18 paired timing rounds, descriptive geomean speedup was **1.229× vs B1 singleton**; block/original-BNB was **1.014×**, so aggregate BNB parity was not reached. Prompt-level block/B1 and block/BNB geomeans were: `cnndm_000` 1.250/1.028 and 1.224/1.032; `cnndm_001` 1.330/1.081 and 1.318/1.093; `cnndm_002` 1.171/0.960 and 1.099/0.904. Acceptance was stable within prompt: 14/22, 32/42, and 16/35, respectively. Original BNB matched tokens on `cnndm_000`; divergence was at index 45 on `cnndm_001` and 75 on `cnndm_002`. This is a promising B1 development candidate that clears the 1.15× B1 speed target in these samples, but it does not show a stable BNB-relative speedup and is a distinct R=2 reference schedule from R=3. Do not use these finite prompt results as generalization evidence or claim B2 state equality. Artifacts: [cnndm_000 sessions 32/33](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm000_k1_r2_64_s32_v1.json), [session 33](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm000_k1_r2_64_s33_v1.json); [cnndm_001 sessions 31/34](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm001_k1_r2_128_s31_v1.json), [session 34](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm001_k1_r2_128_s34_v1.json); [cnndm_002 sessions 29/30](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k1_r2_128_s29_v1.json), [session 30](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k1_r2_128_s30_v1.json).

At a 256-token budget, K=1/R=2 was repeated twice per prompt across the same three development prompts. All six runs matched B1 tokens, all 1,536 active KV prefixes, and shared-frontier logical lengths. Across 18 paired timing rounds, descriptive geomeans were **1.291× vs B1** and **1.067× vs BNB**. Per-prompt session geomeans were `cnndm_000` 1.413×/1.430× vs B1 and 1.141×/1.167× vs BNB; `cnndm_001` 1.250×/1.207× and 1.053×/1.010×; `cnndm_002` 1.241×/1.223× and 1.008×/1.033×. Acceptance counts were stable within prompt: 74/102, 51/78, and 51/81. BNB tokens diverged at indices 153, 45, and 75, respectively; no BNB state identity claim is made. All three prompt-level BNB ratios exceeded parity in both sessions, a positive development-only result for this R=2 reference. [cnndm_000 sessions 41/42](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm000_k1_r2_256_s41_v1.json), [cnndm_001 sessions 39/40](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm001_k1_r2_256_s39_v1.json), [cnndm_002 sessions 35/36](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k1_r2_256_s35_v1.json).

At 512 tokens on `cnndm_002`, two further sessions matched all 512 B1 KV prefixes and logical lengths. Six paired rounds gave 1.201× vs B1 and 1.009× vs BNB; each run drafted 154 candidates and accepted 88. BNB ratios ranged 0.972–1.057, so this is parity-level evidence on one prompt. [Sessions 37/38](triton_b1_parallel_attention_norm_fixed_warp1n16_cnndm002_k1_r2_512_s37_v1.json).

A 32-token singleton/block profile was captured using the selected R=2/K=1 schedule. The block used 25 target forwards and 33 target rows versus 31 singleton forwards/rows; each accepted 6/8 candidates. The diagnostic `verify_ms` was 1.490 s singleton and 1.176 s block. Summed packed NF4 self CUDA time fell from 461.2 ms (7,812 calls) to 371.6 ms (6,300 calls); M=2 vocabulary projection fell 107.6→86.6 ms, and attention 20.3→18.1 ms. This supports the R=2 cost reduction seen in end-to-end timing, while packed NF4 remains the largest operator component. Profiler emitted the event-clear warning; these totals are diagnostics, not latency benchmarks. See [singleton profile](triton_b1_profile_cnndm002_n16_r2_singleton_v1/profile.json) and [block profile](triton_b1_profile_cnndm002_n16_r2_block_v1/profile.json).

The R=2 schedule now has a finite composite identity contract, ID `0295c5c92f6e6e6269b2ab3607635db1ab0fd33192351552e9f2232d23fbfc92`, built from the current model-state hash, runtime and source hashes, plus eight full-state validation artifacts. Natural cycle traces across those eight runs covered 2,026 cycles: 528 accepted draft tokens, 302 rejects at the only K=1 rejection position (index 0), 1,196 cycles with no proposal, and all eight output-budget endings. Two runs ended the budget inside an accepted candidate cycle; six ended through the final pending-token path. No EOS terminal or EOS draft candidate occurred, so EOS acceptance/rejection remains an uncovered contract boundary. The contract is an experimental identity record; it is not wired into runtime fail-closed checks and does not establish B2.

The existing `tests/test_b1_exact.py` EOS fixtures exercise the legacy singleton generator, not the Triton fixed-row block with `physical_rows=2`; I did not run tests. The generic `require_b1_fixed_row_capability` still gates architecture/attention only. New contract-bound R=2 entry points provide a stricter experimental path: live verification hashes the model state and runtime, checks the v4 contract's pinned sources, and returns a handle tied to that model/tokenizer instance. The entry points accept that handle and fix K=1, n-gram 3, rows=2, Triton batched attention, and the N=16/one-warp NF4 linear strategy.

The first live-gated run on development prompt `cnndm_000` (64 output tokens) passed identity preflight and matched B1 tokens, all 64 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Its single timing pair was 1.301x vs B1 singleton and 1.097x vs original BNB; treat this only as a launcher smoke measurement. See [preflight](triton_b1_contract_live_preflight_v1.json), [gated run](triton_b1_contract_gated_cnndm000_64_s2_v1.json), and [launcher](../../scripts/run_triton_b1_contract_audit.py).

The R=2 EOS branch audit covers both outcomes through the contract-bound API and v4 live preflight. The initial pair matched B1 tokens, active KV prefixes (2/2 and 3/3), and logical lengths. A four-prompt stress cohort then exercised two accepted-EOS and two rejected-EOS candidates; all four runs matched token sequences, KV prefixes, and logical lengths. See the [v4 API audit](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_eos_audit_v2.json), [four-prompt stress report](triton_b1_r2_eos_branch_stress_v1.json), and [stress prompt cohort](b1_eos_branch_stress_prompts_v1.jsonl). The v4 contract pins the [decoder API](../../src/zassd/decoding/b1_triton_contract.py), [live verifier](../../src/zassd/certification/triton_b1_reference_contract.py), and [audit runner](../../scripts/audit_triton_b1_eos_branches.py). This is still a small constructed development cohort, not an all-input guarantee.

The generic fixed-row API still supports unconstrained research experiments; use the new contract-bound entry points whenever results are reported against the R=2 contract. Keep this schedule experimental.

The v5 contract-bound paired runner was measured on `cnndm_000/001/002`, two rounds per prompt. At 128 tokens, B1 token/KV prefixes matched in all 3 prompts (91/91, 97/97, 113/113); block geomean speedup was **1.276× vs B1 singleton** (descriptive paired log-t 95% interval 1.159–1.405) and **1.030× vs original BNB** (0.915–1.160). At 256 tokens, B1 exactness again held on all prefixes (183/183, 205/205, 205/205); geomeans were **1.301× vs B1** (1.063–1.592) and **1.137× vs BNB** (1.033–1.252). The 256-token acceptance counts were 74/102, 51/78 and 51/81. Only 2/6 128-token comparisons and 0/6 256-token comparisons matched original BNB tokens; divergences at 256 were indices 153/45/75. The intervals are descriptive over three fixed prompts on one GPU, not prompt-level uncertainty estimates; BNB latency comparison does not imply output fidelity. See the [128-token run](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm3_128_v1.json), [256-token run](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm3_256_v1.json), and [v5 contract](b1_triton_batched_k1r2_n16_reference_contract_v5.json). This is an encouraging development result against B1 and a preliminary BNB speed signal at 256 tokens, but it needs more prompts and independent sessions before treating it as stable.

That repeat is complete on three additional development prompts (`cnndm_003/004/005`), in two separate processes with one timing pair per prompt per process. All six runs were exact to B1 across 1,206 shared prefixes. Session geomeans were 1.286× and 1.255× vs B1, and 1.040× and 1.051× vs BNB. Combined over six pairs: **1.271× vs B1** (descriptive paired log-t 95% interval 1.245–1.297) and **1.045× vs BNB** (1.005–1.088). Acceptance was 334/508. Every block output differed from BNB; the BNB-relative gain was not uniform per prompt (one pair was 0.989×). See the [summary](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm003_005_256_summary_v1.json), [session 0](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm003_005_256_session0_v1.json), and [session 1](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm003_005_256_session1_v1.json). The paired interval is descriptive over three prompt clusters on one GPU, not a generalization interval. This strengthens the B1 speed result but leaves B2 token fidelity unsatisfied.

The 512-token repeat is complete on the same three prompts in two processes. All six runs were exact to B1 over 2,416 shared prefixes; acceptance was 656/1,038. Combined geomean speedups were **1.246× vs B1** (descriptive paired log-t 95% interval 1.200–1.294) and **1.012× vs BNB** (0.960–1.068); per-process BNB geomeans were 1.010× and 1.014×. No output matched BNB tokens, and one pair was 0.927× vs BNB. The [summary](triton_b1_r2_contract_api_perf_cnndm003_005_512_summary_v1.json) links both process reports. The B1 gain repeats; BNB speed parity remains uncertain.

Next, profile latency by phase on these longer runs and broaden development evaluation to another task before any held-out evaluation. Keep claims limited to exactness against the R=2 B1 contract and report BNB divergence separately. Held-out GSM8K remains sealed.

A `num_stages=1/2/3` screen also found no useful tuning: M=2 CUDA-event medians were effectively equal across stages, and NCU showed unchanged registers/thread (168 q_proj, 166 down_proj) for stage 1 versus stage 3. Occupancy changed by less than half a point and local-memory sectors remained zero. No whole-model run was warranted for stage=1. This suggests a new dataflow/kernel design is needed to move the current register/throughput frontier; changing the launch pipeline stage count is not enough. See the [stage summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/pipeline_stage_summary.json) and [operator benchmark](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_n16_warp_sweep_v1/pipeline_stage_benchmark.json).

A pairwise-nibble dataflow prototype removed the full interleave vector and reduced high/low contributions in pairs. Both split and fused pair variants passed M=1/2 rowwise singleton checks, but neither improved M=2 latency in the initial comparison: split was 1.017×/1.008× the interleaved baseline on q_proj/down_proj; fused was 1.016×/1.051×. NCU on the split variant showed 246 registers/thread on both projections, versus 168/166 for interleaved. A follow-up 1/2/4-warp sweep again passed singleton equality, but pairwise latency regressed on down_proj at every warp count (1.041×–1.312×); q_proj showed one small M=2/warp=1 win (0.972×) that did not reproduce the earlier comparison consistently, while the remaining q_proj cases regressed (1.025×–1.140×). The prototype changes outputs (maximum absolute difference up to 0.00195), so it needs a separate reference and has no stable speed benefit for whole-model evaluation. It remains unregistered. See the [pairwise summary](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_pairwise_dataflow_v1/summary.json), [warp sweep](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_pairwise_dataflow_v1/operator_benchmark_warp_sweep.json), and [operator artifacts](triton_nf4_packed_rows2_pairwise_dataflow_v1/).

The pilot runner now exposes `--ngram-size` so draft matching can be screened without changing the target reference. One process run per prompt at n-gram sizes 2 and 4 retained token/KV equality and produced the same acceptance counts as size 3 (7/28 each for n=2 and n=4; 21/84 across the six n=3 runs). Their latency observations were similar to the existing n=3 range, but the unequal repeat count means this screen does not justify selecting a new draft size. See the [screen artifact](triton_nf4_group_scale_packed_k256_k2_ngram_size_screen_v1.json).

A K256 synthetic boundary audit exercised 15 combinations of K/N shape, nested and non-nested NF4 scales, and bias, comparing M=1…5 (**75 first-row checks**). All 75 were bitwise shape invariant, and all 15 repeated-row checks matched. In one non-nested, no-bias case (K=256,N=17), the output differed from BNB dequantize+`F.linear` by at most 0.0078125; the other 14 cases were bitwise equal to that BNB construction. This reinforces that the empirical contract is shape invariance for the fixed K256 schedule, not equivalence to the original BNB arithmetic. See the [stress artifact](triton_nf4_group_scale_k256_qwen3b_synthetic_boundary_stress_v1.json) and [extended audit script](../../scripts/stress_nf4_fixed_rows_boundaries.py).

No second GPU is connected in this workspace. All 50 IDs from an attempted GSM8K extension reservation appeared in prior result artifacts; [`frozen_gsm8k_ext50_provenance_audit.json`](frozen_gsm8k_ext50_provenance_audit.json) marks that reservation invalid for a newly unseen held-out claim. A new, untouched local cohort has now been frozen at GSM8K test indices 300–349 with a [pinned source revision and prompt hashes](frozen_gsm8k_300_349.json). None of those IDs appeared in prior local result artifacts. It has **not** been used to tune or evaluate this prototype. A second GPU and a complete three-backend, three-model study remain necessary for the planned final paper claim.


A direct BNB Linear4bit shape screen used the first decode-step activation from cnndm_000, repeated identically across M=1/2/3/5 rows, then called each original module directly. At the sampled q_proj, k_proj, v_proj, o_proj and MLP projections in layers 0, 33 and 35, all rows were bitwise equal to M=1 at M=2 and M=3. At M=5, q_proj and the sampled MLP projections changed; k_proj and v_proj stayed equal in layer 0, while o_proj changed. This supports the observed R=3 versus R=5 boundary, but is a one-activation operator screen, not proof for distinct speculative activations or the full model. The LM head differed from M=1 at every tested M>1 (max absolute difference 0.0625), consistent with keeping its projection rowwise in the BNB-compatible wrapper. Sampled MLP projection medians grew to about 1.2–1.7× at M=2/3 and about 3.2× at M=5 relative to M=1; the first-layer q_proj was about 0.87× at M=2/3 but 1.32× at M=5. These are direct-module CUDA-event timings with three rounds and one prompt, not end-to-end speed results. See the [shape screen artifact](qwen3b_bnb_linear_shape_invariance_cnndm000_v1.json) and [runner](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_linear_shape_invariance.py).

Next operator-audit step: repeat the direct screen over multiple development prompts and decode positions, capture distinct block-row activations from the actual verifier, and add per-row equality checks for every row, not just replicated inputs. Then isolate whether the first BNB mismatch at M=5 is specific to q_proj dispatch or also appears in MLP/o_proj before choosing a kernel intervention. Keep BNB-compatible B2 restricted to R=3 until a replacement reproduces singleton BNB outputs at every covered operator. The GSM8K 300–349 held-out cohort remains unopened.


The same direct shape screen was repeated on development prompt cnndm_001. Its 14 sampled modules reproduced the same equality pattern: all sampled projections except lm_head remained bitwise equal at M=2/3; at M=5 the same 11 q/o/MLP modules differed, while layer-0 k_proj and v_proj remained equal. This is a second prompt-level activation replication and strengthens the dispatch diagnosis, but does not remove the distinct-row limitation above. See [cnndm_001 artifact](qwen3b_bnb_linear_shape_invariance_cnndm001_v1.json).


A corrected actual-block audit now captures Transformers keyword-argument inputs, including the Qwen attention wrapper (the earlier hook omitted it). For K=2 / M=3 on cnndm_000 and cnndm_001, layer-0 input_layernorm and q_proj/k_proj/v_proj outputs match the concatenated singleton calls bitwise. The layer-0 self_attn wrapper receives identical hidden states but its output differs (max abs 0.00390625 and 0.0078125, respectively); the resulting input to o_proj differs by 0.001953125 on both prompts. Differences then propagate through the residual/MLP and later layers. This localizes the first observed BNB block-versus-singleton mismatch to within the attention path after q/k/v projection (for example RoPE/causal SDPA handling); the hooks do not yet distinguish those sub-operations. Artifacts: [cnndm_000 corrected audit](qwen3b_bnb_actual_block_operator_audit_cnndm000_k2_v3.json) and [cnndm_001 corrected audit](qwen3b_bnb_actual_block_operator_audit_cnndm001_k2_v2.json). The audit compares one fixed prefix and three candidate rows with CPU hook copies, so it is diagnostic and not a general guarantee.

This changes the G2 priority: implement or force a shape-invariant attention path first, while retaining the BNB singleton linear schedule; the M=5 replicated-activation result remains a separate linear dispatch issue. Next, instrument post-RoPE q/k, the attention context immediately before o_proj, causal-mask/position inputs, and KV writes on the same two development prefixes. Only after locating the exact sub-operation should we choose a controlled kernel or reference schedule.


A deeper SDPA trace on the same K=2/M=3 block confirms the first attention-path mismatch. At layer 0, all three rows have bitwise-equal post-RoPE q/k, SDPA queries, and the visible K/V prefixes after normalizing the block path’s repeated GQA heads against singleton enable_gqa. The block mask restricted to each row’s valid prefix is all-visible, matching the singleton no-mask call. Despite those equal semantic inputs, base SDPA context outputs differ on all rows of both prompts: cnndm_000 max abs by row = 0.0009765625, 0.001953125, 0.000244140625; cnndm_001 = 0.000244140625, 0.001953125, 0.001953125. On cnndm_000, forcing PyTorch SDPBackend.MATH reduces the differences to 0.000244140625, 0, and 9.5367431640625e-7; it does not make all rows bitwise exact. Explicitly expanding singleton GQA heads before the same math backend did not further change those results. This points to shape-dependent SDPA arithmetic, and shows a generic math backend alone is not an exact fix. The forced-backend screen is diagnostic only and includes no latency claim. See [cnndm_000 SDPA backend screen](qwen3b_bnb_sdpa_backend_screen_cnndm000_k2_v2.json), [cnndm_000 internal trace](qwen3b_bnb_sdpa_internal_audit_cnndm000_k2_v3.json), and [cnndm_001 internal trace](qwen3b_bnb_sdpa_internal_audit_cnndm001_k2_v1.json).

G2 next: prototype a fixed per-query SDPA reduction schedule or a shape-invariant attention kernel, first on captured layer-0 tensors. Require bitwise output equality against singleton for every query row and verify causal-prefix/KV semantics. Then profile the cost before integrating into the full model. Keep the production candidate at K=2/R=3 and the held-out cohort sealed.


A per-query SDPA override was prototyped in the diagnostic runner: for a block call, it compacts the repeated GQA heads, slices each query to its visible KV prefix, and invokes SDPA with the same singleton call shape/flags; the LM head is also evaluated one row at a time. On K=2/M=3 fixed prefixes cnndm_000 and cnndm_001, all 398 hooked module outputs matched the three sequential singleton forwards bitwise, including every decoder-layer output and LM-head logits (0 differing module outputs on each prompt). This is a finite module/logit check; it did not yet compare canonical KV cache tensors after the calls, did not generate a longer closed-loop sequence, and includes no latency result. The diagnostic change lives in [audit_operator_shape.py](../../scripts/audit_operator_shape.py). See [override cnndm_000](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_sdpa_override_cnndm000_k2_v2.json) and [override cnndm_001](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_sdpa_override_cnndm001_k2_v1.json).

This validates a correctness fallback, not a fast parallel attention kernel. Next measure the rowwise attention/LM-head cost and compare KV-cache tensors; then implement a per-query fixed-reduction kernel that can process rows concurrently while preserving the singleton accumulation schedule. Do not promote this override to a performance claim.


Canonical cache snapshots were added to the per-query fallback audit. On both fixed K=2/M=3 prefixes, native BNB block verification first diverges in layer-1 KV (cnndm_000: key max abs 0.03125, value 0.00390625; cnndm_001: key 0.0625, value 0.00390625). With per-query SDPA and rowwise LM head, all 36 layers have exact key and value tensors and all 398 hooked module outputs are bitwise equal to the singleton reference. This closes the state check for these two transitions, but not longer closed-loop decoding or all decoder branches. See [cache-checked cnndm_000](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_sdpa_override_cnndm000_k2_v3.json) and [cache-checked cnndm_001](qwen3b_bnb_rowwise_sdpa_override_cnndm001_k2_v2.json).

A separate 15-round paired compute-only screen on the same prefixes measured singleton / native block / exact rowwise fallback medians of 70.41 / 26.77 / 35.31 ms on cnndm_000 and 67.65 / 25.84 / 33.13 ms on cnndm_001. The rowwise fallback was 1.99× and 2.04× faster than three sequential singleton forwards, but 1.32× and 1.28× slower than the native block. Cache restoration was outside the timer; this is not end-to-end decode latency and the CPU-side loop is not a parallel attention implementation. Artifacts: [timing cnndm_000](qwen3b_rowwise_sdpa_timing_cnndm000_k2_v1.json) and [timing cnndm_001](qwen3b_rowwise_sdpa_timing_cnndm001_k2_v1.json).

The next G2 investment is now specific: preserve singleton SDPA arithmetic while parallelizing independent query rows in a custom attention kernel, then measure full-cycle cache/commit overhead. The fallback establishes a correctness target; the timing screen shows compute headroom but does not prove an end-to-end speedup.


The fixed-row Triton attention prototype has now been integrated for one Qwen decode transition. It assigns one program to each query/head and uses a fixed key-tile online-softmax schedule (BLOCK_K=32). On K=2/M=3 transitions from cnndm_000 and cnndm_001, the Triton block matched its Triton singleton reference across all 398 hooked module outputs, greedy next-token IDs, and K/V tensors in all 36 layers. This validates shape identity only for these two transitions from a shared original-BNB prefill cache; it is not a long closed-loop or universal proof. The cleaned integration artifacts are [cnndm_000 v7](qwen3b_triton_fixed_row_attention_cnndm000_k2_v7.json) and [cnndm_001](qwen3b_triton_fixed_row_attention_cnndm001_k2_v1.json). Earlier integration artifacts v1–v5 were generated before fixing non-contiguous output allocation and must not be used as evidence.

The custom singleton schedule is not state-equivalent to original BNB: on both prefixes, the first K/V mismatch is at layer 1 (cnndm_000 key/value max abs 0.03125/0.00390625; cnndm_001 0.0625/0.00390625), although the three greedy next-token IDs matched on these short transitions. Therefore this is a B1 fixed-row direction, not B2 certification to original BNB. The current audit includes BNB-vs-fixed module output comparison and all-layer KV comparison.

A 15-round paired compute-only screen measured fixed-Triton singleton/block medians of 70.72/34.32 ms on cnndm_000 and 69.18/33.67 ms on cnndm_001, giving 2.06× and 2.05× block speedups within this fixed schedule. Original BNB singleton/block medians were 65.60/26.88 ms and 63.89/26.20 ms. The fixed block costs 1.28× the native BNB block, which is not an exact comparator. Cache restore, prefill, drafting, and commit are outside the timer; these numbers are not end-to-end speedups. See [timing cnndm_000](qwen3b_triton_fixed_row_attention_timing_cnndm000_k2_v1.json) and [timing cnndm_001](qwen3b_triton_fixed_row_attention_timing_cnndm001_k2_v1.json).

This was the first actual-model B1 feasibility signal: exact block/singleton state equality on two decode transitions with about 2× compute-only gain over the same singleton schedule. Longer closed-loop validation is now recorded below. Next, measure phase-level overhead on the closed-loop runner and profile the custom attention kernel; keep B2 and end-to-end claims separate and the held-out GSM8K cohort sealed.

The fixed-row attention path completed a closed-loop 128-token audit on two additional development prompts in separate processes (`cnndm_003` and `cnndm_004`), with K=2/R=3, block K=32, and BNB fixed-row linears. Both runs matched B1 token IDs, all 128 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Each prompt accepted all proposal counts consistently within its two timing rounds (38/70 for `cnndm_003`, 22/58 for `cnndm_004`). Paired timing geomeans were 1.474× and 1.287× versus B1 singleton; versus original BNB they were 1.118× and 0.919×. Across the four rounds, descriptive aggregate geomeans were 1.377× versus B1 and 1.013× versus BNB, but **0/4 block outputs matched BNB token IDs**. The BNB timing result is therefore mixed across prompts and does not establish a stable gain or B2 fidelity. See the [aggregate](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm3_4_128_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 run](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm003_k2_r3_128_s0_v1.json), and [cnndm_004 run](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm004_k2_r3_128_s1_v1.json). This extends empirical B1 closed-loop exactness beyond the two-transition audit; it remains a two-prompt development result on one GPU. Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.

The same two prompts were extended to 256 generated tokens in fresh processes. Both runs matched the fixed-row B1 token sequence, all 256 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Acceptance was 78/142 on `cnndm_003` and 71/135 on `cnndm_004`. One paired timing round per prompt gave a two-prompt descriptive geomean speedup of **1.445× vs B1 singleton** and **1.068× vs original BNB**; neither block output matched BNB tokens. This supports long closed-loop B1 state equality and a preliminary BNB latency signal, but two prompt-level measurements are insufficient for a stable performance claim. See the [256-token summary](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm3_4_256_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 run](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm003_k2_r3_256_s2_v1.json), and [cnndm_004 run](triton_fixed_row_attention_closed_loop_cnndm004_k2_r3_256_s3_v1.json). The cycle trace shows verification dominates measured cycle time (median about 33.5 ms); profiling the custom attention and per-layer linears is the next step. Held-out GSM8K remains sealed.

A 24-token PyTorch profile of the custom-attention block (`cnndm_003`, K=2/R=3, BNB fixed-row linears) measured 1.123 s in verification. Summed CUDA kernel time was 615.4 ms: BNB 4-bit GEMM used 295.6 ms (48.0%); other GEMM kernels used 184.8 ms (30.0%); the custom `_fixed_row_attention_tiled` kernel used 10.6 ms (1.7%). This is an instrumented diagnostic trace with a profiler event-clear warning, not a latency comparison, but it indicates attention is no longer the main optimization target. It motivated profiling the already-screened N=16/one-warp NF4 schedule at K=1/R=2; that matching profile follows below. See the [profile](triton_fixed_row_attention_profile_cnndm003_24_v1/profile.json) and [family analysis](triton_fixed_row_attention_profile_cnndm003_24_v1/analysis.json).

A matching 24-token profile was captured for the combined K=1/R=2 path on `cnndm_003` using Triton attention and the N=16/one-warp NF4 linear schedule. Verification measured 960 ms; summed CUDA kernel time was 587.4 ms, of which `_packed_two_rows_kernel` contributed 295.7 ms (50.3%) and `_fixed_row_attention_tiled` contributed 9.6 ms (1.6%). This is a profiler-instrumented diagnostic with an event-clear warning and must not be compared as a latency benchmark. It confirms the packed NF4 projection remains the primary custom operator target; attention is a smaller component at this workload. See the [combined profile](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_profile_cnndm003_24_v1/profile.json) and [kernel-family analysis](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_profile_cnndm003_24_v1/analysis.json).

The combined custom-attention + N=16/one-warp NF4 linear path was first run closed-loop at K=1/R=2 on two development prompt/budget pairs. `cnndm_003` at 128 tokens matched the same B1 reference across all tokens, 128 KV prefixes, and logical lengths; `cnndm_004` at 256 tokens matched across 256 prefixes. Both used the contract-compatible row-2 NF4 shape and `triton_batched` attention. Paired timing geomeans were 1.288×/1.295× versus B1 singleton and 1.073×/1.057× versus original BNB; both outputs diverged from BNB token IDs. These were one process per prompt with only two timing rounds on the 128-token run and one on the 256-token run, so treat them as integration feasibility, not a stable speed claim. See the [summary](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_summary_v1.json), [128-token run](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_cnndm003_128_s4_v1.json), and [256-token run](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_cnndm004_256_s5_v1.json). The fixed-budget independent-process repeat is summarized below. The held-out GSM8K cohort remains sealed.

The fixed-budget repeat now covers 256 output tokens on `cnndm_003/004/005`, with two independent process sessions per prompt. All six runs matched B1 tokens, all 256 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Across eleven timing pairs, descriptive geomean speedup was **1.302× vs B1 singleton** and **1.074× vs original BNB**. Prompt-level estimates were 1.308×/1.113× for `cnndm_003`, 1.302×/1.045× for `cnndm_004`, and 1.296×/1.058× for `cnndm_005`. Acceptance totaled 116/182, 110/168, and 108/158 draft tokens, respectively. **0/11 outputs matched BNB tokens.** The B1 speed result repeated across these three prompts; the BNB timing signal is positive in this development cohort, but three prompt clusters on one GPU do not establish general performance. See the [multisession summary](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_256_multisession_summary_v1.json) and runs: [cnndm_003 sessions 6/8](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_cnndm003_256_s6_v1.json), [cnndm_004 sessions 5/7](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_cnndm004_256_s5_v1.json), and [cnndm_005 sessions 9/10](triton_fixed_attention_nf4_r2_closed_loop_cnndm005_256_s9_v1.json). Next evaluate additional development workloads and independent hardware before opening held-out data; the held-out GSM8K cohort remains sealed.

An experimental singleton-only NF4 kernel removes the unused second-row accumulator from M=1 calls while retaining N=16/K=256/one-warp reduction order. On the first Qwen layer's q_proj and down_proj, it was bitwise equal to row 0 of the existing two-row kernel and reduced operator latency by 9.6% and 15.7%, respectively. BNB singleton was still substantially faster on down_proj (59.8 μs vs 102.8 μs for the one-row prototype). A random-activation screen across all **252 Linear4bit modules** found exact M=1 output equality against the old two-row kernel in every case; this is finite evidence, not a proof for all activations.

The prototype was exercised in closed loop by using the one-row kernel for singleton B1 calls and retaining the current two-row kernel for block verification. At 128 tokens, runs on `cnndm_003` (two processes) and `cnndm_004` (one process) all matched the optimized B1 token sequence, all 128 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Across six timing pairs, the descriptive geomean was **1.212× vs optimized B1 singleton** and **1.107× vs original BNB**; prompt-level ratios were 1.268×/1.136× (`cnndm_003`) and 1.108×/1.050× (`cnndm_004`). No output matched BNB tokens.

The 256-token repeat covers three prompts (`cnndm_003/004/005`), with two independent process sessions per prompt. All six runs matched the optimized B1 token sequence, all 256 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Across eleven timing pairs, descriptive geomean speedup was **1.214× vs optimized B1 singleton** and **1.132× vs original BNB**; prompt-level values were 1.240×/1.143× (`cnndm_003`), 1.196×/1.121× (`cnndm_004`), and 1.212×/1.134× (`cnndm_005`). Acceptance totals were 174/273, 220/336, and 216/316 draft tokens. **0/11 outputs matched BNB tokens.** This is a repeatable development signal on three prompts, not a general performance result. The one-row kernel is **not integrated or pinned by the live reference contract**. See the [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/summary.json), [256-token multisession summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_256_summary.json), [operator benchmark](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/operator_benchmark.json), [252-module audit](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/all_module_operator_audit_cnnd003.json), and [256-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_cnndm003_256_s8_v1.json). Held-out GSM8K remains unopened.


The gated development screen now covers HumanEval extension prompts `/110`–`/113`, each at 128 generated tokens in two independent processes with two paired timing rounds per process. All eight runs matched experimental optimized B1 tokens, all 128 KV prefixes, and logical lengths. Across 16 timing pairs, descriptive geomeans were **1.298× vs optimized B1 singleton** and **1.202× vs original BNB**, but the aggregate is strongly prompt-dependent: per-prompt ratios were 1.173×/1.051× (`/110`), 1.116×/1.070× (`/111`), 1.789×/1.670× (`/112`), and 1.213×/1.112× (`/113`). Prompt `/112` accepted 122/124 draft tokens across its two processes, explaining much of its larger gain. BNB token match stayed prompt-dependent: 0/4 rounds for `/110`, 4/4 for `/111`, and 0/4 for `/112` and `/113` (4/16 overall). These are development results on one GPU, not a general speed claim or B2 guarantee. The manifest gate requires `--enable-experimental-singleton` and validates source/runtime identity before benchmark access/model loading. After load, the runner also validates the base live B1 contract, including model config, tokenizer, quantized-state hash, runtime, and 16 pinned sources, before decoding; this confirms the base reference identity and does not certify the experimental one-row kernel. The valid route completed an 8-token `/110` smoke with exact B1 token/KV state; its single timing pair (0.976× vs singleton, 0.930× vs BNB) is not performance evidence. The artifact records base contract ID `61f0c8f15fbc91b9ce86dd35da55360940accdc59642d1fe9faff985a2019bee` and quantized-state SHA-256 matching the existing B1 reference. Missing opt-in and a tampered source hash were both rejected before model execution; see the [live-identity smoke artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/live_identity_smoke_humaneval114_8_s28.json). See the [HumanEval summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/humaneval_128_summary.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/experiment_manifest.json), [gated smoke](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/gated_smoke_humaneval110_8_s15.json), and the process artifacts for [/110 sessions 11/13](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval110_128_s11_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval110_128_s13_v1.json), [/111 sessions 12/14](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval111_128_s12_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval111_128_s14_v1.json), [/112 sessions 16/17](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_128_s16_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_128_s17_v1.json), and [/113 sessions 18/19](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval113_128_s18_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval113_128_s19_v1.json). The one-row kernel remains an unintegrated prototype; GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.


The 256-token HumanEval screen now covers `/112` and `/113`, each in two independent gated processes. All four runs matched optimized B1 tokens, all **256 KV prefixes**, and logical lengths. Across eight timing pairs, descriptive geomeans were **1.635× vs optimized B1 singleton** and **1.486× vs original BNB**, with strong acceptance dependence: `/112` was 1.910×/1.758× at 250/252 accepted drafts, while `/113` was 1.399×/1.256× at 154/208. The block outputs differed from BNB in **8/8** rounds. These are two prompt-specific results on one GPU, not a general speedup estimate or B2 result. See the [two-prompt 256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/humaneval_256_two_prompt_summary.json), [HumanEval/112 summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/humaneval_112_256_summary.json), [`/112` sessions 20/21](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_256_s20_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_256_s21_v1.json), and [`/113` sessions 22/23](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval113_256_s22_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/closed_loop_humaneval113_256_s23_v1.json). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.


A prompt-level analysis of the existing 256-token artifacts now joins seven prompts across CNN/DM and HumanEval (14 independent process sessions, 27 timing pairs). All runs remained exact to optimized B1. Acceptance ranged from 59.8% to 99.2%; its Pearson correlation with per-prompt geomean speedup was 0.963 vs B1 singleton and 0.974 vs BNB, while Spearman was 0.679 and 0.893, respectively. The HumanEval/112 high-acceptance prompt affects the B1 correlation; excluding it reduces Pearson from 0.963 to 0.906. Treat these as descriptive only: n=7 prompt means, task/prompt effects are confounded, and no population-level inferential interval is claimed. A stratified prompt-cluster bootstrap (20,000 resamples, preserving the observed 3:4 task mix) gives cohort-only 95% percentile ranges of **1.275–1.506× vs B1** and **1.151–1.388× vs BNB**; it resamples prompt means and does not capture within-process timing noise. Equal-prompt-weighted geomean speedups across this selected seven-prompt set were 1.380× vs B1 and 1.256× vs BNB; all 27 block outputs differed from BNB tokens. The additional HumanEval prompts `/114` and `/115` had per-prompt BNB speedups of 1.098× and 1.403× at 59.8% and 86.3% acceptance, respectively, further showing workload dependence. This remains a small development cohort, not a general performance result. See the [acceptance/speedup analysis](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/acceptance_speed_tradeoff_256_analysis.json), [four-prompt HumanEval summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/humaneval_256_four_prompt_summary.json), and [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_nf4_acceptance_speed_tradeoff.py). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.


The gated runner now records per-method GPU memory baselines, absolute peak allocated/reserved bytes, and incremental peak bytes during each timed call. An 8-token HumanEval/115 smoke produced incremental allocated peaks of 114.6 MB for block, 114.0 MB for singleton, and 175.4 MB for original BNB; reserved peak did not increase because the CUDA allocator pool was already warm. These are smoke-run values, not full-length peak-VRAM benchmark results. The output also passed the manifest gate and live base B1 identity check; see the [memory telemetry artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/memory_telemetry_smoke_humaneval115_8_s29.json).


Full-budget memory telemetry covers HumanEval/114 and `/115`, each in two independent processes (four 256-token runs, eight paired timing rounds). All four runs were exact to B1 across 256 prefixes. Incremental allocated peak was repeatable across processes within each prompt: `/114` block/singleton/BNB peaks were **41.6/41.0/101.1 MB**; `/115` peaks were **125.4/124.7/175.4 MB**. Reserved memory did not rise above the already allocated CUDA pool. The measured inputs were 83 tokens (`/114`) and 321 tokens (`/115`); higher peaks co-occurred with the longer prompt, but length and prompt content are confounded and two prompt observations do not establish a causal model. These allocator tensor peaks are not a general device-VRAM bound. The [memory summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/memory_telemetry_summary.json) includes these full-length runs and the 8-token smoke.


A same-prompt output-budget sweep on HumanEval/114 (83 input tokens) now has two independent processes and four paired timing rounds at each of 64/128/256 output tokens. Every run was exact to optimized B1 across the full generated prefix. Acceptance was 3/30 (10.0%), 46/84 (54.8%), and 208/348 (59.8%). Paired geomean speedup vs BNB was **0.952×**, **1.061×**, and **1.120×**; vs optimized singleton B1 it was 1.006×, 1.196×, and 1.281×. Block outputs matched BNB for 4/4, 4/4, and 0/4 timing rounds, showing the finite token-match horizon is not a whole-state guarantee. Incremental allocated peak for the block path was 32.7 MB at 64, 35.6 MB at 128, and 41.6 MB at 256; original BNB stayed at 101.1 MB. This is a repeatable indication that the shortest budget does not cover its overhead against BNB, while 128/256 do on this prompt. The break-even remains prompt- and budget-specific, not generalizable. See the [budget sweep summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/generation_budget_humaneval114_summary.json). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.


The 64/128/256-token sweep was repeated on HumanEval/115 (321 input tokens), with two independent gated processes and four timing rounds at every budget. All six runs were exact to optimized B1. BNB speedups were **1.043×, 1.251×, and 1.387×** at 64/128/256, compared with **0.952×, 1.060×, and 1.120×** on HumanEval/114. HumanEval/115 accepted 18/44, 74/106, and 202/234 draft tokens at those budgets; BNB token match was 0/4, 0/4, and 0/8. Thus the shorter budget crossed the BNB break-even on `/115` but not `/114`, demonstrating prompt dependence; the BNB trajectory also diverged on `/115` even at 64 tokens. Incremental block peak allocation for `/115` was recorded per budget in the [two-prompt sweep summary](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/generation_budget_humaneval114_115_summary.json). Do not generalize these two prompts into a universal threshold.


A token-trace comparison localizes BNB divergence in the repeated output-budget sweep. For HumanEval/114, the block token IDs matched BNB through all 64 and 128 generated tokens; at 256 tokens, all four rounds first diverged at zero-based index **237** (the 238th generated token). For HumanEval/115, all budgets first diverged at zero-based index **4** (the fifth generated token) in every round. These are token-sequence observations only; matching a token prefix does not establish BNB KV/state equality. See the [divergence-horizon artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_row_kernel_v1/bnb_divergence_horizons_humaneval_sweep.json) and [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_nf4_bnb_divergence_horizons.py).

A same-prompt schedule screen compares K=1/R=2 and K=2/R=3 on HumanEval/114 (83 input tokens), using two independent sessions and four paired timing rounds per profile/output budget. All runs in both profiles matched that profile's experimental B1 singleton tokens and every generated KV/state prefix. At 128 output tokens, K=1/R=2 reached 54.8% draft acceptance and geomean speedups of 1.201x vs its singleton and 1.060x vs original BNB; K=2/R=3 reached 38.4%, 1.134x, and 1.018x. Both profiles matched BNB tokens in 4/4 rounds at 128. At 256 tokens, K=1/R=2 reached 59.8% acceptance and 1.281x/1.120x speedups; K=2/R=3 reached 46.5% and 1.174x/1.079x. Neither profile matched BNB tokens in any of the four 256-token rounds. On this prompt and these runs, expanding K/R reduced acceptance and the measured speedup; it is not evidence for a general ordering across prompts. The K2/R3 manifest and separate runner keep this result isolated: its post-load check uses the K1/R2 base contract only to identify the loaded model/tokenizer/weights/runtime, and does not certify K2/R3 arithmetic. This is a singleton-relative development screen, not B2 or cross-profile state equivalence. See the [K1/R2 vs K2/R3 paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/k1r2_vs_k2r3_humaneval114_summary.json), the [K2/R3 manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/experiment_manifest.json), and [K2/R3 128-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval114_128_s0_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval114_128_s1_v1.json), plus the [256-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval114_256_s2_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval114_256_s3_v1.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The matched K1/R2 vs K2/R3 screen was extended to HumanEval/115, with two independent sessions and four timing rounds for each profile at 128 and 256 generated tokens. All eight K2/R3 runs matched their own singleton token/state prefixes exactly. At 128 tokens, K2/R3 reached 66.7% acceptance and 1.357x/1.249x speedups (vs singleton/BNB), close to K1/R2 at 69.8% and 1.361x/1.251x. At 256 tokens, K2/R3 reached 84.0% acceptance and 1.640x/1.480x, while K1/R2 reached 86.3% and 1.557x/1.387x. Both profiles differed from BNB in every measured round on HumanEval/115. Together with HumanEval/114, this shows K2/R3's relative speed is prompt- and budget-dependent: it lagged K1/R2 on `/114`, was essentially tied at `/115` 128, and led at `/115` 256 despite somewhat lower acceptance. These small descriptive cohorts do not establish a general K/R ranking or B2 fidelity. See the [two-prompt K1/R2 vs K2/R3 summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/k1r2_vs_k2r3_humaneval114_115_summary.json), and the [HumanEval/115 K2/R3 128-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval115_128_s4_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval115_128_s5_v1.json), plus the [256-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval115_256_s6_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval115_256_s7_v1.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

K2/R3 was also evaluated on HumanEval/112, a high-acceptance development prompt, in two independent sessions at each 128/256-token budget. All four runs matched K2/R3 singleton tokens and all KV/state prefixes. Acceptance was 97.6% at 128 and 98.8% at 256; geomean speedups were 1.954x/1.782x and 2.260x/2.023x (vs singleton/BNB). The matched K1/R2 runs had 98.4%/99.2% acceptance and 1.789x/1.670x and 1.910x/1.758x speedups. Both profiles differed from BNB in all eight rounds on `/112`. This prompt supports K2/R3 as a promising high-acceptance case, while `/114` shows it can lose to K1/R2; it remains a selected development result, not a general speedup claim or B2 correctness result. The [three-prompt comparison summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/k1r2_vs_k2r3_humaneval112_114_115_summary.json) includes all matched profile/budget results. See the [K2/R3 128-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_128_s8_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_128_s9_v1.json), plus the [256-token runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_256_s10_v1.json) and [repeat](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/closed_loop_humaneval112_256_s11_v1.json). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.

A separate acceptance/latency analysis now reads all 48 paired timing rounds across the three prompts, both K/R profiles, and 128/256-token budgets. It records accepted/drafted ratios, end-to-end milliseconds per generated token, runner-reported verification milliseconds per token, and BNB token-match counts for every round. Within these selected prompt/budget cells, K2/R3's BNB speedup tracks the acceptance pattern: it is behind K1/R2 on low-acceptance `/114`, nearly tied on `/115` at 128, and ahead on high-acceptance `/112` plus `/115` at 256. Verification accounts for most measured block latency (92.5–98.3% of end-to-end block time across these rounds), so the next performance work should target verification execution cost and acceptance-conditioned amortization. This is descriptive evidence only: prompt, budget, acceptance, and timing are confounded, with only three prompts and two sessions per cell. See the [acceptance/latency analysis JSON](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/acceptance_latency_analysis.json) and its [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_nf4_profile_acceptance_latency.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Diagnostic K2/R3 PyTorch operator traces were captured on HumanEval/112 (157 input tokens, 17/19 draft accepted in the 32-token trace) and `/114` (83 input tokens, 0/12 accepted). They show 252 `_packed_two_rows_kernel` launches per target forward (3,780 and 7,812 launches; 322.7 ms and 522.4 ms aggregate CUDA event time), followed by the block LM-head GEMM on shape `[3,2048] × [2048,151936]` (52.8 ms over 15 forwards; 106.3 ms over 31). The fixed-row attention kernel accounts for 6.5/6.8 ms across the traces, only 36 calls per target forward. Remaining bitsandbytes GEMMs have prompt-sequence input shapes and are attributed to prefill, not block verification. This makes packed quantized linear the primary kernel optimization target, with the full-vocabulary LM head the next large block-path cost; attention is not the current bottleneck. Profiler totals are diagnostic and instrumented, and per-operator CUDA event times can overlap, so they are not added into an exact verify-time decomposition or used as speedup measurements. See the [operator profile summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/operator_profile_summary.json) and [summary script](../../scripts/summarize_k2r3_operator_profiles.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An M=3 operator-only tile/warp screen now benchmarks the actual K2/R3 row count on four first-layer Qwen NF4 matrix shapes, with seven alternating timing rounds. All seven candidates were rowwise singleton-equal for M=1/2/3 under their own schedule. At M=3, the fastest tile was N16/K256/warp2 for q_proj (37.96 vs 39.87 us, 4.8% lower) and down_proj (199.68 vs 207.58 us, 3.8% lower); k_proj was marginally fastest at warp4 (23.49 vs 23.87 us, 1.6% lower); gate_proj stayed fastest at warp1 (170.14 us). Most alternate schedules were not bitwise equal to warp1, so they imply a different arithmetic reference and require whole-model validation. A shape-specific mix using those minima would save only about 1.85% across these seven linear calls per layer by the first-layer random-input estimate. **No-go for integration at this point**: the predicted gain is small, measured on one layer with synthetic inputs, and changes operator outputs. The current warp1 reference remains pinned. See the [M=3 tile benchmark](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/m3_operator_tuning/operator_benchmark.json) and [benchmark script](../../scripts/benchmark_nf4_actual_k2_rows3.py). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.

The fused gate/up experiment now has a corrected same-process K2/R3 A/B. The prototype was bitwise equal to the two separate projection calls on all 36 layers for M=1/2/3, and in 20 closed-loop timing rounds across HumanEval/112 and `/114`, fused block output matched both standard K2 block and singleton tokens plus every observed KV/state prefix. The paired fused/standard end-to-end geomean was **1.0003x** with per-round range **0.974–1.036x**, so the launch reduction produced no stable latency gain. Fused-vs-BNB was 1.791x on high-acceptance `/112` and 1.021x on `/114`; this reflects the existing K2 workload behavior, not an added fusion speedup. **No-go for runtime integration**: retain the standard gate/up path, since fused arithmetic is exact but the measured end-to-end benefit is indistinguishable from timing variation. The first paired audit artifacts `s20/s21` were superseded after discovering their observer omitted intermediate committed prefixes; corrected runs `s22/s23` and repeats `s24/s25` cover all prefixes. See the [corrected paired A/B summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/gate_up_fusion/paired_ab_summary.json), [all-layer operator check](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/gate_up_fusion/all_layers_confirmation.json), and [paired A/B runner](../../scripts/audit_nf4_fused_gate_up_paired_k2_r3.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A two-prompt M=3 LM-head screen measured Triton matmul candidates using the last three actual prefill hidden rows for HumanEval/112 and `/114`. PyTorch `linear` took 3.49 ms and 3.48 ms; the fastest viable Triton schedule (N=128/K=128/4 warps) took 3.479 ms and 3.477 ms, a two-prompt geometric latency ratio of **0.9972×**. All tested viable candidates produced logits bitwise equal to PyTorch for these six rows and were rowwise invariant when the other two physical rows were zeroed. N=64/K=32 was about 1.81× slower; N=256/K=128 exceeded the GPU shared-memory limit. The sub-0.3% advantage is too small to justify changing the reference schedule, so the full-logit Triton path is **no-go for integration**. A fused argmax/output-reduction kernel could still be explored because it may remove a separate full-logit reduction, but it needs its own correctness and end-to-end screen. See the [two-prompt LM-head summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/two_prompt_summary.json), [benchmark script](../../scripts/benchmark_qwen_lm_head_triton_m3.py), and source-pinned [LM-head experiment manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/experiment_manifest.json). Held-out GSM8K indices 300–349 remain unused.

A fixed-M=3 Triton LM-head-plus-argmax prototype was screened on the last three actual prefill hidden rows for HumanEval/112 and `/114`. On both prompts, every tested candidate returned the same argmax IDs as Torch, the top values matched after casting to Torch's output dtype, and both IDs and top values were invariant when each physical row was evaluated alone. The best schedule (N=128/K=128/4 warps) had a two-prompt geometric latency ratio of **0.9893x** versus Torch linear+argmax, about a 1.1% operator-only reduction; other candidate ratios ranged from 0.9912x to 0.9961x. FP32 top values before the output-dtype cast differed from Torch by up to 0.0570 and 0.0615 on the two prompts, so this is not full-logit equivalence. The small operator gain, two-prompt sample, and lack of decoder integration/end-to-end measurements make this **no-go for integration for now**. Keep it as a candidate only if a decoder-level paired run can show a repeatable end-to-end benefit while preserving B1 token/KV state. See the [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/fused_argmax_two_prompt_summary.json), [HumanEval/112 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/humaneval112_fused_argmax_m3_v2.json), [HumanEval/114 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/humaneval114_fused_argmax_m3.json), and source-pinned [experiment manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/lm_head_tuning/fused_argmax_experiment_manifest.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain excluded.

A fixed-three-output-row packed NF4 kernel was prototyped for K2/R3. It assigns one output-row group per CTA, avoiding the pairwise schedule's padded fourth row and duplicate weight dequantization for the second row group. On four representative first-layer shapes, the N16/K256/1-warp three-row kernel was bitwise equal to the existing pairwise kernel and to its own M=1/2/3 singleton calls. Across two separate benchmark processes, M=3 median latency ratios versus the pairwise kernel were 0.623–0.645 for q_proj, 0.989 for k_proj, 0.637–0.639 for gate_proj, and 0.575–0.578 for down_proj. The 2-warp alternative for k_proj was faster but changed output bits, so it is excluded from the exact-reference candidate.

A finite random-input audit extended this check to all 252 Linear4bit modules in all 36 layers: M=1/2/3 rowwise invariance and M=3 bitwise equality to the pairwise reference passed for every module. A development closed-loop integration then used the new kernel only for three-row block calls while retaining the existing one-row singleton path. On HumanEval/112 and `/114`, two independent 128-token processes per prompt matched B1 token IDs and all **512/512 shared KV/state prefixes**. Across eight paired timing rounds, descriptive geomean speedup was **1.481x vs B1 singleton** (paired-log 95% interval 1.162–1.888) and **1.342x vs original BNB** (1.042–1.727). Prompt-level results were 1.942x/1.777x vs B1/BNB on `/112` and 1.129x/1.013x on `/114`, showing strong acceptance/workload dependence. Only 4/8 outputs matched original BNB tokens (0/4 on `/112`, 4/4 on `/114`). The intervals cover timing rounds for just two fixed prompts on one RTX 4050 and do not estimate performance across prompts. This is a promising B1 performance result, not a B2 equivalence claim or a production-ready contract. Next: broaden the development cohort, stress rejected suffix/EOS/pending-token paths, and repeat at 256 tokens before deciding whether to pin an M=3 schedule. See the [operator confirmation](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/operator_confirmation.json), [252-module audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/all_layer_operator_audit.json), [128-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/closed_loop_128_summary.json), [source-pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/experiment_manifest.json), and [opt-in closed-loop runner](../../scripts/audit_nf4_fixed_three_rows_closed_loop.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The fixed-three-row K2/R3 path was extended to 256 generated tokens on the same two development prompts. Both independent prompt runs matched B1 tokens and all **512/512 KV/state prefixes**. Across four paired rounds (one process per prompt), descriptive geomean speedup was **1.566x vs B1 singleton** (paired-log 95% interval 0.972–2.515) and **1.413x vs original BNB** (1.055–1.893). Prompt-specific speedups were 2.024x/1.841x on `/112` and 1.207x/1.084x on `/114`; neither prompt's block tokens matched BNB in either round. This reproduces the B1 gain at a longer budget while confirming that BNB token divergence is prompt/budget dependent. The two-prompt, one-session sample remains preliminary. See the [256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/closed_loop_256_summary.json) and [run artifacts](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/).

The new fixed-three-row path was repeated at 128 tokens on two additional development prompts (`HumanEval/113` and `/115`), with two independent processes per prompt. Across all four prompts and eight processes, token IDs and all **1,024/1,024 B1 state prefixes** matched. Across 16 paired timing rounds, the descriptive geomean speedup was **1.381x vs B1 singleton** (paired-log 95% interval 1.230–1.550) and **1.255x vs original BNB** (1.116–1.412). Prompt-level B1/BNB speedups were 1.942x/1.777x (`/112`), 1.205x/1.118x (`/113`), 1.129x/1.013x (`/114`), and 1.376x/1.233x (`/115`). BNB token identity matched only on `/114` (4/16 rounds overall), while all runs stayed exact to B1. Acceptance varied from 38.4% (`/114`) to 97.6% (`/112`), and the gain followed that workload difference. These intervals summarize timing rounds nested in four fixed prompts; they are not prompt-level generalization bounds. This expands the positive B1 result to four prompts and independent process repeats, but branch-specific EOS stress and a broader task are still needed before pinning the new schedule. See the [four-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/closed_loop_128_four_prompt_summary.json) and [new prompt runs](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/).
