The fixed-three-row decoder was also run on two pre-existing constructed K2/R3 EOS development fixtures with cycle tracing. In the accepted-EOS case, the draft contained EOS at index 0, the verifier accepted it, emitted `[3925, 151645]`, and matched B1 tokens plus both observed state prefixes. In the rejected-EOS case, the EOS draft at index 0 was rejected, the verifier emitted the preceding token and continued; the final sequence reached EOS and matched B1 tokens plus all three observed state prefixes. This exercises accepted EOS and rejected-EOS continuation in the experimental schedule, but only once each and does not cover every pending-token or rollback configuration. The inherited runner had labeled M=3 calls as pairwise; a post-run metadata annotation now preserves that prior value and records the actual fixed-three-row dispatch without changing measured outputs or timings. See the [EOS branch summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/eos_branch_summary.json), [accepted trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/eos_accept_trace.json), [rejected trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/eos_reject_trace.json), and [annotation script](../../scripts/annotate_nf4_fixed_three_rows_artifacts.py).

A 64-token cycle trace on HumanEval/114 exercised the fixed-three-row kernel through **60 cycles**. All 64 emitted token prefixes and committed KV states matched B1. The trace contained 14 rejection cycles (13 rejected at the first draft token, one after accepting one of two candidates), one full-accept cycle, and 45 cycles with no draft. For all **59 adjacent cycle transitions**, `next_pending` equaled the following cycle's `pending`. The full-accept cycle emitted the pending token plus both accepted candidates and carried the next target token into the following cycle; the partial-reject cycle emitted the accepted prefix and used a correction token before continuing. This gives direct finite evidence for rejected suffix handling, prefix commit, and pending-token handoff under the new schedule. It does not directly compare temporary KV tensors before rollback and is one prompt/process only. See the [state-machine trace summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/state_machine_trace_summary.json) and [full trace artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/state_machine_humaneval114_64.json).

A direct cache transaction audit instrumented each three-row model forward and every `TargetKVCache.crop` on the same 64-token HumanEval/114 trace. Across 29 M=3 verification calls, the pre-existing canonical KV prefix hash was identical before and after each forward, and every layer length remained aligned. There were 30 crops; all 30 resulting active prefixes matched the singleton reference and all layers had the same post-crop length. Of 27 crops tied to a three-row verification, 25 discarded two speculative rows and two discarded one. The remaining three crops were terminal/budget cleanup without a matching M=3 verification context. This directly checks logical rollback and committed state. The cache uses a shared preallocated backing buffer, so bytes beyond the cropped logical length can remain stale; those rows are not part of valid canonical KV. The audit does not compare rejected-suffix bytes against a separate temporary cache, and hooked timings are not performance evidence. See the [cache rollback summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/cache_rollback_summary.json) and [per-call hashes/crop events](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/cache_rollback_humaneval114_64_v2.json).


An output-budget boundary audit covered seven development runs: HumanEval/114 at budgets 1–4 and HumanEval/112 at budgets 2–4. All **7/7** matched the experimental K=2/R=3 singleton tokens and all **19/19** shared state prefixes (one per generated token). At `/114`, budget 1 ended on the precomputed pending token; budgets 2–4 exercised proposal verification and rejected the first proposal before continuing through correction/pending handoff. At `/112`, no draft proposals were present in the observed cycles, so these cases cover the pending-token path rather than accepted drafts. This audit therefore checks cap accounting and selected reject/no-draft boundary paths; accepted non-EOS proposals at the cap are not covered here. Each case used one process and one timing round, so no performance claim is made. Exactness remains relative to the finite experimental B1 singleton reference; original BNB equivalence is not claimed, and GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [budget boundary summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/output_budget_boundary_summary.json), [run manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/budget_boundary_experiment_manifest.json), and per-budget artifacts in the same directory.

The boundary audit was extended to the known full-accept cycle on HumanEval/114, session 11. The selected cycle starts after 44 emitted tokens and has pending token 284 followed by non-EOS drafts 220 and 15. With cap **46**, the runner limits the final proposal to one token, accepts it, emits `[284, 220]`, and finishes at 46/46 tokens with all states matching B1. With cap **47**, it verifies both proposals, accepts 2/2, emits `[284, 220, 15]`, and stops exactly at the cap; all 47/47 state prefixes match B1. These neighboring runs cover accepted non-EOS proposals at and immediately before the output cap for this trace. They remain one prompt/session, finite evidence relative to the experimental singleton, and are not a universal proof or BNB equivalence claim. Each used one timing round, so no performance conclusion is drawn. The updated [boundary summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/output_budget_boundary_summary.json) now includes the later cross-prompt cases as well.

The accepted-proposal output-cap check was replicated on an independent development prompt, HumanEval/112, session 22. A 64-token scouting trace matched B1 at 64/64 states and contained 19 full-accept cycles. Its first full-accept cycle ended at generated token 9. At cap **8**, one available non-EOS proposal (`697`) was accepted and the output stopped at 8/8 matching states. At cap **9**, both proposals (`697`, `2038`) were accepted, the decoder emitted the exact three-token cycle suffix `[671, 697, 2038]`, and stopped at 9/9 matching states. Combined with the HumanEval/114 cap=46/47 pair, this gives neighboring accepted-proposal boundary evidence on two development prompts. It remains finite, prompt-specific empirical evidence relative to experimental B1; no BNB equivalence or performance claim is made. The [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/output_budget_boundary_summary.json) now records **11 cases and 129/129 matched state prefixes**.


A three-cap audit on HumanEval/114, session 11, exercised the partial-accept/reject and correction-token handoff near the output limit. At cap **52**, the final verify window had one proposal, which was accepted and emitted after the pending token; the output matched B1 at 52/52 prefixes. At cap **53**, the final cycle contained proposals `[284, 2224]`: the first was accepted, the second rejected, and correction token `1308` became pending but was not emitted before the cap; 53/53 prefixes matched B1. At cap **54**, the same partial reject occurred and the following no-draft cycle emitted correction token `1308`; 54/54 prefixes matched B1. This directly exercises the correction handoff at the cap for this one development trace. It is finite B1-reference evidence, not a universal proof, BNB equivalence, or performance result. See the [boundary summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/output_budget_boundary_summary.json) and the three cap artifacts in that directory. The summary now contains 14 cases and 288/288 matched state prefixes.


The partial-reject output-cap cases at 53 and 54 tokens were repeated with direct KV transaction hooks. In each run, all **27/27** three-row verification calls preserved the previously committed canonical KV prefix, layer lengths stayed uniform, and all **26/26** crop results matched the B1 singleton prefix. The cap-53 trace ends after accepting the first proposal and rejecting the second, with correction token `1308` pending; cap 54 then emits that token in the following no-draft cycle. The audit confirms prefix preservation and rollback at these boundaries. It compares active logical KV only; stale bytes beyond cropped lengths may remain in the shared backing buffer. This is finite experimental B1 evidence for one prompt/session, not BNB equivalence or a performance result. See the [cache-boundary summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/cache_boundary_summary.json), [cap 53 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/cache_boundary_humaneval114_53.json), and [cap 54 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/cache_boundary_humaneval114_54.json).


A static fail-closed audit tightened the Qwen2 experimental capability boundary. The registered adapter now admits only `Qwen/Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct` at revision `aa8e72537993ba99e69dfaafa59ed015b17504d1`; its capability report separately states that an exactness claim is not allowed because formal operator and whole-sequence proofs are absent. The Qwen2 adapter entrypoint now repeats the capability check so direct adapter callers cannot bypass the model/revision gate. Under a Triton NF4 strategy, an unexpected unquantized projection now raises instead of silently using its original forward; the known `lm_head` path remains explicit. This is a static code change only and has not been rerun through GPU decoding, so existing experiment results remain tied to their recorded pre-change source hashes.


## Dispatch correction and current fixed-three-row evidence (2026-10-30)

A source audit found that the earlier closed-loop “fixed-three-row” wrapper monkeypatch did not replace the adapter’s quantized-linear branch. Consequently, prior closed-loop, cache, EOS, state-machine, and output-budget artifacts from that wrapper path must **not** be counted as evidence for the fixed-three-row NF4 dispatch. The prior operator-only fixed-three-row audits remain separate evidence. This correction supersedes earlier labels and summaries that attributed those runs to the three-row kernel; the historical files are retained unchanged.

The corrected dispatch is now selected through `quantized_linear_strategy=triton_nf4_fixed_three_rows_group_scale_packed`; its actual closed-loop runs record that strategy in their parameters. On the two development prompts HumanEval/112 and /114 at 128 generated tokens (sessions 32/34 and 33/35), all **4/4 sessions** matched B1 tokens and all **512/512 state prefixes**. Across six paired timing rounds, descriptive geometric-mean speedup was **1.750x vs B1 singleton** and **1.569x vs original BNB**; individual round ranges were 1.222x–2.593x and 1.046x–2.295x, respectively. Original BNB token IDs matched in **3/6** timing rounds. Unique-session proposal acceptance was **218/312 (69.9%)**. Prompt-level estimates were 2.473x/2.231x for /112 and 1.239x/1.103x for /114 (B1/BNB). These timing rounds are nested within only two prompts and one GPU; this is an initial development signal, not a general performance claim or a confidence interval over prompts.

A corrected direct cache transaction audit on HumanEval/114, session 11, at 64 generated tokens used the actual fixed-three-row strategy. All **29/29** three-row verification calls preserved the pre-existing canonical KV prefix and kept layer lengths uniform; all **30/30** crop events produced active prefixes matching B1 and uniform layer lengths. This covers real reject rollback in that trace, while stale bytes beyond logical cropped lengths remain outside the comparison. The audit is finite and hooked; its timing is not performance evidence. See [actual_fixed_three_rows_cache_rollback_humaneval114_64_r2.json](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_cache_rollback_humaneval114_64_r2.json).

The corrected 128-token cohort now includes four development prompts (HumanEval/112–115), two independent sessions each, and **8/8** B1 token/state matches with **1,024/1,024 state prefixes**. Across 14 paired timing rounds, descriptive pooled geomean speedup is **1.481x vs B1 singleton** and **1.330x vs original BNB**; prompt-level B1/BNB geomeans are /112 2.473x/2.231x, /113 1.456x/1.313x, /114 1.239x/1.103x, /115 1.172x/1.051x. BNB token IDs match in **7/14** timing rounds. The rounds are nested within only four prompts on one GPU, so these are development estimates, not workload-general claims. See [the corrected four-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_128_four_prompt_summary.json).

The 256-token repeat now covers two independent processes on each of the four development prompts. All **8/8** runs matched B1 tokens and **2,048/2,048** state prefixes. Across 16 paired rounds, descriptive pooled geomean speedup was **1.709x vs B1 singleton** and **1.534x vs original BNB**; prompt-level B1/BNB estimates were /112 2.751x/2.445x, /113 1.709x/1.526x, /114 1.364x/1.252x, /115 1.332x/1.185x. Original BNB token IDs matched on **4/16** rounds. Unique-session proposal acceptance was **824/1322 (62.3%)**. Timing rounds are nested within two sessions per prompt and are not independent prompt samples. See [the 256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_256_four_prompt_summary.json). This extends empirical B1 state matching to a longer horizon; it does not establish B2 identity or a prompt-general speedup.

The corrected kernel was screened on three CNN/DailyMail development prompts at 128 output tokens, with two independent sessions each. All **6/6** runs matched B1 across **768/768 state prefixes**. Across 12 paired rounds, descriptive geomean speedup was **1.270x vs B1 singleton** and **1.162x vs original BNB**. Prompt-level B1/BNB geomeans were cnndm_003 1.344x/1.226x, cnndm_004 1.093x/0.996x, and cnndm_005 1.395x/1.286x. The block matched BNB tokens in **4/12** rounds. BNB latency was near parity but prompt dependent; this is a three-prompt, one-GPU development result, not a stable cross-task claim. See [the CNN/DailyMail summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_cnndm_128_summary.json).

A token-level comparison of the corrected artifacts locates BNB divergence but does not identify its operator cause. At 128 generated tokens, original BNB matched 6/14 run artifacts; at 256 it matched 2/14. Divergence positions were repeatable across both sessions for prompts that diverged: HumanEval/112 at token index 2 at both budgets, HumanEval/115 at 79 at both budgets, cnndm_003 at 89 and cnndm_004 at 20 at both budgets. HumanEval/114 matched BNB at 128 but diverged at index 226 at 256; cnndm_005 matched at 128 but diverged at index 220 at 256. HumanEval/113 matched BNB through both budgets. These are 0-based generated-token indices. The token-level artifact by itself does not identify the responsible operator or logit margin; a corrected forced-prefix replay below adds that evidence. See [the divergence analysis](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_divergence_analysis.json).

A forced BNB-prefix replay was rerun with the same `fixed_row_sdpa_decode(block_k=32)` attention wrapper and fixed-three-row NF4 linear dispatch used by the closed-loop benchmark. This corrected schedule reproduces the actual token divergence: BNB and fixed-three-row singleton logits/KV first differ after the first forced decode transition (output index 1) on all three 256-token development cases. Greedy argmax first differs at index 2 for HumanEval/112 (BNB/fixed top-1 margins 0.125/0.0) and index 20 for cnndm_004 (0.125/0.0); HumanEval/113 has no argmax divergence through 96 forced tokens despite numerical/KV drift from index 1. This supports a numerical sensitivity explanation for those token divergences. The replay alone does not localize the first differing layer/operator and does not prove B2 equivalence; the separate module trace below narrows the first mismatch. The earlier `actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_replay_margins.json` used an incomplete attention schedule and is superseded; use [the corrected replay artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_replay_margins_corrected.json) and [the pinned replay script](../../scripts/audit_actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_replay.py).

The corrected first-transition module trace now localizes the earliest leaf-level mismatch for HumanEval/112 and cnndm_004 to the input of `model.layers.0.self_attn.o_proj`, i.e. the layer-0 attention context immediately after SDPA. The input is `[1,1,2048]` BF16 and differs by max-absolute **0.001953125** and **0.00390625**, respectively. Earlier leaf-module inputs/outputs in that transition match, so the first observed difference is before `o_proj` and downstream of Q/K/V projection. This points to the BNB SDPA versus custom Triton attention path as the first divergence source on these two traces; it does not show that NF4 linears are universally invariant or prove the cause on other prompts. See [the operator trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_first_decode_operator_trace_numeric.json) and [the trace script](../../scripts/audit_actual_fixed_three_rows_bnb_first_decode.py).

A follow-up captured each SDPA call and compared native BNB with Triton on the same singleton transition. At layer 0, Q/K/V were bitwise identical after expanding the native two-head GQA tensors to 16 heads; on those exact BNB operands, Triton still differed from native SDPA by max-absolute **0.001953125** (HumanEval/112) and **0.00390625** (`cnndm_004`). The first Q/K/V input drift occurs only at layer 1, after the layer-0 context mismatch has propagated. This isolates a shape/schedule-dependent attention-kernel difference in the singleton path for these two prompts and this runtime. The [same-input SDPA artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_sdpa_input_kernel_comparison.json) includes per-layer operand/output metrics; the [audit script](../../scripts/audit_actual_fixed_three_rows_sdpa_inputs.py) pins the comparison protocol.

The native-rowwise SDPA block probe now covers three linear schedules on two development prompts, each with three forced BNB tokens. With fixed-three-row NF4 linears, all **6/6 token argmaxes** matched BNB, but **0/6 logits** were bitwise equal and **0/6 KV prefixes** matched. Layer-0 `input_layernorm` and the `q_proj` input matched, while Q/K/V projection outputs differed (max-absolute Q/K/V differences: HumanEval/112 **0.015625/0.015625/0.001953125**, `cnndm_004` **0.03125/0.03125/0.001953125**). Replacing Q/K/V with original BNB `Linear4bit` made all three layer-0 KV prefixes exact, but `o_proj` output still differed (**0.015625** and **0.00390625**) on identical inputs, and layer 1 was the first KV-mismatching layer. Replacing all attention projections with BNB moved the first mismatch into MLP `gate_proj`; that variant kept layer-0 outputs exact but still had no exact full-state prefixes, and HumanEval/112’s second token argmax changed.

The all-BNB control then used the original BNB `Linear4bit` forward for every quantized linear at block M=3, native BNB-style rowwise SDPA, and rowwise RMSNorm/LM head. It matched the BNB singleton **bitwise for all 6/6 per-prefix logits and 6/6 KV prefixes** across the two prompts and three forced tokens. This teacher-forced result motivated a full speculative-runtime run.

The native-BNB schedule has now run through the K=2/R=3 B1 block decoder on HumanEval/112 at 64 generated tokens in **two independent process sessions**. Both sessions matched all **64/64 tokens**, all **64/64 KV state prefixes**, and logical lengths; neither had a state mismatch. Each accepted **24/40 drafted tokens**. Across six paired timing rounds, the block geometric-mean speedup was **1.317x vs the native-BNB singleton wrapper** (range 1.267–1.373x) and **1.040x vs original BNB greedy** (range 1.016–1.064x). The singleton wrapper itself was **1.267x slower than original BNB** on these paired rounds, so the 1.317x ratio over that wrapper is not the practical headline; the measured margin over original BNB is about 4%. Timings cover one prompt on one GPU, with rounds nested within two sessions; this does not establish prompt-level generalization or a stable positive margin over BNB. All quantized linear projections still use BNB, so this validates an exact BNB-compatible block schedule but not the custom Triton NF4 contribution or its speedup. See the [two-session summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_64_multisession_summary.json), [session 0](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_64_s0_statefull.json), [session 1](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_64_s1_statefull.json), the [teacher-forced all-BNB trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_native_rowwise_sdpa_block_all_bnb_linears.json), and the [closed-loop runner](../../scripts/audit_native_bnb_rowwise_b1_closed_loop.py). The same schedule was run on `cnndm_004` at 64 tokens in two independent sessions. Both matched **64/64 token IDs and 64/64 full KV prefixes**, with exact logical lengths and **13/38 (34.2%)** accepted drafts. Performance was consistently worse than original BNB: across six paired rounds, BNB/block latency geomean was **0.850x** (range **0.831–0.881x**), about 18% slower. One of six native-singleton timing samples was a **12.064s outlier** (>2x its session median), flagged in the summary; excluding it gives a 1.059x block/singleton geomean, which still does not change the comparison against original BNB. See the [two-session summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm004_64_multisession_summary.json), [session 0](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm004_64_s0_statefull.json), and [session 1](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm004_64_s1_statefull.json). These two prompts establish finite state identity for the native-BNB block schedule, while the performance comparison shows no workload-general speedup and no custom Triton NF4 benefit.

The 256-token repeat on HumanEval/112 adds two independent process sessions (sessions 2/3), both with **256/256 token matches**, **256/256 exact KV prefixes**, equal logical lengths, and **137/193 (71.0%)** accepted draft tokens. Across six paired timing rounds, native-BNB block speedup was **1.547x vs its singleton wrapper** (range 1.472–1.620x) and **1.240x vs original BNB greedy** (range 1.197–1.282x). The longer run therefore preserves exactness and shows a larger margin on this high-acceptance prompt, but it remains one prompt on one GPU; it does not resolve the `cnndm_004` slowdown or establish workload-general performance. This schedule still uses BNB for all quantized linears and is separate from the custom Triton kernel result. See the [256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_256_multisession_summary.json), [session 2](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_256_s2_statefull.json), and [session 3](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval112_256_s3_statefull.json).

The same 256-token native-BNB schedule was repeated on CNN/DailyMail `cnndm_005` in two independent sessions (sessions 4/5). Both matched **256/256 tokens**, all **256/256 KV prefixes**, and logical lengths; each accepted **65/118 (55.1%)** drafted tokens. Across six paired rounds, block speedup was **1.203x vs the native-BNB singleton wrapper** (range 1.175–1.243x), but only **0.943x vs original BNB greedy** (range 0.918–0.979x), so the block was consistently about 6% slower than the practical baseline. Alongside HumanEval/112 at 71.0% acceptance and 1.240x vs BNB, this points to acceptance and task-specific overhead as likely performance factors, but two prompt clusters are too few to establish a causal relation. It also confirms the central tradeoff: native BNB keeps finite B2 identity, while its speed benefit does not transfer uniformly across prompts. See the [CNN/DailyMail summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm005_256_multisession_summary.json), [session 4](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm005_256_s4_statefull.json), and [session 5](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm005_256_s5_statefull.json).

A diagnostic 32-token PyTorch profile was captured on both prompts with native BNB linears and rowwise SDPA. HumanEval/112 accepted 11/20 drafts across 20 cycles; `cnndm_005` accepted 7/18 across 24 cycles. In each trace the underlying `gemm_4bit_simt<half,3>` kernel ran **252 times per target forward**, with about **14.8 ms of summed CUDA event time per forward**. The rowwise full-vocabulary LM-head GEMM was also a prominent event group (about 7.31 ms per target forward on `/112` and 5.99 ms on `/005`). These figures identify native 4-bit linear execution and LM head as optimization targets, but kernel and wrapper CUDA events are nested/overlapping and profiler instrumentation perturbs execution; the values must not be summed into a latency decomposition or used as benchmark results. The separate 256-token paired timings remain the performance evidence. See the [profile comparison](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/comparison_32.json).

A direct same-input comparison then captured `gate_proj` and `up_proj` activations from a three-row native-BNB teacher-forced block across all 36 layers, on HumanEval/112 and `cnndm_005`. Across **144 module/prompt cases**, BNB M=3 matched its own rowwise M=1 calls and Triton M=3 matched its own M=1 calls in every case, but **0/144 BNB outputs were bitwise equal to Triton**. The first three full-block greedy predictions still matched the forced BNB continuation on both prompts; this limited argmax agreement does not imply equal logits or KV state. On these actual BF16 activation tensors, Triton median direct-call latency was about **0.891× BNB for `gate_proj`** and **0.891× for `up_proj`** (roughly 11% lower), with timing variation across modules. This is an operator-level performance opportunity that currently fails the B2 output contract, so it is not eligible for BNB-exact integration. See the [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/actual_gate_up_bnb_triton_summary.json), [HumanEval/112 artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/actual_gate_up_humaneval112.json), [CNN/DailyMail artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/actual_gate_up_cnndm005.json), and [pinned audit script](../../scripts/audit_actual_activation_nf4_bnb_vs_triton.py).

A sampled dequantization audit checked **65,536 packed weight values per matrix** for layer-0 q_proj, gate_proj, up_proj, and down_proj. Reconstructing NF4 codebook × nested scale and casting to the stored `quant_state.dtype` matched `bitsandbytes.functional.dequantize_4bit` bitwise for all four samples. The pre-cast FP32 reconstruction differed by at most 0.00086, consistent with the final BF16 rounding boundary. This excludes a mismatch with the standalone BNB dequantization utility on these samples, but it does not prove that the fused `gemm_4bit` kernel uses the same intermediate rounding. The BNB/Triton output difference therefore remains unlocalized within fused GEMM arithmetic and scheduling; reduction order is a candidate explanation, not yet a measured cause. See the [dequantization audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_nf4_dequant_values.json) and [audit script](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_dequant_values.py).

To probe that remaining gap, BNB fused `gemm_4bit` was compared with dense `F.linear` using the same actual activations at gate/up in layers 0, 17, and 35. Dense weights were built both from the public dequantizer and from direct FP32 NF4 code/scale reconstruction rounded once to BNB's FP16 compute dtype. BNB and direct dense outputs were **not bitwise equal in any of the six cases** (max absolute difference 0.0039–0.03125); Triton was much closer to that dense result by mean absolute difference (2.9e-7–4.0e-6), but also not bitwise equal. Rounding both code and scale operands to FP16 before multiplying reduced BNB-vs-dense mean error, yet remained non-exact in all six cases. This supports a BNB fused-GEMM-specific arithmetic difference; the experiment does **not** separate internal dequant rounding from accumulation/reduction order because dense `F.linear` uses a different GEMM implementation. Median direct timings were 0.125 ms for BNB, 0.249 ms for dense `F.linear`, and 0.112 ms for Triton, excluding dequantization. These are operator timings only. See the [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_dense_humaneval112_summary.json), [raw comparison](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_dense_humaneval112_layers_0_17_35_v3.json), and [audit script](../../scripts/audit_actual_bnb_nf4_gemm_vs_dense.py).

A follow-up reduction screen used actual layer-0 `gate_proj` activations for the same three forced BNB rows. With direct NF4 code/scale reconstruction rounded to FP16, simple full-K FP32 summation matched **669/768 outputs (87.1%)** at 256 output channels and **2,654/3,072 (86.4%)** at 1,024 channels. Public BNB dequantization through BF16 then FP16 matched only **499/768 (65.0%)** and **2,000/3,072 (65.1%)**, respectively. FP32 tile sums and a strided-lane FP32 reduction at 256 channels had the same exact count as full-K FP32; FP16 partial accumulation reached 672/768 at 256 but fell to 2,643/3,072 at 1,024. None of these candidates reproduces BNB's remaining outputs, so this screen favors direct FP16 weight conversion over a public BF16 intermediate but does not identify the fused kernel's exact accumulation order. See the [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_screen_summary.json), [256-channel artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_humaneval112_o256_v2.json), [1,024-channel artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_humaneval112_o1024.json), and [audit script](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_gate_reduction_orders.py).

Next, profile proposal/verification cost on a workload with better acceptance and replace one NF4 projection group at a time with Triton; retain the full token/state checks and paired comparison to original BNB. Do not generalize the positive HumanEval timing to other workloads.

The boundary/EOS/rollback summary is [actual_fixed_three_rows_boundary_eos_summary.json](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_boundary_eos_summary.json). The machine-readable summary is [actual_fixed_three_rows_128_two_prompt_summary.json](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_128_two_prompt_summary.json). The corrected boundary suite now covers seven caps: HumanEval/112 at 8/9 and HumanEval/114 at 46/47/52/53/54. All **7/7** runs matched B1 tokens and **269/269** state prefixes. The traces show accepted proposals at caps 8, 9, 46, 47, and 52; a partial accept then reject at 53; and emission of its correction token at 54. The two constructed EOS fixtures were rerun under actual fixed-three-row dispatch: accepted EOS matched 2/2 state prefixes, and rejected EOS matched 3/3 while continuing decode. Together with the direct rollback audit (29/29 verification prefixes preserved; 30/30 crop prefixes matched), these are finite branch-level checks, not a proof over every state transition. The held-out GSM8K cohort remains sealed.


The CNN/DailyMail 256-token screen now has two independent sessions for each of three prompts. All **6/6** runs matched B1 across **1,536/1,536 state prefixes**. Across 12 paired rounds, descriptive geomean speedup is **1.213x vs B1 singleton** and **1.075x vs original BNB**. Prompt-level B1/BNB geomeans were cnndm_003 1.238x/1.109x, cnndm_004 1.090x/0.957x, and cnndm_005 1.322x/1.171x. BNB token IDs matched in **0/12** rounds. The first /003 round in session 43 is an outlier (0.86x vs B1; 0.73x vs BNB) and used block-first method order; the other two rounds of session 43 were faster. The /004 block remained slower than BNB across all four paired rounds. Timing rounds are nested within two sessions per prompt, so these results are descriptive and prompt-specific. See [the 256-token CNN/DailyMail summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/actual_fixed_three_rows_cnndm_256_summary.json).

An SM89 SASS inspection of the actual `gemm_4bit_simt<__half,3>` template found packed `HMUL2` products, conversion of half lanes to FP32, `FADD.FTZ` accumulation, and warp `SHFL.DOWN` reductions before FP16 output conversion. The M=1 and M=3 templates are distinct compiled schedules. This motivated a new direct-dequant candidate that rounds each product to FP16 before FP32 accumulation: it matched **677/768 (88.2%)** outputs at 256 channels and **2,679/3,072 (87.2%)** at 1,024 channels, up from 669/768 and 2,654/3,072 for the previous FP32-product sum. The change is modest and remains non-exact, so it localizes an important rounding boundary but does not reconstruct BNB's accumulation order. The static SASS and one-prompt finite probe are configuration-specific. See the [SASS audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_sm89_sass_audit.json), [updated summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_screen_summary.json), [256-channel result](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_humaneval112_o256_v3.json), and [1,024-channel result](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_humaneval112_o1024_v2.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A follow-up lane-strided Triton screen rounded each product to FP16 before FP32 accumulation and varied lane count across 16, 32, 64, and 128. Every candidate still matched **677/768** outputs, the same as the simple FP16-product/full-K sum. This means the currently tested lane count and reduction choices do not explain the residual BNB values; a faithful reconstruction likely needs the kernel's actual assignment of unpacked NF4 values, vectorized half2 operations, and per-lane partial-sum sequence. This remains a single activation/prompt localization result. The candidate rows are recorded in the [256-channel v4 artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_humaneval112_o256_v4.json) and the [updated reduction summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/gate_reduction_screen_summary.json).

The remaining NF4 linear mismatch is now explained by the CUDA SIMT arithmetic in the official bitsandbytes **0.50.0** source. For the FP16 path, the LUT centroid is cast to FP16, the nested scale is separately rounded to FP16, and their half2 product is rounded to FP16; each activation-weight half2 product is then rounded before its pair is accumulated in FP32, followed by the documented 16/8/4/2/1 warp shuffle tree. The same source dispatches M<=3 to SIMT and compiles M_BLOCK=M for M=1..8 ([SIMT kernel](https://github.com/bitsandbytes-foundation/bitsandbytes/blob/0.50.0/csrc/gemm_4bit_simt.cu), [dispatch](https://github.com/bitsandbytes-foundation/bitsandbytes/blob/0.50.0/csrc/gemm_4bit.cu)).

A Triton operator audit reproducing that sequence matched **3,096,576/3,096,576** outputs from BNB M=3 and the rowwise BNB M=1 calls for every one of the **252 Linear4bit modules** on each of three development prompts: HumanEval/112, HumanEval/114, and CNN/DailyMail/005. Across the three prompt runs, both comparisons were exact for **9,289,728/9,289,728** output elements. This resolves the prior linear arithmetic discrepancy for the pinned model, bitsandbytes 0.50.0, FP16 compute path, and SM89 test configuration. It is finite operator evidence; the audit materializes FP16 weights, provides no performance result, and does not prove the remaining model operators or all-input behavior. The immediate next step is a fused NF4 Triton kernel implementing this staged rounding without materializing the weights, then test its operator parity and latency. See the [full operator summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_simt_full_operator_audit_summary.json), [HumanEval/112 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_simt_all_linear4bit_humaneval112_m1check.json), [HumanEval/114 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_simt_all_linear4bit_humaneval114_m1check.json), and [CNN/DailyMail/005 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_simt_all_linear4bit_cnndm005_m1check.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The first fused NF4 Triton kernel now reads packed uint32 weights directly, reproduces the nested-scale FP16 rounding and half-product accumulation, and holds all three row accumulators without padding a fourth row. It was bitwise exact on three layer-0 projections for HumanEval/112: gate_proj (3x2048→11008), down_proj (3x11008→2048, including the partial final 1024-K group), and q_proj (3x2048→2048), totaling **45,312/45,312** output elements. Best single-operator timings were 0.182 ms vs BNB 0.152 ms for gate_proj, 0.159 ms vs 0.140 ms for down_proj, and 0.0428 ms vs 0.0427 ms for q_proj. These are diagnostic measurements from one process, not paired confidence intervals. The exact fused implementation remains slower on the two large projections and roughly tied on q_proj; it is not ready for integration. A warp-shuffle lookup variant was rejected because only its one-warp layout was exact; faster multi-warp layouts produced severe mismatches. See the [prototype summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_triton_prototype_summary.json), [fused kernel prototype](../../scripts/prototype_bnb_nf4_simt_fused_triton.py), and per-projection artifacts. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Further lookup tuning did not produce a speed win. Repeating the cached-LUT gate run gave 0.181 ms vs BNB 0.148 ms; the best exact configs remained slower on gate/down and approximately tied on q_proj. Using hardware `%laneid` for warp-shuffle lookup was bitwise exact only when output tile and warp count aligned in the tested layouts, and those exact variants were still slower (gate 0.189 ms vs 0.147 ms; q_proj 0.0436 ms vs 0.0411 ms). Other layouts were incorrect. Loading the LUT once and indexing it with `tl.gather` hit a Triton 3.6.0 `isWarpLocal()` compiler assertion before execution. These experiments support keeping native BNB SIMT M=3 as the B2 quantized-linear path and directing optimization effort toward verification/runtime/state unless a new lookup layout proves both exact and faster. See the [updated fused prototype summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_triton_prototype_summary.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A new native-BNB B2 closed-loop development audit on HumanEval/114 ran 128-token generations in two independent processes (sessions 6 and 7). In both, block verification matched the native-BNB singleton for all **128/128 tokens**, all **128/128 committed KV state prefixes**, the full prefix domain, and logical lengths; each trace also exercised 26 reject cycles. Both sessions accepted 28 of 73 drafted tokens. Across six paired timing rounds, block verification was **1.095x** the instrumented native-BNB singleton, but only **0.882x** the original BNB greedy path (slower); per-round speedup ranges were 1.085–1.111x and 0.846–0.936x respectively. This supports finite exactness through reject/commit on this prompt and runtime, while the latency result confirms that the runtime remains slower than the actual BNB baseline. The two sessions are nested on one prompt and one RTX 4050, so they do not establish prompt-level generalization or a confidence interval. See the [two-session aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval114_128_multisession_summary.json), [session 6](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval114_128_s6_statefull.json), and [session 7](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval114_128_s7_statefull.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A second native-BNB B2 closed-loop development audit on HumanEval/113 also ran 128-token generations in two independent processes (sessions 8 and 9). Both matched **128/128 tokens**, **128/128 committed KV state prefixes**, the full prefix domain, and logical lengths; each exercised 16 reject cycles and accepted 40 of 68 drafted tokens. Across six paired rounds, block verification was **1.235x** the instrumented singleton and **0.994x** original BNB greedy, with per-round speedup ranges 1.188–1.265x and 0.965–1.023x respectively. Thus this prompt is approximately at BNB break-even, with individual paired rounds on either side of parity; it does not yet establish a stable end-to-end win. Together with HumanEval/114, two development prompts now show finite state/token identity, while the BNB-relative latency varies materially by prompt. These remain two prompts on one RTX 4050 and do not support cross-prompt statistical generalization. See the [two-session aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval113_128_multisession_summary.json), [session 8](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval113_128_s8_statefull.json), and [session 9](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_humaneval113_128_s9_statefull.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A CNN/DailyMail development prompt (`cnndm_003`) was then audited at the same 128-token budget in two independent processes (sessions 10 and 11). Both matched **128/128 tokens**, **128/128 committed KV state prefixes**, the full prefix domain, and logical lengths; each exercised 17 reject cycles and accepted 39 of 69 drafted tokens. Across six paired rounds, block verification was **1.183x** the instrumented singleton but **0.939x** original BNB greedy (slower in every paired round; range 0.895–0.987x). Exactness therefore held on a third development prompt across a different task type, but the speed target remains unmet against original BNB. The paired rounds are nested within two sessions on one prompt and one RTX 4050; they are not an independent-prompt confidence interval. See the [two-session aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm003_128_multisession_summary.json), [session 10](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm003_128_s10_statefull.json), and [session 11](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_rowwise_cnndm003_128_s11_statefull.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

To locate the remaining B2 overhead, a 24-token PyTorch profile was captured on `cnndm_003` with the native-BNB K=2/R=3 path. Verification took 1,247 ms of the 1,395 ms instrumented run (20 cycles, 32 target rows); the profile's largest CUDA entries included 297 ms in `gemm_4bit_simt<half,3>` and 116 ms in 32 single-row vocabulary `aten::mm` calls. Flash SDPA kernels totaled about 5.6 ms in this profiler view. Cache commit/rollback from uninstrumented traces has a median around 0.04–0.05 ms per cycle, so changing the state bookkeeping is not a material lever. The profiler warned that events were cleared during capture; its totals are diagnostic and must not be summed as mutually exclusive kernel cost. See the [profile summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/cnndm003_24/profile.json).

A native-BNB K=1/R=2 repeat on `cnndm_003` matched all 128 tokens, all 128 state prefixes, and logical lengths in both sessions. It was 1.150x faster than its singleton but only **0.925x** original BNB greedy across six paired rounds. The prior K=2/R=3 result on the same prompt was 1.183x/0.939x (block/singleton and block/BNB). K=1 therefore did not improve the BNB-relative result in this matched prompt screen; retain K=2/R=3 as the current B2 schedule. See the [K=1/R=2 manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/native_bnb_k1_r2_manifest.json), [aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_k1_r2_cnndm003_128_summary.json), [session 12](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_k1_r2_cnndm003_128_s12.json), and [session 13](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_k1_r2_cnndm003_128_s13.json). These paired rounds are nested within one prompt/GPU and are descriptive only.

An opt-in prototype then bypassed the rowwise adapter for the unquantized LM head on block verification, issuing one native Torch Linear call for the real rows while keeping singleton/reference calls unchanged. This remained token/state exact on `cnndm_003` and HumanEval/113 in two process sessions per prompt (all **512/512** block state prefixes matched their singleton references, with matching token sequences and logical lengths). Six paired rounds gave block/BNB ratios of **0.966x** on `cnndm_003` and **0.988x** on HumanEval/113, both below 1.0 in geometric mean; the original rowwise path was 0.939x and 0.994x on those prompts. The relative change is small and inconsistent across prompts, so this prototype is not integrated. See the [source-pinned experimental runner](../../scripts/audit_native_bnb_rowwise_batched_lm_head.py), [cnndm_003 aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_batched_lmhead_cnndm003_128_summary.json), and [HumanEval/113 aggregate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_batched_lmhead_humaneval113_128_summary.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A targeted 10-round BNB Linear4bit shape microbenchmark on `cnndm_003` captured real singleton decode activations and repeated them across M=1/2/3/5. At M=3, layer-0 q_proj cost **0.98x** its M=1 call, while gate_proj/up_proj cost **1.76x/1.79x** and down_proj **1.62x**; the corresponding layer-35 gate/down costs were **1.79x/1.65x**. Every M=3 repeated row was bitwise equal to M=1 for the tested NF4 linears. M=5 was not bitwise invariant and cost roughly 2.9x on these MLP projections. This confirms that exact M=3 execution is achievable in the tested range, but the BNB SIMT schedule makes the MLP projections the expensive part of block verification. Together with the profiler, this puts the next kernel work on speeding up the existing M=3 NF4 MLP schedule while retaining its staged arithmetic and reduction order. The repeated-input screen is diagnostic and does not represent distinct block activations; full-model evidence remains in the operator audit and closed-loop runs. See the [cnndm_003 shape artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/bnb_linear_shape_cnndm003_v1.json) and [benchmark script](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_linear_shape_invariance.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An expanded fused-Triton tile sweep on `cnndm_003` initially appeared to beat BNB gate_proj in one unpaired timing pass, but the across-process result did not reproduce. A new paired operator runner alternated BNB and the fastest exact Triton tile for 30 rounds, eight calls per sample. It measured the scalar fused kernel at a median **0.844x** BNB speed on gate_proj. A half2 variant then replaced six scalar FP16 products with three `mul.rn.f16x2` operations while keeping FP32 pair accumulation and the 16/8/4/2/1 reduction. It was bitwise exact on **33,024/33,024** gate_proj and **6,144/6,144** down_proj outputs, but paired medians were only **0.848x** and **0.876x** BNB, respectively. Thus the false-positive single-pass result was timing drift; the present fused Triton/half2 family remains slower and is not integrated. The data indicates that reproducing arithmetic is insufficient: the implementation must also improve memory layout and instruction scheduling relative to BNB SIMT. See the [scalar gate paired run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_gate_l0_cnndm003_paired_v1.json), [half2 gate run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_half2_gate_l0_cnndm003_v1.json), [half2 down run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_half2_down_l0_cnndm003_v1.json), and [paired benchmark source](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_nf4_fused_half2_paired.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.


### Fused NF4 M=3: explicit BNB-style vector load

A development-only Triton gate projection prototype now issues predicated inline PTX `ld.global.cs.v4.u32` to load each 16-byte packed NF4 vector, followed by FP16x2 products and FP32 accumulation. On layer-0 `gate_proj` for `cnndm_003` (activation `3x2048`, output `3x11008`), all **33,024/33,024 outputs were bitwise equal** to the native BNB M=3 result. SASS confirms one `LDG.E.EF.128` instruction is present, so the explicit load does reproduce the vector width. The full Triton tile still has 133 load instructions and 1,614 SASS instruction lines, compared with 13 load instructions and 1,248 instructions in the audited BNB M=3 template. The remaining generated schedule is therefore much heavier even after the packed weight load is fixed. The same-process interleaved 30-round timing remains slower: median Triton **0.1611 ms** vs BNB **0.1333 ms**, paired geomean BNB/Triton **0.830x** (paired P10–P90: 0.797–0.876). Thus vector-load width alone did not address the prototype's overhead. This remains one operator, one prompt and one GPU; no runtime integration is warranted. Artifact: [paired result](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_half2_gate_l0_cnndm003_v1.json), [SASS](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_half2_gate_l0_cnndm003_v1.sass.txt), and [source](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_nf4_fused_inline_ldcs_half2.py).


### Fused NF4 M=3: vectorized activation loads

The next prototype vector-loads each lane’s 32 contiguous FP16 activation values with four aligned `ld.global.cs.v4.u32` operations per row, while preserving the prior explicit packed-weight load, NF4 decode and FP16x2 multiply/FP32 accumulation. It matched native BNB output bitwise on both `cnndm_003` and the independent development prompt `cnndm_002`: 33,024/33,024 for every `gate_proj` and `up_proj` run, and 6,144/6,144 for every `q_proj` run. Across the two prompts and 90 paired rounds each, median BNB/Triton speedup was **1.37x** for gate and **2.30x** for q; `up_proj` reached **1.38x** on `cnndm_003`. The gate timing was 0.088–0.090 ms Triton vs 0.122–0.124 ms BNB on `cnndm_003`, and 0.0903 vs 0.1236 ms on `cnndm_002`; q was 0.022–0.023 ms Triton vs 0.049–0.056 ms BNB. Bootstrap intervals over paired timing rounds are recorded in the summary artifact. The gate candidate SASS has 49 load instructions, including 13 vector LDG.E.128 and no U16 scalar loads, versus 133 loads for the previous activation-scalar Triton variant; its 1,250 SASS instructions are close to the audited BNB M=3 template’s 1,248. This is the first fused prototype in this branch to show a repeatable single-operator win across multiple projections and development prompts. The q/gate/up measurements above use K=2,048 and layer 0. A separate tail-mask extension for K=11,008 `down_proj` is recorded below. These operator results do not prove whole-model KV/token identity or justify decoder integration. See the [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_paired_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_002 gate](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2_gate_l0_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), [cnndm_002 q](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2_q_l0_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), the [SASS](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2_gate_l0_cnndm003_v1.sass.txt), and [prototype source](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_nf4_fused_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2.py).


### Fused NF4 M=3: partial-K tail handling

A separate extension predicates the four 16-byte activation loads for each lane and masks nested-scale reads when the final 1024-element group is partial. It was checked on layer-0 `down_proj` for `cnndm_003` and layer-35 `down_proj` for `cnndm_002`, both with input shape `3x11008` (K=11,008). Both outputs were bitwise identical to BNB (**6,144/6,144** each). Across 60 paired rounds, combined median BNB/Triton ratio was **1.35x** (bootstrap 95% interval 1.341–1.352); per-case medians were 1.34x and 1.35x. This supports the partial-K address/mask path across two prompts and layers on one GPU. The [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_paired_summary_v1.json), [layer-0 result](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2_down_l0_cnndm003_v1.json), and [layer-35 result](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fused_nf4_inline_ldcs_activationvec_half2_down_l35_cnndm002_s1_v1.json) are source-pinned to the [tail-mask prototype](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_nf4_fused_inline_ldcs_activationvec_tailmask_half2.py). These remain single-operator checks, with no whole-layer or decoder exactness claim.


### Vectorized NF4 operator: all-layer audit

The activation-vectorized kernel was applied to all seven quantized linear projections (`q/k/v/o/gate/up/down`) in all 36 Qwen2.5-3B layers. On a single teacher-forced M=3 block, every module output matched the native BNB M=3 output bitwise for both development prompts: **252/252 modules and 3,096,576/3,096,576 elements per prompt**, or **504/504 modules and 6,193,152/6,193,152 elements total**. This includes the partial-K `down_proj` path. Paired operator timing was measured at layers 0, 18 and 35 for each projection (8 alternating rounds, 4 calls/sample). Across these sampled modules, median BNB/Triton speed ratios were **2.28x q_proj, 2.58x k_proj, 2.63x v_proj, 1.69x o_proj, 1.39x gate_proj, 1.39x up_proj and 1.37x down_proj**. This removes the earlier layer-0-only limitation for operator output identity and shows gains for every sampled projection type. It still checks one M=3 block position per prompt, not sequential singleton equality, committed KV, whole-sequence tokens or end-to-end latency. The [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_all36_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_all36_cnndm003_s1_v1.json), [cnndm_002 audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_all36_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), and [all-layer audit source](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_activationvec_all_layers.py) record the protocol and measurements.


### Vectorized NF4 kernel: integrated M=3 block comparison

All 252 linear modules were replaced by the vectorized Triton kernel for one teacher-forced M=3 forward block, with native BNB used for prefill and attention/normalization/residual operations left on the standard model path. On both `cnndm_003` and `cnndm_002`, the integrated block produced **bitwise-identical logits** to native BNB M=3 (`max_abs_diff=0`) and the SHA-256 digest of the active KV prefix matched exactly across all 36 layers; logical lengths were 265 and 422 respectively. All three block argmax tokens also matched. This lifts the previous evidence from isolated operators to an integrated model block. The reference here is native BNB M=3 block execution, not greedy singleton execution; it is not yet a proof of B2, closed-loop token/state exactness, reject/rollback behavior, or end-to-end performance. See the [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_block_integration_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_block_integration_cnndm003_s1_v1.json), [cnndm_002 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_block_integration_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), and [integration audit source](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_activationvec_block_integration.py).


### Vectorized NF4 backend: BNB singleton-to-block identity

The integrated check was extended to the B2 reference: it greedily generated three tokens one at a time with original BNB, then fed those same three tokens to one M=3 block using Triton for all 252 linears and the fixed-row BNB-rowwise attention contract. On both `cnndm_003` and `cnndm_002`, the three per-position next-logit vectors were bitwise identical to sequential BNB (`max_abs_diff=0`), the next greedy tokens matched, all 36 active KV prefixes had identical SHA-256 digests, and cache lengths matched (265 and 422). This is finite one-cycle B2 evidence for the all-accepted path. It does not cover rejected candidates, suffix rollback, pending/bonus-token transitions, EOS or repeated cycles; it is not yet a general B2 guarantee. See the [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_singleton_identity_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_singleton_identity_cnndm003_s1_v1.json), [cnndm_002 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_singleton_identity_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), and [audit source](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_activationvec_singleton_identity.py).


### Vectorized NF4 backend: multi-cycle rollback and pending-token trace

A three-cycle trace was run against greedy BNB singleton on `cnndm_003` and `cnndm_002`. Each prompt exercised: (1) rejecting an EOS draft at the first candidate and emitting the target replacement as the next pending token; (2) accepting both candidates and emitting the bonus token; and (3) accepting one candidate, rejecting a second EOS draft, and rolling back the suffix. All **6/6 cycle cases** emitted the same target token as singleton, matched the retained next-logit vector bitwise, and matched the singleton active-KV SHA-256 after rollback; every layer length matched. The final state after six processed greedy tokens matched the BNB singleton reference on both prompts. This is stronger finite B2 state-machine evidence than the isolated one-cycle cases, including pending-token reuse across cycles. It does not exercise accepted target EOS/termination or long decode, and it provides no end-to-end speedup claim. See the [two-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_multicycle_summary_v1.json), [cnndm_003 trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_multicycle_cnndm003_s1_v1.json), [cnndm_002 trace](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_multicycle_cnndm002_s1_v1.json), and [audit source](../../scripts/audit_bnb_nf4_activationvec_multicycle.py).


### Activation-vectorized NF4 backend: closed-loop K=2/R=3 performance

The prototype was connected to the B1 K=2/R=3 speculative state machine and measured end to end against BNB singleton and original BNB greedy. All twenty-three process runs matched BNB singleton tokens, every committed active-KV prefix, and cache lengths; all 66 paired rounds also matched original BNB token IDs. Performance is workload dependent; acceptance helps but does not determine the result. `HumanEval/112` (60–65% acceptance), `/116` (88%) and `/119` (67%) had repeated gains over BNB greedy: 1.14–1.28x, 1.26–1.28x, and 1.16–1.21x, respectively. `/120` accepted 66% but was near break-even against BNB (0.953–0.990x) despite 1.07–1.24x vs singleton. The new screens `/121` (7/18, 39%), `/122` (7/29, 24%), `/123` (2/26, 8%) and `/124` (6/18, 33%) were all slower than BNB: **0.825x, 0.871x, 0.813x and 0.891x**, respectively; each was around or slightly faster than singleton. `/117` and `/118` show similar losses. A prompt-cluster analysis of the full 64-token HumanEval screen (14 prompts, 20 process runs) found only **3/14 prompts faster than original BNB**. Prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **0.918x** (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **0.855–0.997x**) versus **1.091x** against B1 singleton. Acceptance correlated positively with both speedup measures (Spearman **0.811** vs BNB, **0.877** vs singleton) in this selected development cohort, while `/120` illustrates that acceptance is not sufficient to predict a BNB win. These intervals describe only these prompt clusters on one GPU. See the [tradeoff analysis](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_humaneval64_tradeoff_v1.json), [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_activationvec_closed_loop_tradeoff.py), and the [full run summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_closed_loop_summary_v1.json). Exactness is relative to the BNB singleton fixed-row schedule; original BNB token matches across this finite run set do not establish state equivalence. This is development evidence on one GPU across fifteen prompt IDs, not a general performance claim. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A cycle-cost decomposition of the 64-token HumanEval traces (14 prompt IDs, 20 process traces) shows that median `verify_s` accounts for **99.42%** of recorded cycle time (range 99.17–99.60%). Draft construction accounts for **0.079%** and cache commit for **0.362%** at the median. The median trace has 44.5 of 57 cycles with no draft proposal. Here `verify_s` includes the target forward on both proposal and no-proposal cycles, so this points to target execution across many decode cycles as the measured cost center; it does not isolate kernel-level attribution or host overhead. Cache rollback and draft construction are therefore poor optimization targets for this prototype. See the [cycle-cost artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_humaneval64_cycle_costs_v1.json) and [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_activationvec_cycle_costs.py).

An experimental fallback variant padded singleton/partial linear inputs with zeros to physical M=3, routed them through the same Triton NF4 kernel used for block verification, then cropped non-real outputs. It matched B1 singleton tokens, committed KV prefixes, and cache lengths on both `HumanEval/116` and `/123`. The three-round `/116` pilot reached 1.507x vs singleton and 1.272x vs BNB; `/123` reached 0.959x and 0.808x. Compared with the existing M=3-block-only runs on those prompts, the differences are small and inconsistent, so this pilot gives no clear incremental benefit from replacing the singleton fallback. Keep it experimental pending same-process A/B and broader exactness checks. See the [variant comparison](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_allrows_variant_comparison_v1.json), [source-pinned variant manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_allrows_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/benchmark_bnb_nf4_activationvec_allrows_closed_loop.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A same-process paired A/B then compared the block-only and all-rows variants directly on `HumanEval/116` and `/123` (64 tokens, four rounds each). Both variants matched the B1 singleton across all 64 tokens and every committed KV prefix on both prompts. Routing singleton calls through Triton M=3 was slightly slower in each same-process comparison: all-rows/block-only timing ratio **0.989x** on `/123` and **0.999x** on `/116` (paired geomean **0.994x**). This supersedes the earlier cross-process comparison for incremental latency: it provides no evidence that extending Triton to singleton fallback helps, so keep the block-only schedule. See the [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_allrows_paired_ab_summary_v1.json) and per-prompt [HumanEval/123](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_allrows_paired_ab_humaneval123_64_s1_v1.json) and [HumanEval/116](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_allrows_paired_ab_humaneval116_64_s1_v1.json) audits. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A same-process activationvec A/B batched the native unquantized LM head across real block rows on `HumanEval/116` and `/123` (64 tokens, four rounds each). The candidate preserved B1 tokens and all committed KV states on both prompts. It was about **1.3% slower** than rowwise on high-acceptance `/116` (28/32 accepted) and effectively tied on low-acceptance `/123` (2/26 accepted). Across the two prompt clusters, the paired geomean batched/rowwise timing ratio was **0.994x**; this is too small and inconsistent to justify integration. These results check token/KV behavior, not full-logit identity. See the [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_batched_lmhead_summary_v1.json), [HumanEval/116 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_batched_lmhead_humaneval116_64_s1_v1.json), [HumanEval/123 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_batched_lmhead_humaneval123_64_s1_v1.json), and [source-pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_batched_lmhead_manifest.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A corrected same-process paired comparison used the proper reference for each schedule: activationvec block against BNB singleton, and fixed-three-row block against its matching custom fixed-three-row singleton. Both block methods matched their own reference tokens and all 63 committed KV prefixes on `HumanEval/116` and `/123` (64 tokens). At `/116` both schedules accepted 28/32 drafts; fixed-three block was about **0.3% faster** than activationvec block, and delivered **1.39x** over its own singleton. At `/123` both accepted 2/26; fixed-three block was about **3.8% slower** than activationvec and **0.92x** its own singleton. Fixed-three singleton diverged from BNB singleton at token index 8 on `/123`, so its valid B1 result is under a distinct reference schedule; token identity there is not BNB identity. The apparent cross-schedule KV mismatches from the earlier audit were expected schedule differences, not failures of fixed-three against its own reference. This two-prompt screen does not show a stable advantage over activationvec. See [HumanEval/116](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_fixedthree_linear_humaneval116_64_contract_paired_v2.json), [HumanEval/123](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_fixedthree_linear_humaneval123_64_contract_paired_v2.json), the [source-pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_fixedthree_linear_manifest.json), and [audit runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_vs_fixedthree_linear_paired.py). The first cross-schedule `/116` timing artifact is retained but explicitly labeled ineligible for exact speedup claims. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The paired comparison was repeated in a second independent process for each of five HumanEval prompt clusters (`/112`, `/116`, `/120`, `/123`, `/124`), with 64 output tokens and four timing rounds per process. All **10/10** processes preserved activationvec tokens/KV versus BNB singleton and fixed-three tokens/KV versus its own fixed-three singleton. Fixed-three matched BNB token IDs in both sessions for **3/5** prompts. Its prompt-cluster geometric mean speedup versus activationvec block was **1.021x**, with descriptive prompt bootstrap 95% interval **0.993–1.044x** (prompt ratios 0.974–1.059x), so the timing evidence still does not establish an advantage. Fixed-three achieved **1.124x** versus its own singleton (interval 0.988–1.272x). Latency-only ratios versus original BNB were **1.031x** for fixed-three and **1.010x** for activationvec, both with intervals spanning 1.0; these are not BNB-exact sequence claims. At low-acceptance `/123` and `/124`, both methods remained slower than BNB and fixed-three also failed to beat its own singleton on the prompt-cluster geomean. The five selected prompts and one GPU limit generalization. See the [two-process paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_fixedthree_paired_summary_v2.json) and per-process artifacts whose hashes are listed there. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An exactness-gated no-proposal fallback now runs native BNB M=1 operators when the n-gram proposer returns no candidates; proposal-bearing cycles still use activationvec M=3. On five selected HumanEval prompts, two independent processes each and four paired rounds per process, all **10/10** runs matched B1 singleton token IDs and every committed KV prefix. The fallback activated on 19–48 cycles per 64-token decode. It improved geometric-mean latency by **1.148x** over activationvec (prompt-cluster bootstrap 95% interval **1.106–1.192x**, every prompt 1.092–1.210x), and reached **1.151x** versus original BNB latency (interval **1.021–1.288x**; prompt range 0.949–1.374x). Both fallback and activationvec matched original BNB token IDs in **40/40** paired rounds; no original-BNB KV identity claim is made. This is the clearest development performance lead so far, but it covers five selected prompts on one GPU; validate it on a broader development cohort and exercise EOS/rollback boundaries before integration. See the [10-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_summary_v1.json), [pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_manifest.json), and [audit runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_no_proposal_fallback.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Controlled branch scripts exercised rollback, EOS and no-proposal fallback on five prompts: `HumanEval/111`, `/114`, `/116`, `/121` and `/123`. Each prompt covered (a) one accepted proposal followed by an EOS rejection and suffix rollback, then fallback; (b) rejection of the first proposal with an EOS suffix, followed by fallback; and (c) accepted terminal EOS. All **15/15** scenarios matched B1 tokens and every observed KV prefix. Each rollback path invoked fallback 9 or 10 times; accepted terminal EOS invoked it zero times. The terminal fixture uses a test-only tokenizer proxy that marks the next reference token as EOS; rejected-EOS cases use the tokenizer's actual EOS ID. This is finite branch evidence under scripted proposals, not a natural EOS frequency study. See the [five-prompt branch summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_branches_summary_v1.json), `/116` [branch artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_branches_humaneval116_v2.json), `/121` [branch artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_branches_humaneval121_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_branches_manifest.json), and [branch audit runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_branches.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The fallback screen was extended to **11 HumanEval prompt clusters** (`/111`–`/124`, selected development prompts), with **two independent processes per prompt**, four paired timing rounds each, and 64 generated tokens. All **22/22** processes and **88/88** timed rounds matched B1 singleton tokens; every committed KV prefix matched. Fallback token IDs also matched original BNB in **88/88** rounds, without establishing BNB KV-state identity. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.180x** over activationvec (bootstrap 95% interval **1.151–1.207x**, prompt range 1.092–1.234x) and **1.069x** over original BNB latency (interval **1.004–1.149x**); **8/11** prompt clusters were faster than BNB, with the remaining three at 0.947–0.969x. This reproduces the fallback benefit across more prompts and processes, but remains single-model/single-GPU development evidence; GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [11-prompt, 22-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_summary_v3.json) and the source-pinned per-process artifacts listed by its SHA-256 map.

The same fallback comparison was then run on three selected CNN/DailyMail prompts (`cnndm_003`–`005`), with two independent processes per prompt, four paired timing rounds per process, and 64 generated tokens. All **6/6** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; all **24/24** timed rounds matched B1 singleton and original-BNB token IDs (activationvec also matched original-BNB IDs in 24/24 rounds). The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.135x** over activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.130–1.143x**, prompt range 1.130–1.143x) and **1.080x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.027–1.164x**, prompt range 1.027–1.164x). The fallback ran on 28–36 cycles per 64-token decode. This cross-workload result supports continuing development, but the interval resamples only three selected prompt clusters on one model and one GPU; it is not a workload-population guarantee, and no original-BNB KV identity claim is made. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [CNN/DailyMail summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_cnndm_summary_v1.json) and its six process artifacts listed by the summary's SHA-256 map.

The CNN/DailyMail extension now covers six selected prompt clusters (`cnndm_001`–`006`), two independent processes per prompt, four paired timing rounds per process and 64 generated tokens. All **12/12** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; fallback and activationvec each matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48** timing rounds. Prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.148x** over activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.136–1.160x**, prompt range 1.130–1.170x) and **1.051x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.018–1.098x**, prompt range 1.000–1.164x); all six prompt-level geomeans exceeded 1.0x, although one individual process was slightly slower than BNB. Fallback ran on 28–41 cycles per 64-token decode. This doubles the CNN/DailyMail prompt clusters and supports the cross-workload signal, while the bootstrap still resamples only six selected prompts from one model and one GPU. No original-BNB KV identity claim is made; GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [six-prompt, 12-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_cnndm_summary_v2.json) and its process artifacts listed by the summary's SHA-256 map.

A longer decode check ran 256 tokens on three CNN/DailyMail prompts (`cnndm_001`, `_004`, `_005`), with two processes and four paired rounds per prompt. All **6/6** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; fallback and activationvec both matched original-BNB token IDs in **24/24** timing rounds. Fallback's prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.160x** over activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.151–1.166x**, prompt range 1.151–1.166x) and **1.123x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.032–1.174x**, prompt range 1.032–1.174x). It used 121–148 no-proposal cycles per 256-token decode. These three prompt clusters show that the measured gain persists in longer decodes, but the sample remains narrow and single-GPU; the 64-token and 256-token cohorts differ, so their latency margins do not isolate output length as a cause. No original-BNB KV identity claim is made, and the GSM8K held-out set remains unused. See the [256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_cnndm_256_summary_v1.json) and six source-hashed process artifacts.

The 256-token check now covers all six selected CNN/DailyMail prompts (`cnndm_001`–`006`), with two independent processes and four paired rounds each. All **12/12** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; fallback and activationvec each matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48** timed rounds. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.149x** over activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.139–1.159x**, prompt range 1.132–1.166x) and **1.123x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.071–1.176x**, prompt range 1.032–1.207x); all 12 process-level BNB latency ratios also exceeded 1.0x. Fallback ran on 112–154 cycles per 256-token decode. This broadens the longer-decode result to six prompt clusters, but remains a selected cohort on one model and one GPU, not a general workload guarantee. Original-BNB KV identity is not claimed; GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [six-prompt, 12-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_cnndm_256_summary_v2.json) and its source-hashed process artifacts.

An independent 256-token development screen on HumanEval prompts /112, /116 and /123 adds another task type, using two processes and four paired rounds per prompt. All **6/6** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; fallback and activationvec both matched original-BNB token IDs in **24/24** rounds. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.063x** over activationvec (descriptive bootstrap 95% interval **1.028–1.127x**, prompt range 1.028–1.127x) and **1.368x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.089–1.606x**, prompt range 1.089–1.606x).
