The fallback's effect over activationvec varied with its invocation count: /112 and /116 used 22 and 19 fallback cycles and were 1.036x and 1.028x faster; /123 used 109 cycles and was 1.127x faster. This suggests fallback's incremental contribution is larger when empty-proposal cycles are common, but three selected prompt clusters do not establish that relationship causally. Results remain single-model/single-GPU development evidence; no original-BNB KV identity claim is made and GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [HumanEval 256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_humaneval_256_summary_v1.json) and its six hashed process artifacts.

The HumanEval 256-token screen now includes six prompt clusters (/112, /114, /116, /121, /123, /124), two processes each and four paired rounds per process. All **12/12** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; fallback and activationvec each matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48** rounds. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.086x** over activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.054–1.118x**, prompt range 1.028–1.127x) and **1.266x** over original-BNB latency (interval **1.130–1.431x**, prompt range 1.077–1.606x); all 12 process-level BNB latency ratios exceeded 1.0x. Fallback ran on 19–116 cycles per decode. The modest 2.8–12.7% incremental gain over activationvec varies by prompt, while B1 state identity held throughout. This is a selected six-prompt cohort on one model and GPU; it supports cross-workload development but does not establish population-level performance or BNB KV-state identity. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [six-prompt HumanEval 256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_no_proposal_fallback_humaneval_256_summary_v2.json) and its twelve source-hashed process artifacts.

A prompt-level exploratory analysis pooled the twelve 256-token clusters from HumanEval and CNN/DailyMail. The number of no-proposal fallback cycles correlated with the fallback's incremental speedup over activationvec (Pearson **r=0.959**, prompt-stratified bootstrap 95% interval **0.796–0.992**; Spearman **rho=0.867**, interval **0.663–0.993**). After centering each workload to remove between-workload mean differences, Pearson **r=0.919** (interval **0.547–0.986**). The per-workload signal differs: HumanEval r=0.975, while CNN/DailyMail r=0.456 with an interval spanning zero (**−0.534–0.985**). This supports investigating fallback frequency as a candidate performance moderator, but the association is descriptive, based on six selected prompts per workload, and does not show causality. See the [prompt-dependence analysis](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_fallback_prompt_dependence_256_v1.json) and [analysis script](../../scripts/analyze_activationvec_fallback_prompt_dependence.py).

A controlled 64-token fraction ablation ran on three development prompts (HumanEval /112, HumanEval /123, and CNN/DailyMail cnndm_003), with two independent processes and four paired timing rounds each. The three schedules used native BNB M=1 on **0%, 50%, or 100%** of empty-proposal cycles. All **6/6** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix under every schedule; all timed rounds remained token-identical to B1. The prompt-cluster geomean was **1.050x** for 50% fallback versus activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.039–1.065x**), **1.105x** for 100% fallback (interval **1.074–1.138x**), and **1.052x** for 100% versus 50% (interval **1.036–1.067x**). The ordering was monotonic on all three prompts: 1.039/1.074x at /112 (19 empty-proposal cycles), 1.065/1.138x at /123 (48 cycles), and 1.046/1.103x at cnndm_003 (36 cycles), for half/full fallback versus activationvec. This same-prompt dose-response is stronger evidence that the native singleton substitution contributes to the measured latency change, but it covers only three prompts, one GPU and 64-token runs; it omits original-BNB comparison and does not establish broader performance. See the [fraction-ablation summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_summary_v1.json), [source-pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_fraction_ablation.py).

The same 0/50/100% ablation was repeated at 256 generated tokens on the same three prompt IDs, again with two processes and four paired timing rounds each. All **6/6** processes remained exact to B1 tokens and every committed KV prefix. The prompt-cluster geomean speedups were **1.028x** for 50% fallback versus activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.014–1.039x**), **1.060x** for 100% fallback (interval **1.024–1.086x**), and **1.032x** for 100% versus 50% (interval **1.009–1.046x**). The full-fallback advantage remained monotonic on every prompt: 1.024x at /112 (22 empty-proposal cycles), 1.071x at /123 (109 cycles), and 1.086x at cnndm_003 (112 cycles). On the same prompt cohort, the aggregate full-fallback ratio was lower at 256 tokens than at 64 tokens (1.060x vs 1.105x), so the incremental gain appears smaller in the longer run; this is descriptive and reflects separate process timing sessions. The result confirms the exact dose-response persists at longer decode, but with a reduced margin and only three prompts on one GPU. This ablation does not compare to original BNB and makes no BNB KV claim. See the [256-token fraction-ablation summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_256_summary_v1.json), [256-token manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_256_manifest.json), and [256-token runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_fraction_ablation_256.py).

The 256-token fraction ablation now includes six prompt clusters, adding HumanEval /114 and /121 and CNN/DailyMail cnndm_004 to the earlier three. Across **12/12** independent processes, all three fallback schedules matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix; all **48** timed rounds per schedule matched B1 tokens. The six-prompt geomean speedup was **1.034x** at 50% fallback versus activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.024–1.042x**), **1.071x** at 100% fallback (interval **1.050–1.089x**), and **1.036x** for full versus half fallback (interval **1.023–1.046x**). Full fallback beat half fallback on all six prompt-cluster geomeans; prompt-level full-fallback ratios ranged from 1.024x to 1.104x. No-proposal counts ranged from 22 to 148 per decode. The dose-response therefore persists across six selected prompts, with a moderate aggregate margin, but remains one model/GPU development evidence and omits original-BNB latency. No BNB KV identity claim is made; GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused. See the [six-prompt 256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_256_summary_v2.json), the source-pinned [256-token manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_256_manifest.json), and the hashed process artifacts referenced by the summary.

A 512-token extension of the 0/50/100% fallback-fraction ablation now covers six development prompt clusters (HumanEval /112, /114, /121, /123 and CNN/DailyMail `cnndm_003`, `cnndm_004`), with two independent processes on /123 and `cnndm_003`, and one process on each other prompt. All **8/8** processes matched B1 singleton tokens across 512 positions and every committed KV prefix; all **32 timing rounds per schedule** remained token-identical. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.048x** for 100% fallback versus activationvec (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.027–1.069x**, prompt range 1.009–1.082x), **1.021x** for 50% fallback (interval **1.003–1.036x**, range 0.981–1.041x), and **1.025x** for 100% versus 50% (interval **1.017–1.032x**); full fallback beat half fallback on all six prompt-cluster means. The low-fallback `/112` prompt was approximately at parity (1.009x full fallback, 22 empty-proposal cycles), while the high-fallback `/114` and `cnndm_004` prompts gained 8.2% and 8.0% (169 and 234 cycles). This supports a modest incremental 512-token benefit across these selected workloads, with four prompt clusters represented by one process only. The bootstrap is descriptive and does not establish general performance. This ablation omits original-BNB latency and makes no BNB KV-state identity claim. The source-pinned [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_512_manifest.json), [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_ablation_512_summary_v1.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_fraction_ablation_512.py) record the protocol and eight process artifacts. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The 512-token fallback schedules were then compared with the original BNB greedy path in the same process, using four paired timing rounds per prompt on the same six-prompt cohort. All **18/18** prompt/mode preflights matched B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix. Each candidate also matched original-BNB token IDs in **24/24** timed comparisons per mode (72/72 combined); this is token identity only and does not establish original-BNB KV identity. The prompt-cluster geomean speedup over original BNB was **1.150x** for activationvec (descriptive 95% prompt-bootstrap interval **1.001–1.318x**, 4/6 prompts faster), **1.197x** for 50% fallback (**1.044–1.365x**, 5/6 faster), and **1.252x** for full fallback (**1.102–1.403x**, 5/6 faster). In these same paired runs, 50% and 100% fallback improved over activationvec by **1.041x** (interval **1.031–1.051x**) and **1.088x** (interval **1.056–1.119x**), respectively; full fallback improved on all six prompt means. The exception against BNB was HumanEval /123, where even full fallback reached only 0.959x; full fallback ranged from 1.030x to 1.555x across prompts. This provides a promising finite end-to-end result and supports fallback as a performance contributor, but the six prompts are selected, the bootstrap is descriptive, and the experiment uses one model/GPU. The paired [comparison manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_bnb_compare_512_manifest.json), [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_bnb_compare_512_summary_v1.json), [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_bnb_compare_512.py), and [summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_fallback_bnb_compare_512.py) preserve the protocol and six process artifacts. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A second independent paired process was collected for each of the same six 512-token prompts (12 processes total, four timing rounds per process). All **12/12** processes again matched B1 singleton tokens and committed KV state; every candidate matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48 rounds per mode**. Combining the two processes per prompt, the geomean speedup over original BNB was **1.148x** for activationvec (prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **0.996–1.322x**, 4/6 prompts faster), **1.196x** for 50% fallback (**1.044–1.359x**, 5/6 faster), and **1.251x** for full fallback (**1.095–1.404x**, 5/6 faster). In same-process pairs, 50% and 100% fallback improved over activationvec by **1.042x** (interval **1.027–1.057x**) and **1.090x** (interval **1.056–1.121x**), and full fallback improved on all six prompt means. The HumanEval /123 exception repeated: full fallback averaged **0.947x** versus BNB, while improving **1.087x** over activationvec. Phase timing points to decode as the issue there: full-fallback decode was about **0.946x** BNB decode, while their prefill times were nearly equal. These two-process results make the measured dose-response more reproducible, but remain selected single-GPU development evidence. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_bnb_compare_512_summary_v2.json), [repeat manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_bnb_compare_512_repeat_manifest.json), [repeat runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_bnb_compare_512_repeat.py), and [repeat summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_fallback_bnb_compare_512_repeats.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A paired 512-token decoder phase breakdown was collected for HumanEval /123 (the repeated slowdown prompt) and /114 (a faster control), with four in-process timing rounds each under original BNB, activationvec, 50% fallback and 100% fallback. All candidate paths remained exact to B1 and matched original-BNB token IDs in all four rounds. Prefill was small for both prompts (about **0.06–0.10 s**); verification dominated candidate decode time. The principal difference was proposal efficiency: /123 drafted 471 tokens, accepted 102 (**21.7%**), and processed **880 target rows** across 409 cycles; /114 drafted 304, accepted 190 (**62.5%**), and processed **625 target rows** across 321 cycles. Full fallback cut /123 verification from **14.25 s** to **12.99 s**, but it did not change acceptance or target-row count, leaving total time at **13.18 s** versus **12.53 s** for original BNB. On /114, full fallback cut verification to **9.87 s** and total time to **9.98 s** versus **12.93 s** BNB. This points to low acceptance and wasted verification rows as the /123 decode bottleneck; changing the singleton fallback schedule alone cannot remove that excess work. These are two selected prompts and four paired rounds, not CUDA-kernel attribution. See the [decomposition summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_bnb_decomposition_512_summary_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_bnb_decomposition_512_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_decomposition_512.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A paired K=1 versus K=2 sweep with full no-proposal fallback was run at 512 tokens on HumanEval /123 and /114 (four rounds in one process per prompt). Both K values matched the B1 singleton tokens and every committed KV prefix on both prompts; timed token IDs matched original BNB in **4/4 rounds per K and prompt**. K=2 was faster than K=1 in both cases: **1.071x** on /123 and **1.186x** on /114. Relative to original BNB, K=1/K=2 achieved **0.912x/0.966x** on /123 and **1.076x/1.291x** on /114. The proposal traces help explain the prompt difference: /123 accepted 79/259 drafts at K=1 (30.5%) and 102/471 at K=2 (21.7%); /114 accepted 142/200 (71.0%) and 190/304 (62.5%), respectively. K=2 had more target rows on both prompts (880 vs 691 on /123; 625 vs 569 on /114) but fewer cycles, and still had lower latency, showing that target-row count alone does not predict runtime. This small sweep favors K=2 for these two prompts; it is not enough to justify adaptive K or a general policy. See the [K-sweep summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_summary_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_k_sweep_512.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The K=1/K=2 full-fallback comparison was extended to the four remaining prompts, giving six prompt clusters total at 512 tokens, one process and four paired rounds per prompt. All **6/6** prompts were exact to B1 tokens and committed KV for both K values; original-BNB token IDs matched in **24/24 rounds per K**. K=2 was faster than K=1 on all six prompts, with a prompt-cluster geomean ratio of **1.189x** (descriptive bootstrap 95% interval **1.119–1.272x**, prompt range 1.069–1.363x). Against BNB, K=2 reached **1.252x** overall (interval **1.105–1.397x**) and was faster on 5/6 prompts; K=1 reached **1.053x** (interval **0.988–1.109x**) and was also faster on 5/6. HumanEval /123 remained the lone prompt where K=2 did not beat BNB (**0.969x**), while it still beat K=1 by **1.069x**. K=2 had lower draft acceptance fractions than K=1 on all six prompts, yet also lower paired latency on all six; the advantage therefore comes from block throughput and fewer decode cycles in this sample, not from a higher per-draft acceptance probability. This favors K=2 for the tested cohort but remains a single-process-per-prompt, single-GPU development result. See the [six-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_summary_v2.json), [expanded manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_expand_manifest.json), and [expanded runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_k_sweep_512_expand.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A second independent K=1/K=2 process was completed for all six 512-token prompts (12 processes total, eight timing rounds per prompt after combining the two runs). All **12/12** processes were exact to B1 singleton tokens and committed KV for both K values; each K matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48 paired rounds**. K=2 beat K=1 on all six prompt means: geomean **1.191x** (descriptive prompt-cluster bootstrap 95% interval **1.119–1.272x**, prompt range 1.068–1.362x). Against BNB, K=2 reached **1.252x** (interval **1.103–1.402x**) and was faster on **5/6** prompts; K=1 reached **1.051x** (interval **0.984–1.107x**) and was also faster on 5/6. The HumanEval /123 case persisted as the only K=2 regression to BNB (**0.966x**), though K=2 remained **1.068x** faster than K=1 there. This confirms a repeatable K=2 advantage for the selected cohort, not universal BNB superiority; results remain single-GPU development measurements with descriptive prompt-level uncertainty. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_summary_v3.json), [repeat manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_512_repeat_manifest.json), and [repeat runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_k_sweep_512_repeat.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A K=2 ngram-size sweep (ngram 2/3/4) was run with full fallback on HumanEval /123 and /114, four paired rounds per prompt. All six prompt/config preflights matched B1 tokens and committed KV; each configuration matched original-BNB token IDs in **8/8** timed rounds across the two prompts. On /123, speedups versus BNB were **0.947x/0.945x/0.949x** for ngram 2/3/4; ngram4 raised acceptance only from 21.7% to 22.3% and reduced target rows from 880 to 874, leaving the BNB slowdown essentially unchanged. On /114, ngram4 was best at **1.321x** (vs 1.305x for ngram3 and 1.186x for ngram2), with acceptance rising to 64.8% and target rows falling to 616. The result supports ngram4 as a possible small tuning win on the high-acceptance prompt, but it does not fix the low-acceptance /123 case; two prompts and one process each are insufficient to justify changing the default. See the [ngram summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_summary_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_ngram_sweep_512.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The K=2 ngram-size sweep now covers all six 512-token prompts, one process and four paired rounds per prompt. All **18/18** prompt/ngram configurations remained exact to B1 tokens and committed KV; each ngram matched original-BNB token IDs in **24/24** timed rounds. Across the six prompt means, ngram4 reached **1.260x** versus BNB (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.100–1.417x**, 5/6 prompts faster), compared with **1.246x** for ngram3 (**1.091–1.395x**) and **1.209x** for ngram2 (**1.070–1.356x**). Ngram4 was **1.011x** faster than ngram3 overall (interval **1.001–1.025x**) and improved on five prompts, with near parity on /112 and `cnndm_004`; the HumanEval /123 slowdown remained essentially unchanged at **0.948x** BNB for all three ngram sizes. This is a small, promising tuning signal for ngram4, not enough to change the default without independent process repeats. See the [six-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_summary_v2.json), [expanded manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_expand_manifest.json), and [expanded runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_ngram_sweep_512_expand.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The independent ngram sweep repeat is complete: six prompts, **12 processes**, and eight paired timing rounds per prompt after combining both processes. All **12/12** processes remained exact to B1 tokens and committed KV; each ngram matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48 rounds**. Aggregated across prompts, ngram4 was **1.264x** versus BNB (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.105–1.427x**, 5/6 prompts faster), ngram3 was **1.248x** (**1.094–1.400x**), and ngram2 was **1.212x** (**1.073–1.364x**). Ngram4's paired improvement over ngram3 was **1.013x** (interval **1.002–1.028x**) on 5/6 prompt means; the largest gain was HumanEval /121, while `cnndm_004` was approximately tied and slightly favored ngram3. HumanEval /123 still lagged BNB at **0.954x** even with ngram4. This repeated six-prompt evidence makes ngram4 a reasonable optimization candidate, but the aggregate gain is small, the cohort is selected, and only one model/GPU is covered, so the default remains ngram3 pending broader validation. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_summary_v3.json), [repeat manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_512_repeat_manifest.json), and [repeat runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_ngram_sweep_512_repeat.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A paired 256-token K=2 ngram-size sweep was run on the same six selected development prompts, with one process and four timing rounds per prompt. All **18/18** prompt/ngram configurations matched B1 singleton tokens and committed KV; each ngram matched original-BNB token IDs in **24/24** timed rounds. Against BNB, prompt-cluster geomean speedups were **1.172x** for ngram2 (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.083–1.294x**), **1.193x** for ngram3 (**1.096–1.319x**), and **1.198x** for ngram4 (**1.103–1.321x**); all six prompt means favored each candidate over BNB. Ngram4 beat ngram3 on four of six prompt means, but its aggregate margin was only **1.004x** (interval **0.997–1.010x**), so the 256-token data do not show a clear ngram4 advantage. Ngram3 remains the default; ngram4 stays an optimization candidate for further independent repeats. Unlike at 512 tokens, HumanEval /123 was faster than BNB at this budget (about 1.08x for all three sizes), showing that the earlier slowdown depends on decode budget or measurement context. These are six selected prompts on one model/GPU, with one process per prompt; bootstrap intervals are descriptive across prompt clusters. See the [256-token summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_256_summary_v1.json), [source-pinned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_256_manifest.json), [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_ngram_sweep_256.py), and [summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_ngram_sweep_256.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An independent second process was collected for each of the same six prompts at 256 tokens (12 processes total; eight paired rounds per prompt after combining processes). All **12/12** processes again matched B1 singleton tokens and committed KV state; each candidate matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48** timed rounds per ngram. Across the six prompt means, speedup versus BNB was **1.165x** for ngram2 (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.073–1.286x**), **1.184x** for ngram3 (**1.083–1.307x**), and **1.187x** for ngram4 (**1.087–1.313x**); all three were faster on all six prompt means. Ngram4 beat ngram3 on five of six means, but only by **1.002x** overall (interval **0.997–1.007x**), with the interval spanning parity. This repeats the weak ngram4 tuning signal without establishing a practically reliable gain, so ngram3 remains the default. At 256 tokens, the HumanEval /123 prompt was faster than BNB on both processes, while `cnndm_004` varied around parity, illustrating workload and process sensitivity. These paired comparisons use two processes per prompt on one model/GPU; bootstrap uncertainty is across six selected prompt clusters and remains descriptive. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_256_summary_v2.json), [repeat manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_ngram_sweep_256_repeat_manifest.json), [repeat runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_ngram_sweep_256_repeat.py), and [repeat summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_ngram_sweep_256_repeats.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The K=1/K=2 comparison was independently repeated across the same six development prompts at 256 tokens (12 processes total, eight paired timing rounds per prompt after combining repeats). All **12/12** processes matched B1 singleton tokens and committed KV for both K values; each candidate matched original-BNB token IDs in **48/48** rounds. Across prompt means, K=2 reached **1.152x** versus BNB (descriptive prompt-cluster bootstrap 95% interval **1.056–1.265x**, faster on 6/6 prompts), while K=1 reached **1.011x** (**0.974–1.050x**, faster on 3/6). K=2 beat K=1 on all six prompt means by **1.139x** overall (**1.084–1.205x**, prompt range 1.045–1.286x). This independently repeats the 256-token K2 advantage and is consistent with the 512-token K sweep, while retaining workload-specific variation in absolute BNB speedup. Results cover one model and GPU; the prompt-cluster bootstrap is descriptive. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_256_summary_v2.json), [repeat manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_256_repeat_manifest.json), [repeat runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_k_sweep_256_repeat.py), and [repeat summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_k_sweep_256_repeats.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A paired budget synthesis joined the two-process K1/K2 summaries at 256 and 512 generated tokens on the same six prompts. K2 remained faster than K1 on all six prompts at both budgets; its geomean advantage was **1.139x** at 256 tokens (prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.084–1.205x**) and **1.191x** at 512 tokens (**1.119–1.272x**). Against original BNB, K2 measured **1.152x** at 256 (**1.056–1.265x**, 6/6 prompts faster) and **1.252x** at 512 (**1.102–1.402x**, 5/6 faster). The paired 256/512 ratio of these speedups was **0.956x** for K2 over K1 (interval **0.935–0.985x**) and **0.920x** for K2 versus BNB (**0.872–0.985x**), with the shorter budget lower on five of six prompts in both comparisons. This is consistent with the block-verification benefit being more pronounced over longer decode runs; the shared six-prompt, single-GPU sample is descriptive and does not establish a universal length policy. See the [budget-effect summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_k_sweep_budget_effect_summary_v1.json) and its reproducible [summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_k_sweep_budget_effect.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A same-process K2/R3 phase decomposition was collected at 256 tokens for HumanEval /123 (the low-acceptance case) and /114 (control), using activationvec, 50% fallback, full fallback, and original BNB. All three candidate schedules matched B1 tokens and committed KV; every schedule matched BNB token IDs in **4/4** paired rounds per prompt. Proposal behavior was unchanged by fallback rate: /123 accepted **63/168 (37.5%)** drafts over 193 cycles and processed **361 target rows**, while /114 accepted **67/144 (46.5%)** over 188 cycles and processed **332 rows**. Verification dominated candidate decode time. Full fallback measured **1.080x** BNB on /123 and **1.136x** on /114; activationvec measured **0.978x** and **0.997x**, respectively. Since acceptance and target-row counts stayed fixed, the gain in these runs came from a lower verify phase cost under fallback, not from fewer verified rows. This is one process and four rounds per prompt; see the [256-token decomposition summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_bnb_decomposition_256_summary_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/fallback_fraction_bnb_decomposition_256_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_activationvec_fallback_decomposition_256.py).

Nsight Systems CUDA traces were then collected for K2/R3 full fallback on the same two prompts at 256 and 512 tokens. All **4/4 captures** passed B1 token/KV preflight and completed the requested output budget. Across the four traces, three kernel families repeatedly accounted for **79–80%** of accumulated GPU kernel time: a BF16 GEMV kernel (31–34%), BNB `gemm_4bit_simt` (17–28%), and `_fused_nf4_m3_kernel` (21–29%). The GEMV instance count exactly matched target-row count in every capture (361/880 on /123 and 332/625 on /114), consistent with rowwise LM-head projection; this mapping is an inference from call counts and the kernel signature. The trace therefore points to LM-head GEMV and the two 4-bit linear paths as the next optimization targets. Nsight timings are instrumented diagnostics and are not latency benchmarks. See the [Nsight profile summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/nsys_k2_profile_summary_v1.json), [capture runner](../../scripts/profile_k2_nsys_capture.py), and [stats summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_k2_nsys_profiles.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A paired LM-head batching ablation was run under K2/R3 with full native-BNB fallback on HumanEval /123 and /114 at 256 and 512 tokens (four prompt-budget strata, four paired timing rounds each). Batched LM head and rowwise LM head both matched B1 tokens and committed KV in **4/4** artifacts; both matched original-BNB token IDs in **16/16** paired rounds. Batching changed latency by a geomean ratio of **0.9984x** relative to rowwise (descriptive stratum-bootstrap 95% interval **0.9969–1.0002x**), and was faster in only **1/4** strata. The largest positive case was /123 at 256 tokens (**1.0013x**); at the other three prompt-budget combinations it was slightly slower. It did not fix the /123 512-token slowdown: both variants remained below BNB (0.959x rowwise, 0.956x batched). Despite Nsight showing one GEMV per target row and about one-third of GPU kernel time in that kernel family, replacing rowwise LM-head calls with one batched Linear call did not yield a repeatable end-to-end gain. Keep the rowwise path; do not integrate this batching prototype. Next optimize and profile the fixed-row NF4 and BNB 4-bit GEMM paths. See the [summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/k2_batched_lmhead_fallback_summary_v1.json), [manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/k2_batched_lmhead_fallback_manifest.json), and [runner](../../scripts/audit_k2_batched_lmhead_fallback.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An M=1 NF4 operator screen compared BNB, the packed two-row kernel with one active row, and an experimental one-row kernel on layer-0 `q_proj` (K=2048) and `down_proj` (K=11008), using seeded random FP16 activations, 24 CUDA-event iterations per sample, and seven alternating rounds. The one-row kernel matched the packed two-row prototype exactly but did **not** match BNB on either projection (max-absolute difference **0.0234375** for q_proj and **0.0166016** for down_proj). It was about **1.11x faster** than BNB on q_proj (23.25 vs 25.78 μs), but **1.73x slower** on down_proj (102.57 vs 59.26 μs). Because it fails the BNB singleton arithmetic contract and loses on the large down projection, it is not suitable as an exact fallback or for runtime integration. Keep native BNB M=1 as the fallback reference; prioritize exact schedule-preserving optimization of the M=3 verification kernels. This is a two-module, one-layer diagnostic on one RTX 4050, not an end-to-end result. See the [operator artifact](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/singleton_nf4_bnb_operator_paired_v1.json) and [runner](../../scripts/benchmark_nf4_singleton_row_kernel.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A source-pinned M=3 launch sweep tested six `(OUT_TILE, num_warps)` configurations on actual layer-0 Qwen activations for `q_proj`, `gate_proj`, and `down_proj` from `cnndm_003` and HumanEval/114. All **36/36** projection/config/prompt outputs were bitwise identical to native BNB M=3. The `(4,4)` launch was consistently faster than the current `(8,8)` launch for the two wide MLP projections: BNB-over-kernel ratios improved from **1.325x to 1.412x** on gate_proj and **1.302x to 1.376x** on down_proj for `cnndm_003`; on HumanEval/114 they improved from **1.325x to 1.406x** and **1.302x to 1.392x**, respectively. q_proj was effectively tied across prompts, so retain `(8,8)` there. This supports trying `(4,4)` only for projections with K or N equal to 11008, while keeping `(8,8)` elsewhere. It is a two-prompt, layer-0 operator result; see the [CNN/DailyMail sweep](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_sweep_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval sweep](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_sweep_humaneval114_v1.json), and [sweep source](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_m3_launch.py).

The shape-specific launch map was then applied in K2/R3 closed-loop decoding with native BNB M=1 on no-proposal cycles. On HumanEval/114 and /123 at 256 generated tokens, both runs matched B1 singleton token IDs and all **256/256 committed KV prefixes**; each also matched original-BNB token IDs in **4/4** paired timing rounds. The full fallback schedule was **1.148x** and **1.097x** faster than original BNB, respectively, and **1.376x** and **1.320x** faster than B1 singleton. The M=3 proposal counts were unchanged by the launch map (67/144 and 63/168 accepted/drafted); native M=1 fallback ran on 116 and 109 no-proposal cycles. A separate 64-token tuned run without fallback on /114 remained exact but was only **0.967x** B1 singleton and **0.809x** original BNB. Thus the current end-to-end gain depends on the fallback schedule; the launch sweep alone is not enough to attribute it. These runs validate exactness and demonstrate useful overall latency on two development prompts, but they do not isolate the marginal end-to-end contribution of `(4,4)` from the previously measured fallback schedule because there is no same-process full-fallback `(8,8)` control. Keep the change experimental until that paired control and broader prompt repeats are complete. See the [HumanEval/114 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tuned_fallback_humaneval114_256_v1.json), [HumanEval/123 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tuned_fallback_humaneval123_256_v1.json), [tuned no-fallback run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tuned_humaneval114_64_v1.json), [source-pinned tuned manifest](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tuned_fallback_manifest_v1.json), and [runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_tuned_fallback.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A same-process paired closed-loop A/B then compared full fallback under the default `(8,8)` launch and the shape-tuned map, with four timing rounds each at 256 tokens. Both modes matched B1 singleton tokens and all **256/256 KV prefixes** on both HumanEval/114 and /123; every method matched the B1 token IDs in **4/4** timed rounds. The tuned/default speed ratio was **0.985x** on /114 and **0.992x** on /123, for a two-prompt geometric mean of **0.989x**: tuned was about **1.1% slower** overall despite the isolated 6% gate/down projection gains. Both modes still beat original BNB (default/tuned **1.120x/1.104x** on /114 and **1.073x/1.064x** on /123). This indicates the 4x4 launch change does not currently improve end-to-end latency; keep `(8,8)` as the default and do not integrate the shape-specific dispatch. The operator timing win is real for the sampled projections, but its impact is diluted or offset elsewhere in the verification path. This is two prompts, one process per prompt and four rounds; it is a tuning no-go for this configuration, not a universal performance conclusion. See the [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_ab_summary_v1.json), [HumanEval/114 A/B](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_ab_humaneval114_256_v1.json), [HumanEval/123 A/B](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_ab_humaneval123_256_v1.json), and [runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_ab.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

A lower-warp M=3 operator sweep expanded the launch set to ten `(OUT_TILE, num_warps)` choices on `q_proj`, `gate_proj` and `down_proj` from `cnndm_003` and HumanEval/114. All **60/60** prompt/projection/config outputs were bitwise equal to BNB M=3. Tile16 with one or two warps was substantially slower; 4x1 showed a prompt-dependent q_proj result (up to 10.7% faster than 8x8 on cnndm_003, but only 1.9% on /114). In contrast, 4x4 remained consistently faster on gate_proj (**6.3%/5.5%**) and down_proj (**5.9%/4.8%**) across the two prompts. A direct same-process K2/R3 fallback A/B then applied 4x1 to K=N=2048 projections and 4x4 to wide MLP projections. Both configurations matched B1 token/KV state for all **256/256 prefixes** on HumanEval/114 and /123; timed B1 tokens matched **4/4** rounds for each method. The combined tuned/default end-to-end ratio was **0.9935x**, with per-prompt ratios **0.9912x** and **0.9958x**; the more detailed launch map therefore also failed to improve latency. Keep default 8x8; do not dispatch these operator-only winners in the decoder. See the [CNN/DailyMail operator sweep](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_lower_warps_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval operator sweep](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_lower_warps_humaneval114_v1.json), [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_lower_warp_ab_summary_v1.json), and [A/B runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_lower_warp_ab.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The activation-vectorized NF4 M=3 kernel was then tested with cache-all activation loads (`ld.global.ca.v4.u32`) instead of streaming loads (`ld.global.cs.v4.u32`), leaving packed-weight loads and the arithmetic schedule unchanged. On layer-0 `q_proj`, `gate_proj`, and `down_proj`, both policies matched native BNB M=3 bitwise for both `cnndm_003` and HumanEval/114 (**6/6 module/prompt cases**). Paired operator timings showed `ca` near parity on q_proj, about **0.8–0.9% faster** on gate_proj, and **1.5–1.7% faster** on down_proj. A same-process closed-loop fallback A/B at 256 tokens then found both cache policies exact to B1 for all **256/256 KV prefixes** on HumanEval/114, /123, and CNN/DailyMail `cnndm_003`; all methods matched B1 token IDs in **4/4** paired timing rounds per prompt. The `ca`/`cs` end-to-end speed ratios were **1.0071x**, **1.0097x**, and **0.9997x**, respectively, with a three-prompt geomean **1.0055x**. Thus cache-all shows a small, repeatable signal on the two HumanEval cases but parity on the CNN/DailyMail case. Keep it experimental; the aggregate gain is below 1%, and independent process repeats plus broader task coverage are needed before considering runtime integration. No original-BNB KV identity is claimed. See the [operator result for cnndm_003](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_cnndm003_v1.json), [operator result for /114](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_humaneval114_v1.json), [paired summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab_summary_v1.json), [cache-policy A/B runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab.py), and [generated CA kernel](../../scripts/_nf4_activationvec_ca_kernel.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The cache-policy A/B was repeated in a second independent process for each prompt, giving **6 process runs** total at 256 tokens and four paired rounds per run. Both policies again matched B1 tokens and all **256/256 KV prefixes** in every run; timed tokens matched the B1 reference in **24/24** rounds per policy. After aggregating the two processes within each prompt, `ca`/`cs` speedup was **1.0049x** on HumanEval/114, **1.0068x** on /123, and **0.9991x** on CNN/DailyMail/003; the equal-prompt geomean was **1.0036x**. This repeats the small HumanEval gain and the CNN/DailyMail parity/slight loss. The effect is too small and workload-dependent to justify changing the runtime default; retain `.cs` and keep `.ca` as an experimental option. These are three selected prompt clusters on one GPU, with no population-level confidence interval and no original-BNB KV identity claim. See the [two-process summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab_repeats_summary_v1.json) and its six process artifacts listed in the summary. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An L2-only activation-load policy (`ld.global.cg.v4.u32`) was screened against the existing streaming `.cs` policy on the same three M=3 projections and two development prompts. All **6/6** outputs matched BNB M=3 bitwise. The gate projection was effectively tied (cg/cs **1.0013x** on `cnndm_003`, **1.0007x** on HumanEval/114); q_proj varied (**1.0002x**, **0.9791x**), while the large down_proj was consistently slower (**0.9858x**, **0.9861x**). This provides no strong operator case for `.cg`, so no end-to-end decoder test or integration is warranted. Keep `.cs` for M=3 activation loads and retain `.ca` only as the small experimental candidate described above. See the [CNN/DailyMail operator screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_cg_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval operator screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_cg_humaneval114_v1.json), and [runner](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_cache_cg.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

To check the cache-all `.ca` signal on new development prompts, same-process K=2/R=3 A/B runs were added for HumanEval/50 and CNN/DailyMail/050 (256 generated tokens, four paired timing rounds each), then repeated in independent processes. Both `.ca` and default `.cs` matched B1 tokens and all **256/256 KV prefixes** in all four runs; all methods matched B1 token IDs in **16/16** timed rounds. The `.ca/.cs` ratios were about **0.992–0.993x** on HumanEval/50 and **0.9956–0.9964x** on CNN/DailyMail/050. Aggregating all five prompt clusters and **10 process runs** gives a `.ca/.cs` geomean of approximately **0.9999x** (near parity, with prompt-dependent variation). Do not integrate `.ca`; `.cs` remains the runtime default. This is a descriptive sample on one GPU, with two processes per prompt, and claims B1 state identity only—not original-BNB KV identity. See the [five-prompt summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab_5prompt_summary_v1.json), [HumanEval/50 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab_humaneval050_256_v1.json), [CNN/DailyMail/050 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab_cnndm050_256_v1.json), and [A/B runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_cache_policy_ab.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The same ten paired process artifacts were summarized for the current default `.cs` activation-vectorized K2/R3 block fallback against original BNB greedy and B1 singleton. All **10/10 process runs** passed B1 token and full observed-state preflight, and all methods reproduced the B1 token IDs in every **4/4 timing rounds** per process. Across five selected prompt clusters, equal-prompt geomean speedup was **1.212x vs original BNB** (hierarchical paired bootstrap 95% interval **1.104–1.386x**) and **1.457x vs B1 singleton** (interval **1.328–1.671x**). Per-prompt speedups versus original BNB ranged from **1.071x to 1.574x**; each sampled prompt was positive. This is stronger evidence that the current B1 block backend can provide an end-to-end latency benefit than the marginal cache-policy experiment. The intervals resample five prompt clusters, two processes per prompt and four paired rounds; selected prompts are not a random population sample, and the result is limited to one GPU and this model/configuration. Original-BNB KV identity is not claimed. See the [paired performance summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_k2r3_paired_performance_5prompt_summary_v1.json) and [reproducible summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_k2r3_paired_performance.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Acceptance and decoder-cycle counters were added to that paired summary to explain the prompt variation. The observed ordering of draft acceptance exactly matched the speedup ordering across these five prompts: HumanEval/50 had **79.1%** acceptance, **111** mean verification cycles and **1.574x** speedup over BNB; HumanEval/123 had **37.5%**, **193** cycles and **1.072x**. The other prompts fell between those endpoints (CNN/DailyMail/003: 58.3%, 178 cycles, 1.219x; HumanEval/114: 46.5%, 188 cycles, 1.133x; CNN/DailyMail/050: 45.4%, 191 cycles, 1.125x). This supports acceptance and reduced cycle count as likely workload drivers of the speedup, while it does not isolate causality from prompt length, prefill share or other effects. Five selected prompts are too few to treat the rank relationship as a general law. GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

The paired runner now records B1 prefill, draft, verify, post-prefill wall time and residual time for each timed round. A new three-prompt phase run covered high-acceptance HumanEval/50, low-acceptance HumanEval/123 and the longer CNN/DailyMail/003, with 256 output tokens and four paired rounds each. Every run remained exact to B1 for all **256/256 observed KV prefixes**, and all methods matched B1 token IDs in **4/4** timing rounds. Equal-prompt geomean speedup of the `.cs` block was **1.276x end-to-end** and **1.281x on decode-only time** versus original BNB. The per-prompt end-to-end/decode-only ratios were **1.559x/1.567x** on HumanEval/50, **1.099x/1.100x** on HumanEval/123, and **1.212x/1.218x** on CNN/DailyMail/003. B1's verify phase (block forward plus host-side argmax synchronization) consumed about **99.1–99.2%** of post-prefill wall time; draft work was **0.10–0.19%**, with about **0.65–0.72%** residual. The similar end-to-end and decode-only ratios show that prefill is not driving the measured speedup on these three prompts. The next optimization target should therefore remain target verification and its GPU kernels, not n-gram proposal overhead. These are three selected prompts, one process each, and phase timers are wall-clock diagnostics rather than GPU-only kernel time. See the [phase summary](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_phase_timing_3prompt_summary_v1.json), [HumanEval/50 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_phase_ab_humaneval050_256_v1.json), [HumanEval/123 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_phase_ab_humaneval123_256_v1.json), [CNN/DailyMail/003 run](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_phase_ab_cnndm003_256_v1.json), [timing instrumentation](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_launch_ab.py), and [phase summarizer](../../scripts/summarize_activationvec_phase_timing.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Nsight Compute was run on one exact layer-0 `down_proj` M=3 kernel using actual CNN/DailyMail/003 activations. The candidate matched native BNB M=3 bitwise (**6,144/6,144 outputs**). The default `(OUT_TILE=8, num_warps=8)` launch took **95.2 μs** under profiling, used **42 registers/thread**, had **74.4% achieved occupancy** (83.3% theoretical), and showed no local/shared-memory spill requests. SM throughput was **74.1%** and DRAM throughput **67.0%** (128.5 GB/s), so the kernel is balanced between compute and memory rather than limited by occupancy alone. Nsight flagged **9% excess global-memory sectors** and a tail-wave opportunity due to 256 CTAs over 20 SMs; its estimated **33.3%** tail figure is a profiler model, not a measured end-to-end speedup. Existing exact operator screens show tile16x8 was about **15% slower** on this projection and 8x4 was near parity/slightly slower, so simply reducing CTA count or warps has not fixed the end-to-end bottleneck. This points to coalescing/reuse and arithmetic instruction count as the next microkernel questions; keep 8x8 unchanged until a candidate passes paired end-to-end A/B. See the [Nsight report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [profile input/correctness record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_cnndm003_v1.json), and [profile runner](../../scripts/profile_activationvec_nf4_m3_ncu.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

Matching NCU captures were added for q_proj and gate_proj on the same prompt/toolchain, both with full K=2,048 (no K tail). They matched BNB M=3 bitwise and still showed **8%** excess sectors for q_proj and **9%** for gate_proj, versus **9%** for down_proj. Thus down_proj's partial-K tail is not the sole source; the source-level counter could not be correlated to individual SASS loads in these reports, so metadata/codebook loads remain hypotheses rather than identified causes. q_proj took **22.5 μs** (40 registers/thread, 85.6% achieved occupancy, 60.3% SM/56.4% DRAM throughput); gate_proj took **93.3 μs** (44 registers/thread, 79.7% occupancy, 77.1% SM/67.9% DRAM). See the [q_proj report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_q_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [gate_proj report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_gate_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [q_proj correctness record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_q_cnndm003_v1.json), and [gate_proj correctness record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_gate_cnndm003_v1.json).

A pairwise scale-load reuse prototype was screened on q_proj, gate_proj and down_proj for CNN/DailyMail/003 and HumanEval/114. The first inline warp-butterfly version was non-exact and was discarded. A logical `tl.gather` version loaded nested-NF4 scale metadata on even lanes then reused it for the paired lane; it matched BNB M=3 on all **6/6** projection/prompt cases, but was slower in **5/6**. Candidate/default speed ratios were **1.076x** and **0.879x** for q_proj, **0.709x/0.747x** for gate_proj, and **0.823x/0.827x** for down_proj (speedup >1 means faster). Gather/warp data movement outweighed fewer scale loads for the wide projections, so this is a no-go for runtime integration and does not justify an end-to-end run. Keep the existing scale schedule. See the [operator results](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_scale_reuse_operator_v2.json), [logical gather kernel](../../scripts/_nf4_activationvec_scale_gather_v2_kernel.py), [initial non-exact screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_scale_reuse_operator_v1.json), and [screen runner](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_m3_scale_reuse.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300–349 remain unused.

An NCU capture of the exact down_proj gather candidate confirms the likely mechanism: versus the existing kernel, shared-memory load instructions increased from **10,496 to 78,080 (7.4x)** and shared stores from **12,288 to 79,872 (6.5x)**; shared wavefronts rose from **100,686 to 302,136 (3.0x)**. Total L1TEX sectors stayed essentially unchanged (**3.258M vs 3.270M**), so the intended scale reuse did not reduce aggregate sector traffic in this implementation. The candidate remained bitwise exact (**6,144/6,144**), but this hardware evidence aligns with the operator timing regression and closes the scale-gather branch as a no-go. Nsight runs used different profiling captures, so these counters diagnose generated work rather than establish a direct latency ratio. See the [gather NCU report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_scale_gather_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [candidate correctness record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_scale_gather_cnndm003_v1.json), and [baseline NCU report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_memtable_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

Compute/scheduler NCU profiles were collected for the unchanged 8x8 exact kernel on the same development activation. `down_proj` had **73.95% SM busy**, **74.52% achieved occupancy**, no Tensor Core instructions, and average warp issue gaps of **11.74 cycles**; long-scoreboard waits contributed **4.20 cycles (35.8%)** and math-pipe throttle **1.77 cycles (15.1%)**. `gate_proj` had **77.18% SM busy**, **80.02% occupancy**, no Tensor Core instructions, and **12.22 cycles** per issued instruction; long-scoreboard waits contributed **2.24 cycles (18.3%)** and math-pipe throttle **1.97 cycles (16.1%)**. The instruction chart counted about **2.19M / 2.28M non-fused FP32 instructions** versus only **22.5K / 22.0K fused FP32** instructions for down/gate. These counters suggest both memory latency and scalar arithmetic matter, with their balance differing by projection; they do not justify a generic memory-only optimization. See the [down compute report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_compute_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [gate compute report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_gate_compute_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), and their [down profile record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_compute_cnndm003_v1.json) / [gate profile record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_gate_compute_cnndm003_v1.json). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

Because NCU identified many non-fused FP32 instructions, compiler fusion was screened as a minimal arithmetic candidate with geometry fixed at 8x8. It was bitwise exact to BNB M=3 for q_proj (**49,152/49,152**), gate_proj (**33,024/33,024**) and down_proj (**6,144/6,144**). A follow-up paired A/B used 32 alternating rounds of eight launches on the same activations. The baseline/candidate median speed ratios were **1.0052x** for q_proj (paired bootstrap 95% interval **0.9902–1.0302**), **0.9983x** for gate_proj (**0.9966–0.9999**), and **1.0003x** for down_proj (**0.9988–1.0017**). Thus FP fusion preserves exact output here but provides no reliable kernel-level gain; keep the existing `enable_fp_fusion=False` schedule. This is one prompt, one layer and one GPU, and the paired intervals only describe timing variation in this sample. See the [paired A/B data](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_fp_fusion_ab_cnndm003_v1.json), [initial exactness-gated screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_fp_fusion_cnndm003_v1.json), and [A/B runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_fp_fusion_ab.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

Packed NF4 weight loads were separately switched from `.cs` to `.ca`, leaving activation and scale loads and the arithmetic schedule unchanged. The candidate was exact on q_proj, gate_proj and down_proj for both CNN/DailyMail/003 and HumanEval/114 (**6/6** projection/prompt cases). On CNN/DailyMail/003 the `.ca/.cs` speed ratios were **1.0151x** for q_proj (paired bootstrap 95% interval **1.0065–1.0386**), **0.9989x** for gate_proj (**0.9983–1.0009**) and **0.9998x** for down_proj (**0.9992–1.0050**). On the independent HumanEval/114 prompt they were **1.0022x** for q_proj (**0.9870–1.0149**), **0.9993x** for gate_proj (**0.9985–1.0011**) and **1.0000x** for down_proj (**0.9979–1.0014**). The small q_proj gain did not replicate; gate/down remained at parity. Do not integrate this policy based on the present evidence. See the [CNN/DailyMail screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_weight_ca_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval screen](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_weight_ca_humaneval114_v1.json), and [screen runner](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_weight_cache_policy.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

A direct PTX `shfl.sync.bfly` replacement for the five staged `tl.gather` reductions was compiled with the same nominal xor distances (16, 8, 4, 2, 1), FP fusion disabled and 8x8 geometry. It failed BNB bitwise equality on CNN/DailyMail/003: q_proj **5/6,144**, gate_proj **44/33,024**, down_proj **5/6,144** elements matched. Latency was correctly not measured after the exactness gate failed. The root cause is visible in actual TTGIR: the accumulator uses `threadsPerWarp=[1,8,4]` and `warpsPerCTA=[1,1,8]`, so a physical warp spans eight output channels and four reduction indices, while the remaining reduction indices are distributed across warps. A direct warp shuffle therefore combines values from the wrong logical reduction positions. The separate `[1,8,32]` probe had a different inferred layout and verified PTX semantics only for that probe; it was not evidence about the accumulator mapping. Keep the original gather reduction. See the [failed exactness record](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_warp_shuffle_cnndm003_retry1.json), [actual compiled TTGIR](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_warp_shuffle_gate_ttGIR_v1.txt), [layout/shuffle probe](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/triton_m3_warp_layout_8x8_v2.json), [probe runner](../../scripts/audit_triton_m3_warp_layout.py), [generated kernel](../../scripts/_nf4_activationvec_warp_shuffle_kernel.py), and [screen driver](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_m3_warp_shuffle.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

To let Triton lower the reduction for its actual layout, an alternate `tl.sum(axis=2, keep_dims=True)` kernel was screened at fixed 8x8 geometry with FP fusion disabled. It matched BNB M=3 bitwise for all three layer-0 projections on CNN/DailyMail/003 and HumanEval/114 (**6/6** cases). A direct 32-round paired A/B showed repeatable gains in gate_proj (**1.0367x**, CI **1.0366–1.0381**; **1.0342x**, CI **1.0332–1.0364**) and down_proj (**1.0221x**, CI **1.0213–1.0224**; **1.0218x**, CI **1.0209–1.0231**) on the two prompts; q_proj was parity (**1.0012x**, CI **0.9949–1.0072**; **0.9991x**, CI **0.9897–1.0073**). NCU on down_proj showed shared load instructions falling **10,496→5,888**, shared stores **12,288→7,680**, and shared wavefronts **100,686→82,073**, while total L1TEX sectors remained approximately unchanged (**3.258M→3.268M**). This supports fewer layout-exchange/reduction instructions as the source of the operator gain.

The full-layer exactness gate then found the key limitation on two development prompts. CNN/DailyMail/003 matched BNB M=3 for **235/252** layer/projection outputs and **3,096,554/3,096,576** scalar values; HumanEval/114 matched **238/252** outputs and **3,096,559/3,096,576** values. The failing operators differed by only 1–3 output elements in most cases, but bitwise equality is still broken (largest observed max-absolute differences **0.0625** and **0.015625**). Thus `tl.sum` is a promising speed candidate for a **new fixed-row B1 reference**, but it is not B2-exact to original BNB across the model and is not ready for runtime integration. Next gate: compare its M=3 rows against a singleton reference defined by the same fixed-row schedule across all layers, then measure end-to-end latency and separately report divergence from original BNB. See the [CNN/DailyMail A/B](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_ab_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval A/B](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_ab_humaneval114_v1.json), [CNN/DailyMail all-layer audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_all_layers_cnndm003_v1.json), [HumanEval all-layer audit](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_all_layers_humaneval114_v1.json), [NCU report](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/ncu_activationvec_m3_down_tl_sum_cnndm003_v1.ncu-rep), [kernel experiment](../../scripts/tune_activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum.py), and [paired A/B runner](../../scripts/benchmark_activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_ab.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

The singleton contract was then defined explicitly for B1: zero-pad one activation row to three physical rows, run the same `tl.sum` kernel, and use its first output row. Across all **252** Qwen2.5-3B layer/projection sites, every M=3 block row matched this fixed-row singleton result for both CNN/DailyMail/003 and HumanEval/114 (**3,096,576/3,096,576** scalar outputs per prompt). The same two 64-token end-to-end B1 pilots used an opt-in `triton_nf4_m3_tl_sum` strategy in the Qwen2 fixed-row adapter. Both matched B1 singleton tokens and all **64/64 KV prefixes**; both also happened to match original BNB greedy tokens on these prompts. Block speedup was **1.050x** vs B1 singleton and **0.750x** vs BNB on HumanEval/114; on CNN/DailyMail/003 it was **1.190x** vs B1 and **0.840x** vs BNB. This is a promising B1 correctness result but still fails the initial performance goal against original BNB, and two prompts are only finite validation. Keep the strategy opt-in and do not run held-out GSM8K yet. See the [HumanEval pilot](b1_tl_sum_humaneval114_64_v1/rows.jsonl), [CNN/DailyMail pilot](b1_tl_sum_cnndm003_64_v1/rows.jsonl), [fixed-row operator audit on HumanEval](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_fixed_row_humaneval114_v1.json), [fixed-row operator audit on CNN/DailyMail](triton_nf4_singleton_k2_r3_v1/three_row_kernel/native_bnb_profiles/activationvec_nf4_m3_tl_sum_fixed_row_cnndm003_v1.json), [certification kernel](../../src/zassd/certification/nf4_m3_tl_sum.py), and [Qwen2 fixed-row adapter](../../src/zassd/certification/qwen2_padded.py). The next engineering target is reducing singleton-kernel cost or improving acceptance without changing the new B1 contract, then repeating paired multi-prompt timing before any broad claim.

An independent second process was added for each of the five development prompts (HumanEval/50, /51, /52, /114 and CNN/DailyMail/003), with 64 output tokens and KV auditing in every run. The block matched B1 tokens and all **64/64 KV prefixes in 10/10 process runs**; it matched original BNB token IDs in **8/10**. Equal-prompt geomean speedup was **1.434x vs B1 singleton** (hierarchical paired bootstrap 95% interval **1.156–1.781**) and **1.010x vs original BNB** (interval **0.816–1.251**). The B1 gain is consistent across this selected cohort, while the original-BNB comparison remains uncertain and acceptance/workload dependent. Keep this as finite development evidence; it does not establish population-level speedup or universal exactness. See the [two-process summary](b1_tl_sum_5prompt_2process_summary_v1.json), [summary script](../../scripts/summarize_b1_tl_sum_pilot_cohort.py), [second HumanEval cohort run](b1_tl_sum_humaneval050_052_64_rep2_v1/rows.jsonl), [HumanEval/114 repeat](b1_tl_sum_humaneval114_64_rep2_v1/rows.jsonl), and [CNN/DailyMail repeat](b1_tl_sum_cnndm003_64_rep2_v1/rows.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

A low-acceptance draft-size screen then compared `ngram_size` 2, 3 and 4 on HumanEval/114 and CNN/DailyMail/003, each with one 64-token process run, K=2/R=3 and the same `triton_nf4_m3_tl_sum` B1 schedule. Every run matched B1 tokens and all **64/64 KV prefixes**; all six also matched original BNB tokens. The proposal trace was identical across these n-gram settings for each prompt: HumanEval accepted **3/29** drafted tokens and used 60 cycles; CNN/DailyMail accepted **10/34** and used 53 cycles. Arithmetic mean speedup across the two prompts was **1.118x / 1.109x / 1.120x vs B1 singleton** for n=2/3/4, and **0.793x / 0.777x / 0.794x vs original BNB**. This screen shows no meaningful acceptance benefit from changing n-gram size in this cohort; the small timing spread is single-run noise until replicated. Because the method remains below original BNB on both prompts, further proposal-size tuning is lower priority than reducing verification/kernel cost. See the [n=2](b1_tl_sum_lowaccept_ngram2_v1/rows.jsonl), [n=3](b1_tl_sum_lowaccept_ngram3_v1/rows.jsonl), and [n=4](b1_tl_sum_lowaccept_ngram4_v1/rows.jsonl) artifacts and the [development prompt cohort](tl_sum_low_acceptance_dev_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

#### Complementary rowwise-attention packed-kernel screen (2026-11-01)

In a separate `rowwise`-attention pilot, I exposed the already implemented `triton_nf4_fixed_three_rows_group_scale_packed` schedule in the pilot CLI and source fingerprint, then compared it with native BNB fixed rows and an N=16/one-warp packed variant. This is a distinct attention schedule from the `fixed_row_sdpa_decode` results above; keep their timing aggregates separate. On the low-acceptance pair, the packed three-row schedule had mean BNB speed ratio **0.902x**, better than native BNB fixed rows (**0.804x**) and `tl.sum` (**0.777x**), but still below parity; N=16/one-warp was **0.816x**. On HumanEval/50 and /52, the packed schedule reached **1.517x** mean BNB ratio, with **32/42** and **30/31** draft acceptance. An independent second process session then repeated all four prompts. Across **8/8 runs**, B1 tokens and all KV prefixes matched, and original BNB token IDs also matched; the combined geomean was **1.414x vs B1 singleton** and **1.174x vs original BNB**. Per-prompt BNB geomeans were **1.536x** (HumanEval/50), **1.542x** (/52), **0.838x** (/114), and **0.955x** (CNN/DailyMail/003). This supports an acceptance-conditioned performance result for a distinct fixed-row schedule, not a universal speedup: only four selected prompts, two process sessions, one GPU, and 64 output tokens were measured. Keep the schedule opt-in; the low-acceptance prompts still miss the BNB speed gate. See the [combined summary](b1_fixed3_packed_acceptance_contrast_summary_v1.json), [first high-acceptance run](b1_fixed3_packed_highaccept_v1/rows.jsonl), [first low-acceptance run](b1_fixed3_packed_lowaccept_v1/rows.jsonl), [independent four-prompt repeat](b1_fixed3_packed_acceptance_contrast_rep2_v1/rows.jsonl), [contrast cohort](tl_sum_acceptance_contrast_dev_prompts.jsonl), and [pilot CLI](../../scripts/run_b1_block_pilot.py). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

With the same `rowwise`-attention configuration, the packed schedule was then evaluated at a 256-token budget in two independent sessions over those four prompts. It matched B1 tokens and all **256/256 KV prefixes in 8/8 runs**. The aggregate geomean was **1.731x vs B1 singleton** and **1.414x vs original BNB**; prompt-level BNB ratios were **1.732x** (HumanEval/50), **1.883x** (/52), **1.089x** (/114), and **1.125x** (CNN/DailyMail/003). Acceptance at this longer budget was 144/182, 149/162, 69/142, and 78/142 respectively, unchanged across sessions. Original BNB token IDs matched on the two high-acceptance prompts (**4/4 runs**) and diverged at generated index **226** on /114 and **89** on `cnndm_003` in both sessions. The measured latency advantage therefore does not imply BNB trajectory equivalence. This is a useful positive development result for the declared B1 schedule, repeated across two sessions, but remains four selected prompts on one GPU. See the [256-token summary](b1_fixed3_packed_acceptance_contrast_256_summary_v1.json), [session 17](b1_fixed3_packed_acceptance_contrast_256_s17_v1/rows.jsonl), [session 18](b1_fixed3_packed_acceptance_contrast_256_s18_v1/rows.jsonl), and [frozen development cohort](tl_sum_acceptance_contrast_dev_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

To extend this rowwise-attention pilot beyond the acceptance-contrast prompts, I ran HumanEval/116–117 and CNN/DailyMail/006–007 at 256 tokens in two more independent sessions. All **8/8 runs** matched B1 tokens and all **256/256 KV prefixes**. Across prompt/session pairs, geomean speedup was **1.469x vs B1 singleton** and **1.195x vs original BNB**. The per-prompt BNB geomeans were **1.794x** (/116), **1.007x** (/117), **0.891x** (`cnndm_006`), and **1.266x** (`cnndm_007`). Original BNB tokens matched **2/8 runs**: both /116 runs; /117 diverged at token 162 and `cnndm_006` at token 12 in both sessions, while `cnndm_007` diverged at token 35 in both sessions. This extends finite B1 evidence to four additional prompts; it also shows BNB-relative latency remains prompt-dependent, with `cnndm_006` slower despite exact B1 matching. No confidence interval or broad workload claim is warranted from four prompt clusters on one GPU. See the [additional-cohort summary](b1_fixed3_packed_newdev_256_summary_v1.json), [session 19](b1_fixed3_packed_newdev_256_s19_v1/rows.jsonl), [session 20](b1_fixed3_packed_newdev_256_s20_v1/rows.jsonl), and [prompt manifest](b1_fixed3_packed_newdev_256_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

Pooling only the three 256-token cohorts that share this exact rowwise-attention, packed-NF4 configuration gives **12 prompt clusters and 24 process runs**. B1 tokens and all **256 KV prefixes** matched in **24/24 runs**. Prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.516x vs B1 singleton** (descriptive prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.336–1.741x**) and **1.228x vs original BNB** (**1.080–1.418x**); 10/12 prompt means were above BNB latency parity. BNB token IDs matched in only **6/24 runs**, so this is evidence for performance against the BNB latency baseline under a different B1 arithmetic schedule, not BNB trajectory equivalence. The interval resamples these 12 selected prompt clusters, with the two process sessions kept within each prompt; it does not represent an independent workload sample. See the [combined 12-prompt summary](b1_fixed3_packed_rowwise_256_all12_summary_v1.json), [cohort 1](tl_sum_acceptance_contrast_dev_prompts.jsonl), [cohort 2](b1_fixed3_packed_newdev_256_prompts.jsonl), and [cohort 3](b1_fixed3_packed_dev_cohort2_256_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

The rowwise-attention packed schedule was also run for **512 generated tokens** on four representative development prompts in two process sessions. B1 tokens and all **512 KV prefixes matched in 8/8 runs**. Geomean speedup was **1.773x vs B1 singleton** and **1.416x vs original BNB**. Per-prompt BNB geomeans were **1.679x** (HumanEval/50), **1.349x** (/117), **1.246x** (`cnndm_006`), and **1.425x** (`cnndm_007`); draft acceptance was 293/364, 223/273, 208/296 and 251/326 tokens. Original BNB tokens matched only on /50 (**2/8 runs**); the repeatable first divergence indices were 162 (/117), 12 (`cnndm_006`) and 35 (`cnndm_007`). This gives finite 512-token B1 state evidence and a positive latency screen, but BNB trajectory differences remain common and the four prompts were selected to span acceptance behavior. No population-level claim follows. See the [512-token summary](b1_fixed3_packed_rowwise_512_summary_v1.json), [session 23](b1_fixed3_packed_512_s23_v1/rows.jsonl), [session 24](b1_fixed3_packed_512_s24_v1/rows.jsonl), and [prompt cohort](b1_fixed3_packed_512_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

A second 512-token cohort (HumanEval/118–119 and CNN/DailyMail/008–009) was run in two additional sessions. All **8/8 runs** again matched B1 tokens and all 512 KV prefixes; BNB token IDs matched in **0/8**. Pooling both 512-token cohorts gives **8 prompt clusters, 16 runs**, and B1 identity in **16/16**. Prompt-cluster geomean speedup was **1.612x vs B1 singleton** (prompt-bootstrap 95% interval **1.406–1.833x**) and **1.300x vs BNB latency** (**1.144–1.465x**). BNB speed ratios by prompt ranged from **0.975x** (`cnndm_008`) to **1.679x** (HumanEval/50), and original-BNB token IDs matched only **2/16 runs**. The prompt-cluster interval is descriptive for these selected prompts on one GPU; it does not establish workload-wide speedup or BNB trajectory equivalence. See the [combined 8-prompt summary](b1_fixed3_packed_rowwise_512_all8_summary_v1.json), [cohort-2 summary](b1_fixed3_packed_rowwise_512_cohort2_summary_v1.json), [session 25](b1_fixed3_packed_512_cohort2_s25_v1/rows.jsonl), [session 26](b1_fixed3_packed_512_cohort2_s26_v1/rows.jsonl), and [cohort prompts](b1_fixed3_packed_dev_cohort2_256_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.

I extended two prompts with earlier BNB divergences—HumanEval/117 and CNN/DailyMail/006—to **1,024 generated tokens**, with two process sessions each. All **4/4 runs** matched B1 tokens and all **1,024 KV prefixes**; BNB tokens matched in **0/4**. Geomean speedup was **1.939x vs B1 singleton** and **1.527x vs BNB latency**. Per-prompt ratios were **1.566x** (/117) and **1.488x** (`cnndm_006`); acceptance totals across both sessions were **1,128/1,228** and **1,098/1,274** drafted tokens. BNB diverged at the same early indices as before: token **162** on /117 and **12** on `cnndm_006`. This extends empirical B1 state identity to a 1,024-token horizon for two prompts, while confirming that faster decoding does not preserve the BNB trajectory. See the [1,024-token summary](b1_fixed3_packed_rowwise_1024_summary_v1.json), [session 27](b1_fixed3_packed_1024_s27_v1/rows.jsonl), [session 28](b1_fixed3_packed_1024_s28_v1/rows.jsonl), and [prompt cohort](b1_fixed3_packed_1024_prompts.jsonl). GSM8K held-out indices 300-349 remain unused.
